BRIDGES Lectures: G 2 G_{2} in action, and a mathematical theory of exceptions
Laurent Manivel Toulouse Mathematics Institute CNRS/Toulouse University manivel@math.cnrs.fr

Abstract
The BRIDGES meeting in gauge theory, extremal structures, and stability was held in June 2024 at l’Institut d’Études Scientifiques de Cargèse in Corsica, organized by Daniele Faenzi, Eveline Legendre, Eric Loubeau, and Henrique Sá Earp. The first week was a summer school consisting of four independent but related lecture series by Oscar García-Prada, Spiro Karigiannis, Laurent Manivel, and Ruxandra Moraru. The present document consists of notes for the lecture series by Laurent Manivel on exceptional complex Lie groups, especially G 2 G_{2} , and some algebraic varieties acted on by these groups. The first lecture is a rather elementary introduction to G 2 G_{2} as an algebraic group, with its various incarnations in terms of trivectors or the Cayley octonions. The second lecture focuses on the geometry of several varieties acted on by G 2 G_{2} , transitively or quasi-transitively. The third lecture explores the Tits-Freudenthal magic square of Lie algebras, leading to a uniform construction of the exceptional simple Lie algebras, starting from Jordan algebras and triality. The fourth and last lecture discusses the more geometric aspects of the magic square, and their connections with a few famous problems in complex geometry. Some assistance in the preparation of these notes by the author was provided by several participants of the summer school. Details can be found at the end of the introduction.



Chapter 1
Introduction

In the introduction to the collection of short writings that Philippe Sollers published in 1985 under the title
Théorie des exceptions
(Theory of exceptions), he recalls that one of the original meanings of the
greek word
\acctonos
is
"une ambassade, une procession, une fête - un défilé, ou plutôt une danse d’exceptions"
("an embassy, a procession, a celebration - a parade, or rather a dance of exceptions"). Let it be clear, once and for all, that there is no theory of exceptions, in the abstract sense of the word. What we hope to offer the reader is rather a colorful parade of exceptional mathematical objects, all deriving from the exceptional complex simple Lie algebras and groups.

The discovery of the exceptional Lie algebras was a completely unexpected
consequence of Killing’s progress on the classification of simple Lie algebras.
The official birthdate of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is 23 May 1887, when Killing, in a letter to
Engel, wrote the root system and multiplication table
[
37
]
. The full classification was announced in October of the same year, and published in Mathematische Annalen as soon as 1888. Killing’s paper actually mentioned two simple Lie algebras of dimension
52
, hence two avatars of
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
, and only after Elie Cartan’s thesis in 1894
did the Killing-Cartan classification reached its final form.

Of course this was only the beginning of a long heroic story, in which the
role of the exceptional Lie algebras has been somehow ambiguous. On the one hand,
they seem to disturb the beautiful harmony of the classical Lie algebras, that
Killing expected to be the only simple ones. On the other hand, they add a little spice to this overly wise story, that has spread in different directions and gave birth to all sorts of exciting exceptional phenomena.

The goal of these lectures is to present some aspects of this story concerning complex projective geometry. Our main characters will be projective varieties, that are acted on by some exceptional Lie groups : we will thus see
G_{2}
in action, as well as the other Lie groups of type
F_{4},E_{6}
,
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
. One of the main occurrences of algebraic groups is indeed as symmetry groups of varieties, and there are at least two reasons to focus on those varieties whose automorphism group is exceptional.
The first one is the particular, intriguing beauty of these objects, that are worth contemplating for themselves – another meaning of the word
theory
, more from the Renaissance period, is "the science that deals with contemplation". Baudelaire wrote
Le beau est toujours bizarre. Tâchez de concevoir un beau banal !"
("Beauty is always bizarre. Try to design a
banal beauty
!"). The second one is that exceptional objects often appear in classifications, in which they are likely to
demonstrate some extremal, unexpected behavior.

But we will also take the opportunity to mention, at least in passing, several general notions and problems that exceptional objects may nicely illustrate. To name a few: symmetric spaces, affine homogeneous spaces and their compactifications, prehomogeneous spaces,
birational maps and homoloïdal polynomials, varieties of minimal rational
tangents, dual varieties, Lagrangian and Legendrian varieties, contact structures, graded Lie algebras, Tits geometries, etc. Our hope is that the reader of these notes, after enjoying the

dance of exceptions
, will feel like delving deeper into the feast.

This text is divided into four chapters, one for each lecture, and three appendices to make the text more self-contained. The first two lectures cover general theory and should be mostly accessible to graduate students and young researchers, both from algebraic and differential geometry (starting from Section
3.2
the arguments become more algebraic in flavour). The last two lectures are more technical and will appeal more to algebraic geometers. Basics from representation theory of semisimple Lie algebras, which are briefly recalled throughout the text, may be very useful to understand at least some of the content of the lectures. Each lecture ends with an exercise section, followed by solutions or hints; but the reader is strongly recommended to try to solve the exercises by himself. They are advertized according to their difficulty:

Exercise
1
.

Computation (easy exercise, verification)

Exercise
2
.

✍
Training exercise (proper exercise)

Exercise
3
.

\noway

☞
Necessary exercise (exercise whose solution is necessary to understand the notes)

Exercise
4
.

Curiosity (not directly useful for reading the notes)

The plan is the following. We start the first chapter by studying three-forms in seven variables and their connections with Cayley’s algebra of octonions. It is a result of Engel that the exceptional complex Lie group
G_{2}
can be realized as the stabilizer of a generic three-form. Later, E. Cartan proved that
G_{2}
can also be realized as the automorphism group of the octonions. In this lecture, we classify three-forms reproving a classical theorem of Schouten on the finiteness of
\GL_{7}
-orbits of three-forms, thus characterizing the generic forms. Then we construct composition algebras, in particular Cayley’s octonions. Finally, we describe
G_{2}
and its representations. Contributed to this chapter: A. Muniz (classification of three-forms), P. Schwahn (composition algebras, representations of G2, and actions of compact Lie groups).

The second chapter is were we really see
G_{2}
in action, since it
focuses on several interesting algebraic varieties acted upon by
G_{2}
. We highlight the roles played by the octonions and the representations of
G_{2}
in their constructions. First we discuss the two five-dimensional
G_{2}
-homogeneous varieties: the quadric
\mathbb{Q}^{5}=G_{2}/P_{1}
and the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian
G_{2}\Gr(2,7)=G_{2}/P_{2}
. Then we construct the Cayley Grassmannian
\CGr\subset\OGr(3,7)
, which parametrizes quaternion subalgebras of the octonions; its
"doubled version"
\DGr\subset\OGr(6,14)
, which compactifies the affine algebraic group
G_{2}
; the Lie Grassmannian
\LieGr(2,7)
, that parametrizes Lie subalgebras of the imaginary octonions; the horospherical
G_{2}
-variety
\HG\subset\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{g_{2}}^{\mathbb{C}})
, an unexpected deformation of the orthogonal Grassmannian
\OGr(2,7)
. We give these varieties several interpretations in terms of octonions. Contributed to this chapter: G. Comaschi (homogeneous
G_{2}
-varieties, the Cayley Grassmannian), V. Benedetti (double Cayley, Lie and horospherical Grassmannian).

The third chapter presents two constructions of exceptional Lie groups via Jordan algebras and Triality. Our main Jordan algebras are spaces of

3\times 3
Hermitian matrices with coefficients in a normed algebra
\mathbb{A}
, and we show how to construct from these "projective planes" over
\mathbb{A}
. In particular
the octonions yield the sixteen-dimensional Cayley plane, in which "projective
lines" are eight-dimensional quadrics. Starting from a pair of normed algebra, we explain how to define a Lie algebra and construct in particular the exceptional series of Lie algebras in a unified way. The result of this construction is the celebrated magic square of Freudenthal and Tits. Contributed to the exercises: V. Benedetti, G. Comaschi, A. Muniz, P. Schwahn.

The last chapter explores the varieties that appear in the
geometric version of the magic square, mainly due to Freudenthal. We will explain how varieties from a same row share very similar geometric properties, and how varieties from a same column are related to each other. It turns out that all these varieties, although not all exceptional, exhibit some special behaviour that we discuss and connect to a series of open problems of general interest in complex projective geometry. Contributed to the exercises: V. Benedetti, G. Comaschi, A. Muniz, P. Schwahn.

Appendices.

Appendix
6.1
covers basic facts of projective duality, drawn mostly from the books
[
34
]
and
[
75
]
. Appendix
6.2
covers graded Lie algebras, following
[
78
]
; this part is not strictly necessary but helps understanding many of the constructions in a uniform way. Appendix
6.3
covers Tits’ shadows, i.e. how to cover homogeneous spaces with homogeneous families of subvarieties; this is part of the theory of Tits geometries, which is very helpful when trying to understand the geometry of homogeneous spaces. Contributed to the Appendices: A. Muniz (projective duality), V. Benedetti (gradings of Lie algebras, Tits’ shadows).

Sá Earp for the invitation to the BRIDGES Summer School, for the friendly atmosphere in Cargese, and for the opportunity to prepare and expand these notes.

Chapter 2
Three-forms and the octonions

In 1887, W. Killing was attempting to show that the only simple complex Lie algebras were
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{so}(n,\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{sp}(n,\mathbb{C})
when he discovered a
14
-dimensional simple algebra of rank two, which we now call
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
: the first exceptional Lie algebra. Later, F. Engel realized a Lie group of type
G_{2}
as the stabilizer of a generic exterior three-form in seven variables. Another realization came from E. Cartan, who showed that the automorphism group of Cayley’s octonions is of type
G_{2}
. This first lecture aims to explore the classification of three-forms, the structure of composition algebras and the octonions, and show how a group of type
G_{2}
can be realized. For more on the historical background, see
[
3
,
37
]
and references therein.

To set up notation:
V_{n}
denotes a complex vector space of complex dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{n}=n
; its dual is
V_{n}^{*}
; a skew-symmetric (or exterior)
k
-form on
V_{n}
is an element of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}^{*}
; for
k=n
, we denote by
\det(V_{n}^{*})=\textstyle\bigwedge^{n}V_{n}^{*}\simeq\mathbb{C}
the top exterior power. Furthermore, if we fix a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
for
V_{n}
, then
\{e^{1},\dots,e^{n}\}
is the dual basis for
V_{n}^{*}
; the vectors
e_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}=e_{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{i_{k}}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
form a basis for
\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}
, and similarly for
e^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}
. For any
k
-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}^{\ast}
, the interior product with a vector
v\in V_{n}
is the
(k-1)
-form
v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega=\omega(v,\ldots\,)
. The
support
of a
k
-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}^{*}
is the smallest subspace
W\subset V_{n}^{*}
such that
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}W
, we will denote it by
S_{\omega}
; the dimension of
S_{\omega}
is the
rank
of
\omega
. When
k=2
,
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{n}^{*}
is isomorphic to the space of skew-symmetric matrices and this notion of rank coincides with the usual rank for matrices.

A desirable property of this rank is that it defines a good stratification of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}^{*}
. Let
\Sigma^{k}_{n,r}
denote the set of
k
-forms of rank
r
on
V_{n}
. If non-empty, it is a smooth algebraic variety of (complex) dimension
\binom{r}{k}+r(n-r)
whose closure is the union of forms of rank
r
or lower

[
59
, Chap. I]
. Moreover, the natural action of
\GL_{n}=\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
on
\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}V_{n}^{*}
preserves the rank, hence
\Sigma^{k}_{n,r}
decomposes into orbits. Each
\GL_{r}
-orbit in
\Sigma^{k}_{r,r}
corresponds to a
\GL_{n}
-orbit in
\Sigma^{k}_{n,r}
of the same codimension. Therefore, before describing three-forms in seven variables, we start with lower dimensions.

2.1
Three-forms in few variables

Three-forms in three variables are volume forms spanning the one-dimensional space
\det(V_{3}^{*})
. In four variables, we have an isomorphism given by contraction:

\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{4}^{*}\simeq V_{4}\otimes\det(V_{4}^{*})\simeq V_{4},\qquad v\otimes e^{1234}\mapsto v\mathbin{\lrcorner}e^{1234}.

Note that
v\mathbin{\lrcorner}e^{1234}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\ann(v))
, where
\ann(v)=\{\,f\in V_{4}^{*}\mid f(v)=0\,\}
; hence, every non-zero element of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{4}^{*}
is decomposable, i.e., a product of one-forms; thus of rank three.

In five variables, three-forms have either rank three or five; from the above discussion, we see that rank four is not possible. Rank-three forms are decomposable, so let
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{5}^{*}
be of rank five. Consider the skew-symmetric bilinear pairing

V_{5}^{*}\otimes V_{5}^{*}\longrightarrow\det(V_{5}^{*});\quad(\alpha,\beta)\longmapsto\alpha\wedge\beta\wedge\omega.

Since the dimension of
V_{5}
is odd, this pairing must degenerate, i.e., there exists
\alpha\in V_{5}^{*}
such that
\alpha\wedge\omega=0
; consequently,
\omega=\alpha\wedge\theta
, with
\theta
a two-form of rank four. Acting with
\GL_{5}
, we can assume that
\alpha=e^{1}
and
\theta\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{2},\dots,e^{5}\right\}
. From the classification of two-forms (or skew-symmetric matrices), we conclude that every rank-five three-form is in the orbit of

\omega=e^{1}\wedge(e^{23}+e^{45})=e^{123}+e^{145}.

In six variables, three-forms of maximal rank are described by the following result.

Proposition 1
.

The set
\Sigma_{6,6}^{3}
decomposes into two
\GL_{6}
-orbits, represented by

\omega_{1}=e^{123}+e^{456}\quad and\quad\omega_{2}=e^{124}+e^{135}+e^{236}.

Moreover,
\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{1}
is an open subset of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}
, and the closure of
\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{2}
is an open subset in a irreducible quartic hypersurface.

We will give a partly geometric proof. We refer the reader to
[
70
]
for a more elementary algebraic proof. Let us begin with a useful lemma.

Lemma 2.1.1
.

Let
V
be a complex vector space with an action of
\GL_{n}
such that there is a Zariski-open orbit. Then any hypersurface in the complement of this orbit is irreducible.

Proof 2.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the open orbit and
D\subset V\setminus\mathcal{O}
be a hypersurface in the complement. If
D=D_{1}\cup D_{2}
with
D_{1}\neq D_{2}
then
D_{1}=\{f_{1}=0\}
and
D_{2}=\{f_{2}=0\}
for two polynomials
f_{1},f_{2}
which must be
semi-invariant
(i.e., invariant modulo some character) under the action of
\GL_{n}
:

f_{i}(g\cdot x)=\det(g)^{t_{i}}f_{i}(x)

for some
t_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i=1,2
. Let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{O}
be a point in the open orbit, and consider the polynomial function
F(x)=f_{1}(x_{0})^{t_{2}}f_{2}(x)^{t_{1}}-f_{2}(x_{0})^{t_{1}}f_{1}(x)^{t_{2}}
. Then,
F
is semi-invariant and
F(x_{0})=0
. Since the orbit of
x_{0}
is an open subset,
F
is identically zero, hence
f_{1}^{t_{1}}
and
f_{2}^{t_{2}}
are proportional, which contradicts
D_{1}\neq D_{2}
.

Proof 2.1.3
.

First, we observe that the stabilizer of
\omega_{1}=e^{123}+e^{456}
under the action of
\GL_{6}
must preserve the pair
\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
of subspaces defined by
P_{1}=\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\right\}
and
P_{2}=\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{4},e^{5},e^{6}\right\}\subset V_{6}^{*}
. Indeed, the set of vectors
v
whose contraction with
\omega
is a two-form of rank at most two is the union of
P_{1}^{\perp}
with
P_{2}^{\perp}
.
We readily deduce that this stabilizer must be isomorphic to
(\SL_{3}\times\SL_{3})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, of dimension
16
. Then
\dim(\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{1})=36-16=20=\dim\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}
, hence
the orbit of
\omega_{1}
must be open.

We proceed to describe the complement.
Consider
X\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}
the set of decomposable three-vectors. It is an affine variety, the affine cone over the Grassmannian
\Gr(3,V_{6})
in its Plücker embedding. The dual variety
X^{\vee}\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}
is the Zariski closure of the set of forms
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}
such that
\ker\omega
contains the embedded tangent space
T_{w}X
for some
w\in X\setminus\{0\}
. See Appendix
6.1
for basics on Projective Duality.

Let
W\subset V_{6}
be the subspace associated with
w\in X
. The tangent space to
T_{w}X
is spanned by three-vectors of the form
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}
where
v_{1},v_{2}\in W
, as can be checked by computing first-order deformations. Therefore,
\omega\in X^{\vee}
if and only if there exists
W\in V_{6}
of dimension three such that

\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\wedge V_{6}^{*}=\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\ann(W)\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\otimes W^{\prime},

for any complement
W^{\prime}
of
W
. Consider the component in
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\otimes W^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}
.
Let
M_{\omega}
be the associated
3\times 3
matrix. Up to the action of
\GL_{3}\times\GL_{3}
it is only determined by its rank. But
\rk M_{\omega}=3
, since otherwise
\omega
would have rank at most
5
. So we can choose basis
\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}\}
of
\ann(W)
and
\{e^{4},e^{5},e^{6}\}
of
W^{\prime}
for which
M_{\omega}
is the identity, and then

\omega=ze^{123}+e^{12}\wedge e^{4}+e^{13}\wedge e^{5}+e^{23}\wedge e^{6}=e^{12}\wedge(ze^{3}+e^{4})+e^{135}+e^{236}=\omega_{2}.

Now, given
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}
, consider the equivariant map
\Psi_{\omega}\colon V_{6}\to V_{6}\otimes\det(V_{6}^{*})
given by
\Psi_{\omega}(v)=u\otimes e^{123456}
, where
u
is the unique vector such that
(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)\wedge\omega=u\mathbin{\lrcorner}e^{123456}
. Then define

\lambda\colon\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{6}^{*}\longrightarrow\det(V_{6}^{*})^{\otimes 2};\quad\omega\longmapsto\frac{1}{6}\tr(\Psi_{\omega}\circ\Psi_{\omega}).

Note that
\Psi_{\omega}
is quadratic in the coefficients of
\omega
, hence
V(\lambda)
, the zero locus of
\lambda
, is a quartic hypersurface. A simple computation shows that
\lambda(\omega_{1})=1
and
\lambda(\omega_{2})=0
; hence
V(\lambda)
is contained in the complement of
\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{1}
, and contains the closure of
\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{2}
. By Lemma
2.1.1
,
V(\lambda)
is irreducible. Since forms of lower rank are contained in
\Sigma^{3}_{6,5}
, which has dimension
15<19=\dim V(\lambda)
, this hypersurface must be the closure of
\GL_{6}\cdot\omega_{2}
.

Remark 2.1.4
.

As we noticed, the decomposition of
\omega_{1}
in Proposition
1
is essentially unique. Geometrically, this can be interpreted as the property, for a general point in
\mathbb{P}(\wedge^{3}V_{6}^{*})
, to be on a unique secant line joining two points of the Grassmannian; this is called the one apparent double point property. Note that
\wedge^{3}V_{6}
is self-dual (choose a volume form and use wedge product); this allows to identify the dual variety of the Grassmannian with its tangent variety (the union of its tangent spaces). The decomposition of
\omega_{2}
in Proposition
1
is also essentially unique (by the same argument as for
\omega_{1}
), and this can be rephrased by saying that a generic point on the tangent variety belongs to a unique tangent line. Note finally that the Grassmannian is cut-out by the quadratic equations
\Psi_{\omega}=0
, parametrized by
SL(V_{6})
. In particular the tangent quartic is singular along the Grassmannian, as expected in general.

Remark 2.1.5
.

The proof of Proposition
1
uses the complex numbers, or at least that the base field is quadratically closed.
Over
\mathbb{R}
there is one more orbit with representative
e^{124}+e^{135}+e^{456}-e^{236}
, cf.
[
70
]
.

2.2
Nondegenerate three-forms in seven variables

For a three-form
\omega
on a seven-dimensional vector space
V_{7}
, define the quadratic form

q_{\omega}(v)=(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)\wedge(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)\wedge\omega\in\det(V_{7}^{*}).

(2.1)

The three-form
\omega
is called
nondegenerate
if
q
has maximal rank. We will prove that the set of nondegenerate three-forms is the unique open orbit for the action of
\GL_{7}
on
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}^{*}
, which justifies calling such forms
generic
. This will be a consequence of the following theorem due to Schouten, see
[
72
]
or
[
62
]
for a modern exposition.

Theorem 2.2.1

(Schouten)
.

Let
\omega\in\Sigma^{3}_{7,7}
be a three-form of maximal rank
on a seven-dimensional complex vector space. Up to the action of
\GL_{7}
,
\omega
is one of the following:

1.

\omega_{1}=e^{125}+e^{136}+e^{147}
;

2.

\omega_{2}=e^{125}+e^{136}+e^{147}+e^{234}
;

3.

\omega_{3}=e^{125}+e^{236}+e^{347}
;

4.

\omega_{4}=e^{125}+e^{147}+e^{346}+e^{327}
;

5.

\omega_{5}=e^{125}+e^{136}+e^{147}+e^{234}+e^{567}
.

We will give here a geometric proof. For an elementary algebraic proof, see
[
62
]
.

The finiteness of the number of orbits follows from general theory of graded Lie algebras, see Example
6.2
. Let us only assume, at this point, that there exists an open orbit; we claim that is must coincide with the set of nondegenerate forms. Indeed, the set of degenerate forms is an invariant hypersurface
D
, and we know by Lemma
2.1.1
that there is a unique such hypersurface: the complement of the open orbit!
One can check that the ranks of the quadratic forms
q_{\omega_{i}}
are respectively
1,1,2,4,
and
7
(
\noway
☞
Exercise
2.6.1
). Thus, the open orbit is that of
\omega_{5}
.

To prove Schouten’s theorem, we follow the same line of arguments as in the proof of Proposition
1
. We need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
H\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{4}^{*}
be a three-dimensional subspace such that the supports of its elements span
V_{4}^{*}
. Then
H
has a basis of one of the following forms,
for some basis
\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3},e^{4}\}
of
V_{4}^{*}
:

\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}=e^{12}\\
\gamma_{2}=e^{13}\\
\gamma_{3}=e^{14}\\
\end{cases}\!\!\!\!\!,\quad\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}=e^{12}\\
\gamma_{2}=e^{13}\\
\gamma_{3}=e^{14}+e^{23}\\
\end{cases}\!\!\!\!\!,\quad\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}=e^{12}\\
\gamma_{2}=e^{23}\\
\gamma_{3}=e^{34}\\
\end{cases}\!\!\!\!\!,\quad\text{or}\quad\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}=e^{12}\\
\gamma_{2}=e^{34}\\
\gamma_{3}=(e^{1}+e^{3})\wedge(e^{2}+e^{4}).\\
\end{cases}\!\!\!\!\!

Proof 2.2.3
.

Consider the quadratic form
q(\eta)=\eta\wedge\eta\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{4}V_{4}^{*}\simeq\mathbb{C}
. The proof is a case-by-case analysis in terms of the rank of the restriction
q|_{H}
, see
[
70
, §3.2]
.

Proof 2.2.4

(Proof of Theorem
2.2.1
)
.

From the discussion below, we just need to determine the orbits of degenerate forms.
As in the proof of Proposition
1
, consider
X
the affine cone over

\Gr(3,V_{7})
, and its dual
X^{\vee}\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}^{*}
. This is a hypersurface (see Example
6.1
),
GL(V_{7})
-invariant, so it has to coincide with
D
.

So let
\omega\in D=X^{\vee}
; there exists
W\in\Gr(3,V_{7})
such that

\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\wedge V_{7}^{*}=\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\ann(W)\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\otimes W^{\prime}

if
W^{\prime}
is any complement.
(Notice that, given any
u\in W
,
(u\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)\wedge\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{4}\ann(W)\otimes W^{\prime}
hence
q_{\omega}(u,v)=0
for every
v\in V_{7}
, confirming that
\omega
is degenerate.)

Consider the decomposition
\omega=\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
where
\eta_{0}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\ann(W)
and
\eta_{1}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)\otimes W^{\prime}
. Notice that
\eta_{0}
is decomposable for
\dim\ann(W)=4
. On the other hand, we determine
\eta_{1}
via the action of
\SL_{4}\times\SL_{3}\subset\SL_{7}
preserving
\ann(W)
and
W^{\prime}
. Fixing a basis
\{e^{5},e^{6},e^{7}\}
for
W^{\prime}
we write
\eta_{1}=\gamma_{1}\wedge e^{5}+\gamma_{2}\wedge e^{6}+\gamma_{3}\wedge e^{7}
. Consider the subspace
H=\mathrm{span}\left\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\right\}\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(W)
. Note that
\dim H=3
, otherwise,
\omega
would have rank at most
6
. For a similar reason, the support of
H
spans
\ann(W)
; then, we may assume that
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}\}
is as in Lemma
2.2.2
for
\{e^{1},e^{2},e^{3},e^{4}\}
a basis of
\ann(W)
.

For the first case of Lemma
2.2.2
, if
\eta_{0}=0
we get

\omega=e^{12}\wedge e^{5}+e^{13}\wedge e^{6}+e^{14}\wedge e^{7}=e^{1}\wedge(e^{25}+e^{36}+e^{47})=\omega_{1}.

If
\eta_{0}\neq 0
then, up to changing
W^{\prime}
, we get
\eta_{0}=e^{234}
, hence

\omega=e^{1}\wedge(e^{25}+e^{36}+e^{47})+e^{234}=\omega_{2}.

In the second case, up to changing
W^{\prime}
we can assume
\eta_{0}=0
. Then

\omega=e^{125}+e^{136}+e^{147}+e^{237}=e^{1}\wedge(e^{25}+e^{36}+e^{47})+e^{237},

which is in the orbit of
\omega_{2}
. In the third case, we can assume again that
\eta_{0}=0
and then

\omega=e^{125}+e^{236}+e^{347}=\omega_{3}.

In the fourth case, once more we can assume
\eta_{0}=0
. Then

\omega=e^{125}+e^{346}+e^{127}+e^{147}-e^{237}+e^{347}=e^{1}\wedge(e^{2}\wedge(e^{5}+e^{7})+e^{47})+e^{3}\wedge(e^{27}+e^{4}\wedge(e^{6}+e^{7})),

so that it belongs to the orbit of
\omega_{4}
.

By semi-continuity of the rank of
q_{\omega}
, the irreducible hypersurface
D
must
be the closure of the orbit of
\omega_{4}
. An equation is

\det(q_{\omega})=0
, a polynomial of degree
21
in
\omega
. So it would seem that the hypersurface
D
is of this degree, but we will see it has in fact degree
7
(so that
\det(q_{\omega})
is a cube).

There are several ways to construct an equation of
D
: since it is the unique invariant hypersurface, we just need to construct a semi-invariant. By the First Fundamental Theorem of Invariant Theory, such a semi-invariant can necessarily be constructed in terms of permutations in tensor products, see
[
68
]
. There are many ways to do that. We could consider the map

\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge\omega}}{{\longrightarrow}}\textstyle\bigwedge^{5}V_{7}^{*}\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}.

The resulting determinant is also a polynomial of degree
21
. The caveat is that this polynomial is zero!

To find a degree seven semi-invariant defining
D
, consider the following equivariant morphism:

\displaystyle\Phi\colon S^{2}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\otimes V_{7}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}^{*}\otimes\textstyle\bigwedge^{4}V_{7}^{*}\simeq\End(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7})\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*}),

\displaystyle(\alpha\cdot\beta,\gamma)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{1}{2}(\alpha\otimes\beta\wedge\gamma+\beta\otimes\alpha\wedge\gamma).

The natural action of
\GL_{7}
defines an equivariant morphism
\End(V_{7})\to\End(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7})
. Transposing and composing with
\Phi
, we get a morphism
S^{2}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\otimes V_{7}^{\ast}\to\End(V_{7})\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*})
, hence,

S^{2}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\longrightarrow\End(V_{7})\otimes V_{7}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*}).

Taking the symmetric square of this morphism, and natural inclusions and projections, we get

S^{4}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\hookrightarrow S^{2}(S^{2}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7}))\to S^{2}(\End(V_{7})\otimes V_{7}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*}))\twoheadrightarrow S^{2}\End(V_{7})\otimes S^{2}V_{7}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*})^{\otimes 2};

composing in the first factor with the trace form
S^{2}\End(V_{7})\to\mathbb{C}
, we deduce a map

S^{4}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\longrightarrow S^{2}\End(V_{7})\otimes S^{2}V_{7}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow S^{2}V_{7}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*})^{\otimes 2}

(2.2)

On the other hand, the map
\omega\mapsto q_{\omega}
from (
2.1
) defines, via polarization, an equivariant morphism

S^{3}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}^{*})\to S^{2}V_{7}^{*}\otimes\det(V_{7}^{*}).

Contracting it with (
2.2
) and symmetrizing yields an equivariant map
S^{7}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{*}_{7})\to\det(V_{7}^{*})^{\otimes 2}
; this is our desired degree-seven semi-invariant.

Claim
. This semi-invariant is nonzero. Consequently, it gives an equation of

D
, and it cannot be a power.
This degree-seven semi-invariant was first constructed in
[
48
]
. Moreover, it can also be constructed through a procedure called Matrix Factorization (see
[
2
]
).

The full classification of complex three-forms in seven variables can be summarized in the following diagram. The numbers are the dimensions of the orbits and the arrows show how their closures are contained in each other
[
1
]
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 28}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 21}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 35}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 34}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 31}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 20}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 13}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 26}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 25}

The orbits of rank-seven forms are those of dimensions
21,28,31,34
, and
35
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

Again the situation is more complicated over the real numbers. For example, there are two open orbits, represented by the canonical three-forms for the real octonions and their split version (see Remark
2.3.9
). We refer to
[
62
, §5]
for a classification over an arbitrary field.

2.3
Composition algebras and the octonions

Many interesting objects of study in geometry, both classical and exceptional, can be traced back to simple algebraic structures permeating our everyday life: the so-called composition algebras.

Definition 2.3.1
.

A
composition algebra
(or
Hurwitz algebra
) is a unital, not necessarily associative or commutative algebra
(\mathbb{A},\cdot\,)
over a field
\mathbb{K}
, together with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form (the
inner product
)

\langle\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle\colon\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{K}

such that its associated quadratic form
q\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{K}
,
q(x)=\langle x,x\rangle
(the
norm
), is
multiplicative
, i.e.

q(u\cdot v)=q(u)q(v)\quad\forall u,v\in\mathbb{A}.

The
real part
of a composition algebra is
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{A}:=\mathbb{K}\,1_{\mathbb{A}}
, and the
imaginary part
is its orthogonal complement
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}:=(\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{A})^{\perp}
. We always have
q(1_{\mathbb{A}})=1
, thus
\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{A}\oplus\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Any composition algebra comes with an involution
x\mapsto\bar{x}
called
conjugation
, defined by reflection along
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{A}
:

\bar{x}=2\langle x,1_{\mathbb{A}}\rangle 1_{\mathbb{A}}-x,\qquad x\in\mathbb{A}.

Many important properties and calculational rules for the conjugation and bilinear form on composition algebras are contained in
[
74
]
.
1
1

1

Note that this book uses the notation
\langle\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle
for
twice
the inner product, that is,
q(x)=\frac{1}{2}\langle x,x\rangle
.
For example, conjugation is an antiautomorphism:

\overline{(xy)}=\bar{y}\bar{x}\qquad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{A},

(2.3)

and it recovers the norm:

x\bar{x}=\bar{x}x=q(x)1_{\mathbb{A}}\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{A}.

(2.4)

Let us for now focus on algebras over the field
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
. A real composition algebra
\mathbb{A}
whose quadratic form
q
is positive-definite is called a
Euclidean Hurwitz algebra
. Such an
\mathbb{A}
is automatically a
division algebra
– that is, every nonzero element
u\in\mathbb{A}
has a two-sided multiplicative inverse
u^{-1}

[
74
, Prop. 1.3.4]
. As a consequence, there are no zero divisors:

x\cdot y=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x=0\text{ or }y=0,\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{A}.

We hence refer to
\mathbb{A}
also as a
normed division algebra
.

On the other hand, if
q
is indefinite (or, equivalently, if there exist zero divisors), the algebra
\mathbb{A}
is called
split
. A vector subspace on which
q
vanishes identically is called
isotropic
.

Definition 2.3.2
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a composition algebra over
\mathbb{R}
. Its
complexification

\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the algebra over
\mathbb{C}
given by
\mathbb{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
, with complex-bilinearly extended product.

The complex algebra
\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{C}}
is again a composition algebra. Complexification is a useful tool: it may simplify the representation theory of groups and algebras, the classification of bilinear forms (signature disappears), and as we have seen, also three-forms in low dimensions (there is only one open orbit of
\GL_{7}(\mathbb{C})
on
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{7}
, instead of two in the real case).

Let us review two normed division algebras whose existence has far-reaching consequences.

Quaternions

The quaternions
\mathbb{H}
are a four-dimensional normed division algebra discovered by Hamilton in 1843. As a vector space over
\mathbb{R}
, it is spanned by the basis vectors
1,I,J,K
, which satisfy the relations

I^{2}=J^{2}=K^{2}=IJK=-1.

Any quaternion
x\in\mathbb{H}
can be written as a linear combination

x=x_{0}+x_{1}I+x_{2}J+x_{3}K,\qquad x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}.

The
real part
is
\operatorname{Re}(x)=x_{0}
.

The quaternions include the algebra of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}=\mathrm{span}\{1,I\}
as a subalgebra. Complex conjugation extends to
\mathbb{H}
as

\bar{x}=x_{0}-x_{1}I-x_{2}J-x_{3}K,

and the inner product is given by

\langle x,y\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(x\bar{y}+y\bar{x})=\operatorname{Re}(x\bar{y}).

This gives rise to the norm

q(x)=|x|^{2}=x\bar{x}=\bar{x}x.

The norm is multiplicative and thus gives
\mathbb{H}
the structure of a normed division algebra. It is associative, but not commutative.

The quaternions can be built from the complex numbers via a construction known as the
Cayley-Dickson doubling process
(
\noway
☞
Exercise
2.6.2
). This process can be iterated also over the quaternions, and this begs the question: what is the result of doubling the quaternions?

Octonions

Discovered by Graves in 1843, and independently by Cayley in 1845,
the octonions are a real algebra
\mathbb{O}
defined by doubling the quaternions via the Cayley–Dickson rule (
2.12
). It is thus an eight-dimensional algebra over
\mathbb{R}
, spanned by the basis vectors
e_{0}=1,e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}
. Their multiplication is encoded in the
Fano plane
(the projective plane over the field with two elements), which has seven points (labeled by the basis vectors
e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}
) and seven lines:

The elements
e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}
are imaginary units: we have

e_{1}^{2}=\cdots=e_{7}^{2}=-1.

Each line
(e_{i}\to e_{j}\to e_{k})
in the Fano plane, cyclically oriented as above, gives the rule

e_{i}\cdot e_{j}=e_{k}=-e_{j}\cdot e_{i}.

The conjugation of
\mathbb{O}
is defined by

\bar{1}=1,\qquad\overline{e_{i}}=-e_{i}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,7.

Again,
\langle x,y\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(x\bar{y}+y\bar{x})=\operatorname{Re}(x\bar{y})
defines an inner product (for which
e_{0}:=1,e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}
form an orthonormal basis) and a norm, making
\mathbb{O}
into a normed division algebra. It is neither commutative nor associative! However it is both alternative (Exercise
2.6.3
) and satisfies the so-called Moufang identities (Exercise
2.6.4
). For more information, see e.g.
[
5
]
.

Hurwitz’s Theorem

Theorem 2.3.3

(Hurwitz 1898)
.

There exist only four real normed division algebras:
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{H}
, and
\mathbb{O}
.

As a consequence of this theorem and Exercise
2.6.3
, any two elements in
\mathbb{O}
are contained in a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathbb{H}
. Hurwitz’s Theorem, as well as the concept of quaternions and octonions, has been generalized to other fields
[
44
]
:

Theorem 2.3.4

(Hurwitz; Jacobson 1958)
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a composition algebra over a field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
\neq 2
. Then one of the following holds:

1.

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{A}=1
and
\mathbb{A}\simeq\mathbb{K}
,

2.

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{A}=2
and either
\mathbb{A}\simeq\mathbb{K}\oplus\mathbb{K}
or
\mathbb{A}
is a quadratic field extension of
\mathbb{K}
,

3.

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{A}=4
(and
\mathbb{A}
is called a
quaternion algebra
over
\mathbb{K}
),

4.

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{A}=8
(and
\mathbb{A}
is called an
octonion algebra
over
\mathbb{K}
).

Moreover, two composition algebras are isomorphic if and only if their underlying quadratic vector spaces
(\mathbb{A},q)
are isomorphic.

Examples of non-Euclidean quaternion algebras can be found in Exercise
2.6.6
.

Cross products

Normed division algebras are related to so-called vector cross products.

Definition 2.3.5
.

Let
(V,\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle)
be an Euclidean vector space. A
vector cross product
is a bilinear map

\times\colon V\times V\to V

which is totally skew-symmetric, i.e.,

\langle u\times v,w\rangle=-\langle v\times u,w\rangle=\langle v\times w,u\rangle\qquad\forall u,v,w\in V,

and satisfies the
magnitude
condition

|u\times v|^{2}=|u\wedge v|^{2}:=|u|^{2}|v|^{2}-\langle u,v\rangle^{2}\qquad\forall u,v\in V.

(2.5)

Any vector cross product gives rise to a three-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V^{\ast}
via

\omega(u,v,w)=\langle u\times v,w\rangle,\qquad u,v,w\in V.

Equation (
2.5
) is equivalent to the condition that for every unit vector
u\in V
, the skew-symmetric endomorphism
u\times\colon V\to V
restricted to
u^{\perp}\subset V
is orthogonal (and thus a complex structure). In particular,
\dim V
has to be odd.

In fact, from any vector space with vector cross product one can construct a normed division algebra (
\noway
☞
Exercise
2.6.7
). Conversely:

Theorem 2.3.6
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a real normed division algebra with imaginary part
V:=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Then

u\times v:=\operatorname{Im}(uv),\qquad u,v\in V,

defines a vector cross product on
V
.

Proof 2.3.7
.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.3.3
,
\mathbb{A}
is isomorphic to a subalgebra of the octonions and thus alternative. The definition of octonionic multiplication implies that

x\times y=\operatorname{Im}(xy)=-\operatorname{Im}(yx)=-y\times x

for all
x,y\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Additionally, we have

\langle x\times y,z\rangle=\operatorname{Re}((x\times y)\bar{z})=-\operatorname{Re}((xy)z)

for
x,y,z\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
, since
\bar{z}=-z
. By alternativity, and since the square of any imaginary octonion is real, we deduce that

\langle x\times y,y\rangle=-\operatorname{Re}((xy)y)=-\operatorname{Re}(xy^{2})=|y|^{2}\operatorname{Re}(x)=0,

and the total skew-symmetry of
\times
follows by polarization. To check the magnitude condition, we use the fact that
\overline{xy}=\bar{y}\bar{x}
together with the Moufang identities and compute

\displaystyle|x\times y|^{2}

\displaystyle=|\operatorname{Im}(xy)|^{2}=|xy|^{2}-|\operatorname{Re}(xy)|^{2}=\operatorname{Re}((xy)(\overline{xy}))-\langle x,y\rangle^{2}=\operatorname{Re}((xy)(yx))-\langle x,y\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Re}((xy^{2})x)-\langle x,y\rangle^{2}=-|y|^{2}\operatorname{Re}(x^{2})-\langle x,y\rangle^{2}=|x|^{2}|y|^{2}-\langle x,y\rangle^{2}.

Together with Theorem
2.3.3
, these results imply that nontrivial cross products exist only in three or seven dimensions, and are related to the quaternions or octonions, respectively
[
11
]
.

The associative three-form

We return to the study of the octonion algebra
\mathbb{O}
, whose imaginary part is
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{span}\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}\right\}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{7}
. As in Exercise
2.6.7
, octonionic multiplication gives rise to a vector cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{7}
, and thus to a canonical three-form.

Definition 2.3.8
.

The
associative
three-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{\ast}
is defined by

\omega(x,y,z)=\langle\operatorname{Im}(xy),z\rangle=-\operatorname{Re}((xy)z),\qquad x,y,z\in\mathbb{O}.

(2.6)

If
(e^{1},\ldots,e^{7})
is the dual basis of
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{7})
, we may also write

\omega=\sum_{(e_{i}\to e_{j}\to e_{k})}e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k},

where the sum ranges over all oriented lines of the Fano plane. Explicitly,

\omega=e^{123}+e^{365}+e^{541}+e^{264}+e^{176}+e^{572}+e^{374}.

(2.7)

Taking
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{7})
as positively oriented, the information of
\omega
is equivalently included in its Hodge dual
\star\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{\ast}
, given by

\star\omega=e^{4567}-e^{1247}-e^{2367}-e^{1357}-e^{2345}+e^{1346}-e^{1256}.

This is also called the
coassociative four-form
. For more information, see
[
36
]
,
[
35
]
.

Remark 2.3.9
.

Later, we will have to complexify the octonions and consider the complex-trilinear extension of
\omega
to
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\mathbb{C}^{7})^{\ast}
. By abuse of notation, we will still call it
\omega
. In the complex setting, one may check that
\omega
is obtained from
\omega_{5}
of Theorem
2.2.1
by the following change of basis:

\begin{matrix}[l]e^{1}\mapsto\lambda\,e^{1},&e^{2}\mapsto\lambda(e^{5}-\mathrm{i}\,e^{2}),&e^{3}\mapsto\lambda^{2}(e^{3}+\mathrm{i}\,e^{6}),&e^{4}\mapsto e^{4}-\mathrm{i}\,e^{7},\\
e^{5}\mapsto-\lambda(e^{5}+\mathrm{i}\,e^{2}),&e^{6}\mapsto e^{3}-\mathrm{i}\,e^{6},&e^{7}\mapsto\lambda^{2}(e^{4}+\mathrm{i}\,e^{7}),&\end{matrix}

where
\mathrm{i}^{2}=-1
and
\lambda^{3}=-\mathrm{i}/2
. In particular, the three-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\mathbb{C}^{7})^{\ast}
is
generic
in the sense that its
\GL_{7}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit is open (see Section
2.2
).

Composition algebras with zero divisors

It is possible to modify the Cayley–Dickson construction to obtain split composition algebras. Starting with a composition algebra
\mathbb{A}
, let the product on
\mathbb{A}\oplus\mathbb{A}
be defined by

(a,b)\cdot(c,d)=(ac+\overline{d}b,b\overline{c}+da)

(2.8)

for all
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{A}
(notice the difference in sign to (
2.12
)). If
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{A}
is associative and split, then
\mathbb{A}\oplus\mathbb{A}
will again be a split composition algebra. Starting from
\mathbb{R}
, we successively obtain the algebras of
split-complex numbers

\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
,
split quaternions

\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
, and
split octonions

\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
. These all have zero divisors, and their inner product is not positive definite.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.3.4
, both
\mathbb{O}
and
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
have isomorphic complexification
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
(since over
\mathbb{C}
nondegenerate quadratic forms are equivalent). These are called
complex octonions
or
bioctonions
. In split or complex composition algebras, zero divisors are of importance. Therefore, we state two results characterizing zero divisors.

Lemma 2.3.10
.

Zero divisors in a composition algebra are precisely the elements of norm zero.

Proof 2.3.11
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a composition algebra, and
x\in\mathbb{A}
such that
q(x)=0
. Then, by (
2.4
),
x\bar{x}=0
, hence
x
is a zero divisor. Conversely, let
x,y\in\mathbb{A}
be nonzero with
xy=0
. It follows from the properties of composition algebras
[
74
, Lem. 1.3.3]
that

0=(xy)\bar{y}=q(y)x,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad 0=\bar{x}(xy)=q(x)y,

and thus
q(x)=q(y)=0
.

This immediately implies that if
x\in\mathbb{A}
is a zero divisor, then
x(\bar{x}z)=q(x)z=0
for any
z\in\mathbb{A}
. There is also a converse:

Theorem 2.3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a composition algebra, and
x,y\in\mathbb{A}
such that
xy=0
. Then there exists
z\in\mathbb{A}
such that
y=\bar{x}z
, and
\bar{x}\mathbb{A}
is a maximal isotropic subspace for
q
.

Proof 2.3.13
.

For
x\in\mathbb{A}
, let
L_{x}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{A}
denote left-multiplication,
L_{x}(y):=xy
. Suppose that
x
is a zero divisor. We show that
\ker L_{x}
and
\im L_{x}=x\mathbb{A}
are isotropic subspaces.

First, any element
y\in\ker L_{x}
satisfies
xy=0
and is thus a zero divisor. So
q(y)=0
by Lemma
2.3.10
. Thus
\ker L_{x}
is isotropic.

Second, let
a\in\mathbb{A}
. Applying the calculational rules in
[
74
, Lem. 1.3.2, 1.3.3]
, we find

q(xa)1_{\mathbb{A}}=\langle xa,xa\rangle=\langle a,\bar{x}(xa)\rangle=\langle a,q(x)a\rangle=0.

Thus
x\mathbb{A}
is isotropic.

Since
\ker L_{x}=(\bar{x}\mathbb{A})^{\perp}
and
q
is nondegenerate, the dimension formula

\dim\bar{x}\mathbb{A}+\dim\ker L_{x}=\dim\mathbb{A}

holds. Recalling that isotropic subspaces are of dimension at most
\frac{1}{2}\dim\mathbb{A}
, it follows that both
\bar{x}\mathbb{A}
and
\ker L_{x}
have this dimension and are thus maximal isotropic. Furthermore, since it has already been remarked above that
\bar{x}\mathbb{A}\subseteq\ker L_{x}
, it follows that
\bar{x}\mathbb{A}=\ker L_{x}
.

Corollary 2
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
be a zero divisor, and
K_{x}=\ker(L_{x}|_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}})
the kernel of left-multiplication by
x
on
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Then
K_{x}=x\mathbb{A}\cap\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
is an isotropic subspace of dimension
\frac{\dim\mathbb{A}}{2}-1
containing
x
.

We may also ask about subspaces of a composition algebra on which the multiplication vanishes identically. In the complex octonions, it turns out that the maximal such subspaces are two-dimensional (see Lemma
3.1.16
).

Definition 2.3.14
.

A two-dimensional subspace
N\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is called a
null-plane
if
xy=0
for all
x,y\in N
.

Lemma 2.3.15
.

Null-planes are isotropic and purely imaginary.

Proof 2.3.16
.

Let
N
be a null-plane. Then for every
x\in N
, we have
x^{2}=0
, hence
q(x)=0
by Lemma
2.3.10
. Thus
q
vanishes when restricted to
N
. Moreover

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=x^{2}+\bar{x}x=2\operatorname{Re}(x)x,

so necessarily
\operatorname{Re}(x)=0
.

We will see in the second lecture that the set of null-planes is homogeneous under the action of the complex Lie group
G_{2}
, see Corollary
7
.

2.4
Automorphisms

Let us finally turn to our desired object of study: the exceptional Lie group
G_{2}
, which arises as the automorphism group of the octonions. For any composition algebra
\mathbb{A}
over
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, its automorphism group is the Lie group defined by

\Aut(\mathbb{A})=\left\{U\in\GL(\mathbb{A})\,|\,U(xy)=U(x)U(y)\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{A}\right\}.

The group
\GL(\mathbb{A})
is to be understood over the same field
\mathbb{K}
.

By
[
74
, Cor. 1.2.4]
, the norm of
\mathbb{A}
is determined by its algebra structure. Thus,
\Aut(\mathbb{A})
is a closed subgroup of the
orthogonal group

\rmO(\mathbb{A},q)
. Since automorphisms preserve
1_{\mathbb{A}}
and the norm, they preserve the splitting into real and imaginary parts and are determined by their action on the subspace
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A}
. Thus, we may view
\Aut(\mathbb{A})
as a subgroup of
\rmO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},q)
.

The Lie algebra of
\Aut(\mathbb{A})
is the set of
derivations
of
\mathbb{A}
, that is,

\Der(\mathbb{A})=\left\{U\in\mathfrak{gl}(\mathbb{A})\,|\,U(xy)=U(x)y+xU(y)\right\}.

From now on, we work over
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
, and take Lie groups and Lie algebras to be
complex
by default.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

For the associative complex composition algebras, we have

\displaystyle\Aut(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\Aut(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}_{2},

\displaystyle\Aut(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=\SO_{3},

\displaystyle\Der(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\Der(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\Der(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{so}_{3}.

Proof 2.4.2
.

Since
\Aut(\mathbb{A})\subset\rmO(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{A},q)
, we have

\Aut(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})\subset\rmO_{0}=1,\qquad\Aut(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})\subset\rmO_{1}=\mathbb{Z}_{2},\qquad\Aut(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})\subset\rmO_{3}.

The first case is trivial. For the second case, we note that the conjugation
z\mapsto\bar{z}
is a nontrivial automorphism by (
2.3
) and commutativity, thus
\Aut(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. For the third case, we observe that
\Aut(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
is precisely the stabilizer in
\rmO_{3}(\mathbb{C})
of the nontrivial three-form
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})^{\ast}
given by

\omega(x,y,z)=\langle\operatorname{Im}(xy),z\rangle.

Since
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\ast}\simeq\mathbb{C}
,
\omega
is a multiple of the volume form, and its stabilizer is

\Aut(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})=\rmO_{3}\cap\SL_{3}=\SO_{3}.

Definition 2.4.3
.

We define the following Lie groups as automorphism groups of the complex, real, and split octonions, respectively:

G_{2}=\Aut(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}),\qquad G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}=\Aut(\mathbb{O}),\qquad G_{2}^{\prime}=\Aut(\mathbb{O}^{\prime}).

Moreover we denote the respective Lie algebras by
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Definition 2.4.4
.

A
basic triple
is a triple
(x,y,z)
of imaginary unit octonions such that
\langle x,y\rangle=0
, and
z
is orthogonal to the (quaternion) subalgebra generated by
\{x,y\}
.

In fact
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts simply transitively on basic triples; moreover
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
is connected of dimension
14
(
\noway
☞
Exercise
2.6.9
). The analogous statement holds for
G_{2}
and the complex octonions
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Corollary 3
.

The group
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts transitively on the unit sphere
S^{6}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}
.

In a sense, octonions are often responsible for the existence of exceptional objects in mathematics. Their derivations give the smallest exceptional Lie algebra in the Cartan–Killing classification:

Theorem 2.4.5
.

\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is an exceptional simple Lie algebra.

The same statement holds true over
\mathbb{R}
for the octonions
\mathbb{O}
(Cartan 1914) and the split octonions
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, provided that
G_{2}
is respectively replaced by its
compact real form

G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}\subset\SO_{7}(\mathbb{R})
or
split real form

G_{2}^{\ast}\subset\SO(3,4)

[
3
,
25
]
. The other exceptional Lie algebras will be addressed in Lecture 3. For now, let us focus on some representation-theoretic properties of
G_{2}
.

2.5
Representations and invariants of
G_{2}

Theorem 2.5.1
.

G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
(resp.
G_{2}
) is the stabilizer of the associative three-form
\omega\in\Lambda^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{7})^{\ast}
(resp. its complexification), which is generic in the sense of Section
2.2
. More precisely, the associated quadratic form
q
defined in (
2.1
) induces the octonionic norm in the sense that

q_{\omega}(v)=6|v|^{2}\otimes e^{1234567}\qquad\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{7}.

(2.9)

Proof 2.5.2
.

As noted above,
G_{2}
preserves the octonionic multiplication, the norm, and the imaginary part. Thus, it also stabilizes the associative three-form

\omega(x,y,z)=\langle\operatorname{Im}(xy),z\rangle.

This shows that
G_{2}\subseteq\Stab(\omega)
.

By Corollary
3
,
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts transitively on the unit sphere while preserving
\omega
. Thus, we may take without restriction
v=e_{1}
and with the help of (
2.7
) compute

\displaystyle v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega

\displaystyle=e^{23}+e^{54}+e^{76},

\displaystyle(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)^{2}

\displaystyle=2e^{2354}+2e^{2376}+2e^{5476},

\displaystyle(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)^{2}\wedge\omega

\displaystyle=6e^{1234567}.

After homogenizing, we obtain (
2.9
). This means that
q_{\omega}
is nondegenerate, that is,
\omega
is a generic three-form.

In particular, (
2.9
) implies that every element in
\GL_{7}(\mathbb{R})
preserving
\omega
also preserves the volume form
e^{1234567}
and the inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{7}
. Thus, it will also preserve the vector cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{7}
, and by Exercise
2.6.7
(and Hurwitz’s Theorem) also the multiplication on
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{7}
. This shows that
\Stab(\omega)\subseteq G_{2}
.

Let
V_{7}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
be the
standard representation
of
G_{2}
. As
G_{2}
preserves a nondegenerate bilinear form on
V_{7}
, this representation is self-dual, i.e.,
V_{7}\simeq V_{7}^{\ast}
. The same thus goes for exterior or symmetric powers of
V_{7}
. In the sequel, we will freely identify these representations with their dual.
For example,
S^{2}V_{7}
identifies with the space of quadratic forms on
V_{7}
, so it contains an invariant line generated by
q_{\omega}
. An invariant complement is the kernel
S^{2}_{0}V_{7}
of the contraction with
q_{\omega}
.

Proposition 4
.

The representation
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}
of
G_{2}
decomposes into invariant subspaces as

\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}\simeq\mathbb{C}\oplus V_{7}\oplus S^{2}_{0}V_{7}.

Proof 2.5.3
.

First note that
\End(V_{7})\simeq\mathbb{C}\oplus S^{2}_{0}V_{7}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}
under
\rmO_{7}
and thus under
G_{2}
. Further, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}\subset\mathfrak{so}_{7}\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}
is invariant, and irreducible by Theorem
2.4.5
. The equivariant map

V_{7}\longrightarrow\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7},\qquad v\longmapsto v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega

is injective since
\omega
is nondegenerate. For dimensional reasons, it follows that

\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}\simeq V_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}.

The infinitesimal action on
\omega
now gives a map

\End(V_{7})\longrightarrow\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7},\qquad A\longmapsto A_{\ast}\omega.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.5.1
, the kernel of this map is precisely
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
, and counting dimensions we see that it must be surjective. Using the decomposition of
\End(V_{7})
, we arrive at the desired statement.

Remark 2.5.4
.

The representations occurring in this decomposition are irreducible. This is clear for
V_{7}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, since
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts transitively on
S^{6}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}
by Corollary
3
. One may employ the theory of highest weights to see that there is a
27
-dimensional irreducible representation of
G_{2}
, necessarily contained in
S^{2}V_{7}
; hence it must coincide with the
27
-dimensional representation
S^{2}_{0}V_{7}
.

The Cartan–Killing classification of simple Lie algebras is mainly based on root systems, which are finite combinatorial objects encoding the Lie algebra structure. The root system of type
G_{2}
is made of twelve roots, described as vectors in a real plane. Up to symmetries one can choose
so-called simple roots, a short one
\alpha_{1}
and a long one
\alpha_{2}
, with an angle of
5\pi/6
between them; the full root system is depicted in Figure
2.1
. in terms of
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, such that
\alpha+\beta+\gamma=0
, the six short roots are
\pm\alpha,\pm\beta,\pm\gamma
, and the six long roots are
\pm(\alpha-\beta),\pm(\beta-\gamma),\pm(\gamma-\alpha)
.

\theta=\omega_{2}
\alpha=\omega_{1}
\alpha_{1}=(1,0)
\alpha_{2}=(\frac{-3}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2})
\beta
\gamma

Figure 2.1:
The root system of type
G_{2}

Dual to the simple roots are the
simple coroots
or
fundamental weights

\omega_{1}=2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=\alpha,\qquad\omega_{2}=3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}=\alpha-\gamma.

To every representation of a semisimple Lie algebra one can associate a set of weights, and any finite-dimensional irreducible representations is characterized (up to equivalence) by its
highest weight
. The possible highest weights are precisely the
dominant integral weights
, that is, nonnegative integer linear combinations of the fundamental weights. In the following, for every such dominant integral weight
\lambda
we shall denote the irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda
by
V_{\lambda}
. In this notation, we have

\displaystyle V_{\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=V_{7},

\displaystyle V_{\omega_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{2},

\displaystyle V_{2\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle=S^{2}_{0}V_{7}.

For a thorough introduction to the theory of roots and weights, we refer to
[
10
,
33
]
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
may be directly constructed from the root system as

\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda\text{ root}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda},

where
\mathfrak{h}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the Cartan subalgebra, and the
\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
are the (one-dimensional) root spaces. A detailed discussion is available in
[
33
, §22]
. For now, let us mention two remarkable shortcuts via subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
.

Proposition 5

(
[
33
]
, §22)
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
may be constructed as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\oplus V_{3}\oplus V_{3}^{\ast},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sl}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\lambda\text{ long root}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda},

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\beta}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\gamma},

\displaystyle V_{3}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\beta}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\gamma}.

Remark 2.5.5
.

Accordingly, the seven-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is obtained as

V_{7}=\mathbb{C}\oplus V_{3}\oplus V_{3}^{\ast}.

For a suitable basis of root vectors,
\mathbb{C}=\mathrm{span}\left\{e_{0}\right\}
,
V_{3}=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{\alpha},x_{\beta},x_{\gamma}\right\}
, and
V_{3}^{\ast}=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{-\alpha},x_{-\beta},x_{-\gamma}\right\}
, and there is an invariant three-form given by

\omega=x^{0}\wedge x^{\alpha}\wedge x^{-\alpha}+x^{0}\wedge x^{\beta}\wedge x^{-\beta}+x^{0}\wedge x^{\gamma}\wedge x^{-\gamma}+x^{\alpha}\wedge x^{\beta}\wedge x^{\gamma}+x^{-\alpha}\wedge x^{-\beta}\wedge x^{-\gamma},

(2.10)

where
x^{0},x^{\alpha}\ldots x^{-\gamma}
denotes the dual basis.
Note that this is precisely the form
\omega_{5}
from Theorem
2.2.1
by setting
e^{1}=x^{0}
,
e^{2}=x^{\alpha}
,
e^{3}=x^{-\alpha}
,
e^{4}=x^{\beta}
,
e^{5}=x^{-\beta}
,
e^{6}=x^{\gamma}
, and
e^{7}=x^{-\gamma}
.

The corresponding quadratic form
q
is

q=2(x^{0})^{2}-x^{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}-x^{\beta}x^{-\beta}-x^{\gamma}x^{-\gamma},

(2.11)

2.6
Exercises

Exercise 2.6.1
.

\noway

☞
The ranks of the quadratic forms
q_{\omega_{i}}
associated to the three-forms
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
,
\omega_{3}
,
\omega_{4}
,
\omega_{5}
appearing in Theorem
2.2.1
are respectively
1,1,2,4,
and
7
.

Exercise 2.6.2
.

\noway

☞
Since
K=IJ
, one can write
x=(x_{0}+x_{1}I)+(x_{2}+x_{3}I)J
and see
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}
. Show that under this isomorphism, quaternion multiplication is given in terms of complex multiplication and conjugation by the rule

(a,b)\cdot(c,d)=(ac-\overline{d}b,b\overline{c}+da)

(2.12)

for
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
. This way to obtain
\mathbb{H}
from
\mathbb{C}
is called the
Cayley–Dickson doubling process
.

Exercise 2.6.3
.

Prove that
\mathbb{O}
has the
alternativity
property: for any
x,y\in\mathbb{O}
,

x(xy)=(x^{2})y,\qquad(yx)x=y(x^{2}),\qquad(xy)x=x(yx).

Conclude that any two elements of
\mathbb{O}
are contained in an associative subalgebra.

Exercise 2.6.4
.

Prove the Moufang identities: for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{O}
,

z(x(zy))=((zx)z)y,\qquad x(z(yz))=((xz)y)z,\qquad(zx)(yz)=(z(xy))z=z((xy)z).

Exercise 2.6.5
.

Recall the definition of conjugation in a composition algebra:

u\mapsto\bar{u}:=2\langle u,1_{\mathbb{A}}\rangle-u,\qquad u\in\mathbb{A}.

Show that associativity of a normed division algebra
\mathbb{A}
is required for its Cayley–Dickson double to be normed. In particular, the
sedenions
, obtained from doubling
\mathbb{O}
, are not a normed division algebra.

Exercise 2.6.6
.

✍
Show that the complexified quaternions
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
are isomorphic to the matrix algebra
M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(
Hint:
Search for Pauli matrices). In the same vein, show that
M_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a four-dimensional real split composition algebra. It is called the algebra of
split-quaternions
.

Exercise 2.6.7
.

\noway

☞
Show that if
(V,\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle)
carries a vector cross product
\times
, then
\mathbb{A}:=\mathbb{R}\oplus V
together with the product

(s,u)\cdot(t,v):=(st-\langle u,v\rangle,sv+tu+u\times v),\qquad s,t\in\mathbb{R},\ u,v\in V,

is a normed division algebra.

Exercise 2.6.8
.

[Sextonions, see
[
53
]
]

Let
N\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
be a two-dimensional subspace. Prove that
S:=N^{\perp}
is a six-dimensional subalgebra if and only if
N
is a null-plane. Prove that such
S
do exist, and that
S\simeq M_{2}(\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}
with multiplication given by

(A,v)\cdot(B,w)=(AB,\tr(A)w-Aw+Bv),\qquad A,B\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\ v,w\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

(One can use the fact that
G_{2}
acts transitively on the set of null planes.)
Since all null-planes are conjugate, we get a unique six-dimensional algebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, up to conjugation. We denote by
\mathbb{S}
and call it the
sextonion algebra
.

Exercise 2.6.9
.

\noway

☞
Show that
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts simply transitively on the set of basic triples. Conclude that
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
is connected and of dimension
14
.

Exercise 2.6.10
.

✍
In Proposition
5
, the action of
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
on itself,
V_{3}
, and
V_{3}^{\ast}
is canonical. Construct the missing parts of the bracket

V_{3}\times V_{3}\longrightarrow V_{3}^{\ast},\qquad V_{3}^{\ast}\times V_{3}^{\ast}\longrightarrow V_{3},\qquad V_{3}\times V_{3}^{\ast}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{3}

and verify the Jacobi identity.

Exercise 2.6.11
.

✍
Define the action of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
on the
V_{7}
of Remark
2.5.5
, and show that
\omega
is invariant and generic.

Exercise 2.6.12
.

Construct directly
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
from
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}=\mathfrak{sl}(A)\times\mathfrak{sl}(B)
, as well as the seven-dimensional representation as follows:

\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{sl}(A)\times\mathfrak{sl}(B)\oplus(A\otimes S^{3}B),\qquad V_{7}=B\oplus(A\otimes S^{2}B).

Solution

(Ex.
2.6.8
)
.

Let us prove the statement that: if
S
is a six-dimensional subalgebra, then
N:=S^{\perp}
is a null-plane. Notice that if
s\in S
also
\overline{s}\in S
. Moreover
N\subset V_{7}
. Let
s,s^{\prime}\in S
and
n\in N
. Applying the calculational rules in
[
74
, Lem. 1.3.2, 1.3.3]
we get
0=\langle n,ss^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\overline{s}n,s^{\prime}\rangle
, and therefore
S\cdot N\subset N
. Since
S
is six-dimensional, its orthogonal is two-dimensional, so the right multiplication by any element
n\in N
cannot be invertible. This shows that
N
is isotropic for the symmetric form. Let us look at the image
R_{n}(S)
of the right multiplication operator
R_{n}
by
n\in N
. The right multiplication version of Theorem
2.3.12
tells us that
R_{n}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
is four-dimensional, and since
S
has codimension two,
R_{n}(S)
has at most codimension two in
R_{n}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
; thus
R_{n}(S)=N
. So for any
n^{\prime}\in N
, we have
n^{\prime}=sn
for a certain
s\in S
. But then
n^{\prime}n=(sn)n=-q(n)s=0
. This shows that
N
is a null-plane.

Consider now
N
a null-plane. By Corollary
7
we know that
G_{2}
acts transitively on the set of null-planes. So we can suppose that
N\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is the plane spanned by
e_{4}+\mathrm{i}e_{5}
and
e_{6}+\mathrm{i}e_{7}
. Then

S:=N^{\perp}=\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus N=\mathrm{span}\{1,e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}+\mathrm{i}e_{5},e_{6}+\mathrm{i}e_{7}\}.

One can check directly that
S
is a subalgebra. Moreover, it contains a copy of
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\simeq M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; the motivated reader can check the multiplication rules in
S
. We remark that even though the sextonions
\mathbb{S}
are a subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the restriction of the quadratic form
q
to
\mathbb{S}
is degenerate (since
N\subset N^{\perp}
). Hence,
S
is not itself a composition algebra, and its existence does not contradict Theorem
2.3.4
.

Solution

(Ex.
2.6.9
)
.

Choose
e_{1}=
any vector of unit norm, then
e_{2}=
any vector of unit norm and orthogonal to
e_{1}
. Then let
e_{3}=e_{1}e_{2}
, it has unit norm and is orthogonal to
e_{1},e_{2}
. Finally choose
e_{4}
of unit norm, orthogonal to
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
, and let
e_{5}=e_{4}e_{1}
,
e_{6}=e_{4}e_{2}
,
e_{7}=e_{4}e_{3}
: you get the correct multiplication table.

The vector
e_{1}\in S^{6}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}
can be chosen arbitrarily, while
e_{2}\in S^{5}\subset e_{1}^{\perp}
and
e_{4}\in S^{3}\subset\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{1}e_{2}\}^{\perp}
. Thus
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
is diffeomorphic to a bundle over
S^{6}
whose fiber is itself a bundle over
S^{5}
with fiber
S^{3}
. In particular it is connected and of dimension
6+5+3=14
.

Chapter 3
A few
G_{2}
-varieties

In this lecture, we primarily focus on the complex group
G_{2}
. Our main objective is to study various interesting projective varieties acted on by
G_{2}
, with particular emphasis on highlighting how octonionic geometry and the representation theory of
G_{2}
are deeply intertwined with their geometry. We will start by looking at the two homogeneous
G_{2}
-varieties, the 5-dimensional quadric
\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{P}^{6}
and the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}})
.
We will then move on to the study of specific families of linear spaces that are acted on by
G_{2}
: the Cayley Grassmannian
\CGr\subset\OGr(3,7)
, its "doubled version"
\DGr\subset\OGr(6,14)
, the Lie Grassmannian
\LieGr(2,7)\subset\OGr(2,7)
and the horospherical
G_{2}
-variety
HG\subset\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{g_{2}}^{\mathbb{C}})
. All have special properties that we will discuss for they own sake, but also place in a more general context.

3.1
Homogeneous varieties associated to
G_{2}

The six-sphere

Over
\mathbb{R}
, the group
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts transitively on imaginary octonions of norm one. Such octonions are parametrized by the 6-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{6}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{7}
, therefore
\mathbb{S}^{6}
is a homogeneous space of
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
(Corollary
3
). As it turns out:

\mathbb{S}^{6}\simeq G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/\SU_{3}.

To prove this isomorphism, it is sufficient to compute the isotropy subgroup of an arbitrary point
x\in\mathbb{S}^{6}
. Since
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}\simeq\Aut(\mathbb{O})
, each
\phi\in G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
preserves both the real and imaginary parts of the octonionic product. If we then take an element
\phi\in\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)
,
\phi
must also fix the entire linear space
V_{x}:=x^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O})
. This also tells us that
\phi
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
V_{x}
which, as
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}\subset\SO_{7}(\mathbb{R})
, must lie in
\SO_{6}(\mathbb{R})
. In other words, the restriction to
V_{x}
defines an injective morphism
\rho_{x}\colon\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)\to\SO_{6}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
x^{2}=-1
, the linear space
V_{x}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{6}
admits a complex structure defined by left-multiplication by
x
. By definition, given
\phi\in\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)
and
v\in V_{x}
,
\phi(x\cdot v)=x\cdot\phi(v)
, hence the image of
\rho_{x}
preserves the complex structure, that is, lies in
\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
.
Since, moreover,
\phi
preserves both (nondegenerate) bilinear forms
\langle\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle|_{V_{x}}
and
(x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)|_{V_{x}}
, it must also preserve the Hermitian form
h:=\langle\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,\rangle|_{V_{x}}+\mathrm{i}(x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)|_{V_{x}}
. Hence, the image of
\rho_{x}
is contained in
\SU_{3}=\SO_{6}(\mathbb{R})\cap\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
. Counting dimensions, this allows us to conclude that
\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)\simeq\SU_{3}
.

Since
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts linearly on
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O})
, we can extend its action to
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}))\simeq\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
, the projective space of lines in
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O})
through the origin. This action is still transitive and, as a result:
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}\simeq G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)
for an arbitrary point
x\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
. Let us consider the projection
\pi\colon\mathbb{S}^{6}\to\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
(mapping a point
x\in\mathbb{S}^{6}
to the line spanned by
x
and the origin) and take an arbitrary point
y\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
. The preimage
\pi^{-1}(y)
consists of two antipodal points
\pm x
in
\mathbb{S}^{6}
; therefore,
\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(y)=\{\pm\phi\mid\phi\in\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(x)\}
. Accordingly
\Stab_{G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}}(y)=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ltimes\SU_{3}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{R}}^{6}\simeq G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ltimes\SU_{3})
.

The quadric and the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian

Let us discuss what this result becomes over the
complex octonions
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{O}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. The complexification of the quadratic form
q
on
\mathbb{O}
to a quadratic form on
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, as well as its restriction to
V_{7}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, shall still be denoted by
q
. Moreover, all linear spaces
\mathbb{P}^{n}
will implicitly be complex projective spaces.

As already mentioned, the first noticeable difference when we work over
\mathbb{C}
, rather than
\mathbb{R}
, is that there exist non-zero imaginary octonions whose “norm" is zero. The quadratic form
q
indeed defines a quadric hypersurface
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
in
\mathbb{P}(V_{7})=\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))
, though without real points. In fact, there are entire linear spaces of octonions all having zero norm, as we already saw in Theorem
2.3.12
. For what concerns the action of
G_{2}
on
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, the complement of the quadric is a
G_{2}
-orbit, and one can prove that
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))\setminus\mathbb{Q}^{5}\simeq G_{2}/(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ltimes\SL_{3})
(
\noway
☞
Exercise
3.6.1
). This implies the existence of an embedding
\SL_{3}\hookrightarrow G_{2}
. We already met such an embedding in the last section (cf. Proposition
5
), where we saw that the subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus_{\lambda\in\Delta_{l}}\mathfrak{g}_{\lambda}
, generated by the set
\Delta_{l}
of long roots, is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
.

Let us recall some further features of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
that will be needed later. We have two fundamental representations of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
: the adjoint representation and the seven-dimensional standard representation. The highest weight of the adjoint representation is the highest root
\omega_{2}=3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
. The fundamental seven-dimensional representation is generated by the short roots; its highest weight is
\omega_{1}=2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
, that we also denote by
\alpha
; the weights are
0
and the short roots (see Remark
2.5.5
).

As previously stated, when we pass to the complex octonions, there appear two-dimensional linear subspaces of
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
on which all octonionic products are zero. Such linear spaces are called
null-planes
(Definition
2.3.14
).
They are parametrized by a closed subvariety
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\subset\Gr(2,7)
, on which
G_{2}
acts transitively.
As it turns out, it is possible to obtain
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
as the zero locus of a global section of a homogeneous vector bundle on the Grassmannian
\Gr(2,7)
.
Recall that on
\Gr(2,7)
there is a
tautological vector bundle

\mathcal{U}
of rank two (this is simply the vector bundle whose fiber over a point
[V]
is the space
V
itself), which fits into an “Euler type" short exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}\otimes V_{7}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}\longrightarrow 0.

Here
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}
is the trivial bundle on
\Gr(2,7)
and
\mathcal{Q}
is the tautological quotient bundle. We also let
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}(1)=\det(\mathcal{Q})
,
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}(-1)=\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}(1)^{*}
, and for any vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote
\mathcal{V}(n):=\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}(1)^{\otimes n}
.

The invariant three-form
\omega
defines a global section of
\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
as follows. For any pair of linearly independent vectors
x,y
in
V_{7}
spanning
U:=\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}
, the linear form
\omega(x,y,\cdot\,)

clearly annihilates
U
, hence defines an element in
(V_{7}/U)^{*}
. Thus
\omega
defines a section of
(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{U})^{\ast}\otimes\mathcal{Q}^{\ast}=\mathcal{Q}^{\ast}(1)
.

Proposition 6
.

G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\subset\Gr(2,7)
is the zero-locus of
\omega
regarded as a section of the rank-five
vector bundle
\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
.

Proof 3.1.1
.

For a pair of linearly independent vectors
x,y
in
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, the linear form
\omega(x,y,\cdot\,)
on
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
is identically zero if and only if the octonionic product
xy
belongs to
\mathbb{C}\,1=\operatorname{Re}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, see (
2.6
).
But if
xy=t1
with
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, then since
x\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
we deduce that
y=\frac{t}{|x|^{2}}\overline{x}=\frac{-t}{|x|^{2}}x
,
contradicting the linear independence of
x
and
y
.

Corollary 7
.

G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
is smooth of dimension five, homogeneous under the action of
G_{2}
.

Proof 3.1.2
.

The zero locus
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
of
\omega\in H^{0}(G(2,7),\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1))
is stable under the action of
\Stab_{\SL_{7}}(\omega)
, which is
G_{2}
. By generic smoothness,
the genericity of
\omega
implies that its zero locus is smooth of the expected codimension
5=\rank(\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1))
.
By Lemma
3.1.5
, there exists a homogeneous space
G_{2}/P
, with
P
parabolic, that is contained in
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
. Now, parabolic subgroups of
G_{2}
are classified, and up to conjugation their dimension can only be equal to
9
or
8
. This shows that
G_{2}/P
in fact must coincide with
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
, which is thus homogeneous.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Given a semisimple Lie group
G
, a
parabolic subgroup
of
G
is a closed subgroup that contains a Borel subgroup of
G
(a maximal, closed connected solvable subgroup). A
homogeneous space
will be in these lectures a quotient
G/P
of a semisimple group by a proper parabolic subgroup.

Remark 3.1.4
.

By
[
64
, Theorem 7.5]
, parabolic subgroups of a semisimple group
G
are those subgroups
P
whose quotient
G/P
is projective. Then homogeneous spaces are rational projective varieties.
In the literature, our
homogeneous spaces
are often referred to as
generalized flag varieties
(or
generalized Grassmannians
when the parabolic subgroup is maximal), while a homogeneous space is simply a variety with a transitive group action.

Fact 8
.

Since parabolic subgroups are classified, the same is true for homogeneous spaces. Moreover, homogeneous vector bundles (vector bundles with a compatible action of
G
) on homogeneous spaces
G/P
are in
1:1
correspondence with representations of
P
. In particular, irreducible homogeneous vector bundles are classified by their highest weight. See
[
64
]
for details.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
G
be a semisimple group and
V
be a
G
-representation with no trivial factor. Then every
G
-stable projective variety
X\subset\mathbb{P}(V)
contains a homogeneous space.

Proof 3.1.6
.

Let
B
be a Borel subgroup of
G
. By Borel’s fixed point Theorem, there exists a point
x\in X
which is fixed by
B
. Then the stabilizer
P
of
x
in
G
is a closed subgroup containing
B
, that is, a parabolic subgroup. Moreover, the point
x
cannot be fixed by the whole

G
, since otherwise it would define a one-dimensional representation of
G
in
V
, i.e., a trivial factor. Hence, the
G
-orbit
G\cdot x
is closed and projective, and of positive dimension, thus a homogeneous space.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Let
V
be a non-trivial irreducible representation of a semisimple group
G
. Then
\mathbb{P}(V)
contains a unique homogeneous space.

Proof 3.1.8
.

From the proof of the previous lemma, the result follows if we can show that
\mathbb{P}(V)
contains a unique
B
-fixed point. However, this is equivalent to the fact that, since
V
is irreducible, it has a unique highest weight vector up to scalar (we let the reader fill up the details with the help of any representation theory textbook, for instance
[
33
]
).

We keep focusing our attention on the projective space
\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})
; in particular, we will establish what the other
G_{2}
homogeneous subvarieties of this space are. Recall that the vector space
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}
decomposes as the direct sum of two irreducible
G_{2}
-representations (see
[
33
, §22]
or the proof of Proposition
4
):

\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}=V_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}

From this decomposition we deduce that there are two closed
G_{2}
-orbits in
\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})
, one in
\mathbb{P}(V_{7})
and the other in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{g}_{2})
. From the general theory of rational homogeneous varieties, since
V_{7}=V_{\omega_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=V_{\omega_{2}}
these two orbits must be isomorphic with
G_{2}/P_{1}
and
G_{2}/P_{2}
, where
P_{1},P_{2}
are the two maximal parabolic subgroups associated with the simple roots
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
(see
[
64
]
for a complete treatment of homogeneous varieties). Moreover we know that their dimensions are both equal to
5
. This immediately implies that
G_{2}/P_{1}=\mathbb{Q}^{5}
and
G_{2}/P_{2}=G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
.

Since
V_{7}
embeds in
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}
via
v\mapsto v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega
.
and any
[v]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
is in the
G_{2}
-orbit of
[x_{\alpha}]
,
a direct computation shows that

[v]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}\quad\implies\quad\,\,\,\,\,\rank(v\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)=4.

In particular the image of
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
does not meet the Grassmannian
\Gr(2,7)
.

Since
B=P_{1}\cap P_{2}
is a Borel subgroup of
G_{2}
, there is an incidence diagram:

\textstyle{G_{2}/B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(V_{7})\supset\mathbb{Q}^{5}=G_{2}/P_{1}}
\textstyle{G_{2}/P_{2}=G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}).}

(3.1)

where the fibers of both projections are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
From a geometric perspective, the diagram can be interpreted as follows.
Each null-plane
\mathrm{span}\left\{u,v\right\}\subset V_{7}
is fixed by a subgroup of
G_{2}
isomorphic to
P_{2}
, and the stabilizer (in
P_{2}
) of an arbitrary point in this null-plane is isomorphic to a Borel subgroup
B\subset P_{2}\subset G_{2}
. In other words, we can identify
P_{2}/B
with the projective line
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{span}\left\{u,v\right\})
, the full flag manifold
G_{2}/B
with the incidence variety

G_{2}/B\simeq\left\{(u,l)\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}\times G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\mid u\subset l\right\},

and the fibration
G_{2}/B\to G_{2}/P_{2}
with the tautological
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration over
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
.
The fiber over a point
u\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
is the family of null-planes through
u
; since the latter must be isomorphic to
P_{1}/B\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we deduce that each point of the quadric belongs to a family of null-planes parametrized by
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Remark 3.1.9
.

Since any point
