u\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
defines a line in
G_{2}/P_{2}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{g}_{2})
, we get an embedding

\mathbb{Q}_{5}\hookrightarrow\Gr(2,\mathfrak{g}_{2}).

Pulling-back the tautological bundle on the Grassmannian, we deduce a special
G_{2}
-homogeneous vector bundle on
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
, called the
Cayley bundle

[
63
]
.

Diagram (
3.1
) is a subdiagram of the usual incidence diagram for lines and planes,

\textstyle{\Fl(1,2,7)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(V_{7})=\mathbb{P}^{6}}
\textstyle{\Gr(2,7)\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})\simeq\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{so}_{7})}

where
\Fl(1,2,7)
denotes the variety of
(1,2)
-flags in
V_{7}
. The fibers of
\pi_{1}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{5}
and the fibers of
\pi_{2}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Remark 3.1.10
.

The homogeneous varieties
G_{2}/P_{1}
,
G_{2}/P_{2}
and
G_{2}/B
discussed above may also be expressed as homogeneous spaces (in the group-theoretic sense) of the compact group
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
. For any complex semisimple Lie group with compact real form
G^{\mathrm{c}}
, the generalized flag manifolds (also called
Kähler C-spaces
) are described equivalently as

1.

quotients
G/P
by a parabolic subgroup,

2.

quotients
G^{\mathrm{c}}/K
by the centralizer
K=G^{\mathrm{c}}\cap P
of a torus,

see for example
[
4
,
15
]
. Under this correspondence, the full flag manifold is given by
G_{2}/B\simeq G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/T^{2}
, with
T^{2}\subset G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
a maximal torus. The flag manifolds from viewpoint (2) are explicitly known – see
[
9
, §8.H]

for the classical groups. For the flag manifolds of
G_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{Q}^{5}=G_{2}/P_{1}=G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/\U_{2}^{-}

with
\U_{2}^{-}
embedded by
\U_{2}\subset\SO_{4}(\mathbb{R})\subset G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
, while

G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)=G_{2}^{\ad}=G_{2}/P_{2}=G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}/\U_{2}^{+}

with
\U_{2}^{+}
embedded by
\U_{2}\subset\SU_{3}\subset G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
, cf.
[
15
]
. Moreover the quadric, defined only in terms of
q
, is naturally a homogeneous space of
\SO_{7}
, indeed also a generalized flag manifold; it can be written as

\mathbb{Q}^{5}\simeq\SO_{7}(\mathbb{R})/(\SO_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\SO_{5}(\mathbb{R})),

which is also its presentation as a compact symmetric space
[
38
]
.

The orthogonal Grassmannian

Definition 3.1.11
.

The
orthogonal Grassmannians
of
V_{7}
are defined as

\OGr(k,V_{7})=\OGr(k,7):=\left\{\,q\text{-isotropic}k\text{-planes in }V_{7}\,\right\}=\left\{\,[P]\in\Gr(k,7)\mid q|_{P}\equiv 0\,\right\}.

They are non-empty only for
k\leq 3
, and
\OGr(1,7)\simeq\mathbb{Q}^{5}
. The action of
\SO_{7}
preserving the quadratic form
q
makes any
\OGr(k,7)
a homogeneous space.
Also notice that
\OGr(2,7)
is isomorphic to the
\SO_{7}
-adjoint variety (see Definition
5.1.1
), as it is a closed orbit inside
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{so}_{7})
:

\OGr(2,7)\simeq\SO_{7}^{\ad}\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})\simeq\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{so}_{7}).

Remark 3.1.12
.

As we will see,
\OGr(k,7)
is not in general a homogeneous space of
G_{2}
. However, it is a generalized flag manifold of
\SO_{7}
, and we can write
\OGr(k,7)=\SO_{7}/P_{k}
,
1\leq k\leq 3
, where
P_{k}
is the parabolic corresponding to the
k
-th simple root of
\SO_{7}
.
We can also describe it as a homogeneous space of the compact group
\SO_{7}(\mathbb{R})
. The Dynkin type of the isotropy group is determined by deleting the
k
-th root from the Dynkin diagram of
\SO_{7}
; this leads (cf.
[
9
, Ex. 8.115]
) to

\OGr(k,7)\simeq\SO_{7}(\mathbb{R})/(\U(k)\times\SO_{7-2k}(\mathbb{R})),\qquad 1\leq k\leq 3.

Definition 3.1.13
.

For any
[x]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
, let
K_{x}:=\ker(L_{x}|_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}})
be the kernel of the left-multiplication by
x
on
V_{7}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
By Lemma
2.3.10
,
x
is a zero divisor, and by Corollary
2
,
K_{x}
is a three-dimensional isotropic subspace containing
x
, namely

K_{x}=x\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}).

In particular,
\mathbb{P}(K_{x})\subset\mathbb{Q}^{5}
. We can also write
K_{x}
in terms of the associative three-form (Exercise
3.6.2
):

K_{x}=\ker(x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega).

Lemma 3.1.14
.

The two-planes
N\subset K_{x}
through
x
are precisely the null planes containing
x
.

Proof 3.1.15
.

If
N
is a null-plane through
x
, then
N\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
by Lemma
2.3.15
, so
N\subset K_{x}
by definition.

Conversely, let
N=\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}
for some
y\in K_{x}
. Then, by definition,
xy=0
. On the other hand
y^{2}=-q(y)=0
since
y
is imaginary and
K_{x}
is isotropic, and the same goes for
x
. By bilinearity, all octonionic products of elements of
N
vanish, so
N
is a null-plane.

Projectivizing, these null-planes give lines in the quadric which we will also call
G_{2}
-lines
. It is crucial to note that not all lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
are
G_{2}
-lines (
\noway
☞
Exercise
3.6.3
); in fact, the union of all lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
is
\OGr(2,7)
.

Information about the family of lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
can also be deduced from the Dynkin diagram of
G_{2}
:

\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{1}

Following
[
52
, Section 4]
(see also Remark
6.3.1
), since
\alpha_{1}
is a short root, the family of lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\simeq G_{2}/P_{1}
is irreducible with two
G_{2}
-orbits, the closed one being
G_{2}/P_{2}\simeq G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
whilst the open one must be the complement
\OGr(2,7)\setminus G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
. We will see later on how to identify
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
with a zero locus of a section of a vector bundle on
\OGr(2,7)
(Remark
3.3.3
).

From Exercise
3.6.3
we will learn that the image of the embedding

\mathbb{Q}^{5}\longrightarrow\OGr(3,7),\qquad[x]\longmapsto[K_{x}]

is a family of planes in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
itself. From the definition of this family of isotropic planes
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\subset\OGr(3,7)
, we also deduce the following.

Lemma 3.1.16
.

The maximal dimension of a linear subspace of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
on which all octonionic products vanish is two; any such maximal linear subspace is a null-plane.

Proof 3.1.17
.

A linear subspace
N\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
on which all octonionic products are zero must necessarily be contained in
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
and be isotropic with respect to the quadratic form (Lemma
2.3.15
). Moreover, it must satisfy
N\subset K_{x}
for any
x\in N
, thus
\dim N\leq\dim K_{x}=3
. But we will prove in Exercise
3.6.3
that the general point in
\Gr(2,K_{x})
is not a null-plane; as a consequence
\dim(N)\leq 2
with equality holding if and only if
N
is a null-plane.

Lines in the
G_{2}
-varieties

Definition 3.1.18
.

For a projective variety
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we denote by

\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(X)=\left\{\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}\text{ linear}\,|\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset X\right\}

the
Fano variety of lines
in
X
(see also Appendix
6.3
).

Inside the Grassmannian
X=\Gr(2,7)\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})
, lines are parametrized by
\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\Gr(2,7))\simeq\Fl(1,3,7)
, the variety of partial flags
(L_{1}\subset L_{3})
, where
L_{k}\subset V_{7}
is a subspace with
\dim L_{k}=k
(see Example
6.3
); any line in
\Gr(2,7)
is of the form

\left\{[P]\in\Gr(2,7)\,|\,L_{1}\subset P\subset L_{3}\right\}\simeq\mathbb{P}(L_{1}\wedge L_{3})\subset\mathbb{P}(\Lambda^{2}V_{7})

for some partial flag
(L_{1},L_{3})
. Let us see what happens for the lines in the orthogonal and
G_{2}
-Grassmannians.

Some terminology: we call a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
embedded smoothly in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
as a subvariety of degree three a
twisted cubic curve
. Moreover, by
pencil
we will mean a family of objects of some type, which are (algebraically) parametrized by a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
; e.g. a pencil of lines, a pencil of planes, etc.

Proposition 9
.

Lines in the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\subset\Gr(2,7)
are parametrized by

\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))\simeq\mathbb{Q}^{5}.

Lines in
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
passing through a fixed null-plane
[N]
are parametrized by a twisted cubic curve
\mathbb{P}(N)=\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))
.

Proof 3.1.19
.

Let us consider a point
(L_{1}=[x],L_{3})\in\Fl(1,3,7)
. By Lemma
3.1.14
, the line
\mathbb{P}(x\wedge L_{3})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
\Gr(2,7)
gives rise to a pencil of null-planes if and only if
[x]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
and
L_{3}=K_{x}
. Hence lines of
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
are parametrized by the map

\mathbb{Q}^{5}\longrightarrow\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)),\qquad[x]\mapsto\mathbb{P}(x\wedge K_{x})\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}).

In the following, we identify the line
\mathbb{P}(x\wedge K_{x})
with the partial flag
([x],K_{x})
.

Let us consider now the universal line
\mathcal{L}\subset\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))\times G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
; this is the subvariety whose fiber over a point
L\in\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))
is the corresponding pencil of null-planes. We have a diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}^{5}}
\textstyle{G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)}

(3.2)

where
p_{1}
is the composition of the projection onto the first factor with the isomorphism above, and
p_{2}
is the projection onto the second factor.

Given a null-plane
[N]=\mathrm{span}\left\{u,v\right\}\in G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
, its pre-image by
p_{2}
is the pencil of partial flags

p_{2}^{-1}([N])=\{([t_{0}u+t_{1}v],K_{t_{0}u+t_{1}v})\,|\,[t_{0}:t_{1}]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\}.

As
p_{1}(p_{2}^{-1}([N]))=\mathbb{P}(N)
, we get
an embedding

\tau_{[N]}:\quad\mathbb{P}(N)\to\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})

which sends a one-dimensional subspace of
N
to the corresponding tangent direction at
[N]
.

Let us show that
\tau_{[N]}
is a degree three Veronese embedding. To ease our task, we choose to work with
N:=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{\alpha},x_{-\beta}\right\}
– the general case can be reduced to this one by the transitivity of the
G_{2}
action on
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
.
To begin, consider the pencil of two-forms
\{[x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega]\,|\,x\in N\}
. Since
x
is a null vector, these forms all have rank 4 and define an embedding

\mathbb{P}(N)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}^{*}),\qquad[x]\longmapsto x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega.

Since
N
is a null-plane, the image lies in
\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\ann(N))
. For our particular choice of
N
we get:

\displaystyle(t_{0}x_{\alpha}+t_{1}x_{-\beta})\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega

\displaystyle=t_{0}(x^{0}\wedge x^{-\alpha}+x^{\beta}\wedge x^{\gamma})+t_{1}(x^{0}\wedge x^{\beta}+x^{-\alpha}\wedge x^{-\gamma}),

\displaystyle\ann(N)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{span}\left\{x^{0},x^{-\alpha},x^{\beta},x^{\gamma},x^{-\gamma}\right\}.

Let us denote by
\Pf(4,V_{7})\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}^{*})
the locus of two-forms of rank at most
4
. For any form
\alpha
of rank
4
, its square
\alpha\wedge\alpha
is decomposable and represents the four-dimensional support of
\alpha
. We thus have a rational map

\Pf(4,V_{7})\dashrightarrow\Gr(4,V_{7}^{*})\simeq\Gr(3,V_{7}),\qquad\alpha\mapsto\alpha\wedge\alpha

(rational since only defined on the open subset of forms of rank
4
). This is a quadratic map whose restriction to the pencil
\{[x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega]\,|\,x\in N\}
is a degree two Veronese embedding

\mathbb{P}(N)\xhookrightarrow{\nu_{2}}\Gr(4,\ann(N))\simeq\mathbb{P}(\ann(N)^{\ast}).

For our choice of
N
, we get:

\nu_{2}([t_{0}x_{\alpha}+t_{1}x_{-\beta}])=[t_{0}^{2}x_{-\gamma}+2t_{0}t_{1}x_{0}+t_{1}^{2}x_{\gamma}].

(3.3)

The pencil of lines through
[N]
therefore embeds into
\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}\Gr(2,7))\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7})
via:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}\Gr(2,7)),\quad[t_{0}:t_{1}]\mapsto

\displaystyle[(t_{0}x_{\alpha}+t_{1}x_{-\beta})\wedge\nu_{2}(t_{0}x_{\alpha}+t_{1}x_{-\beta})]

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle[t_{0}^{3}(x_{\alpha}\wedge x_{-\gamma})+2{t_{0}}^{2}t_{1}(x_{\alpha}\wedge x_{0})+2t_{0}t_{1}^{2}(x_{0}\wedge x_{-\beta})+t_{1}^{3}(x_{-\beta}\wedge x_{\gamma})]

which is indeed a twisted cubic curve.

Remark 3.1.20
.

To determine that the variety
\Sigma_{N}\subset\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))
of lines in
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
through a null-plane
N
is a rational curve, one could also simply look at the Dynkin diagram of
G_{2}
. The semisimple part of
P_{2}
is simply
\SL_{2}
(the Dynkin diagram obtained by erasing the node corresponding to
P_{2}
is the Dynkin diagram of
\SL_{2}
), and as
\alpha_{2}
is a long root,
\Sigma_{N}
is isomorphic to
SL_{2}/B\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
for
B\subset\SL_{2}
a Borel subgroup. That we get a twisted cubic reflects the triple bond in the Dynkin diagram of
G_{2}
(see
[
52
]
for further details, or Appendix
6.3
).

Remark 3.1.21
.

The image of
\tau_{[N]}
in
\mathbb{P}(T_{[N]}G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))
is called the
variety of minimal rational tangents
(VMRT) at the point
[N]
. In the general study of Fano manifolds, VMRT’s have been a tremendously useful tool, as they turn out to give extremely strong information on the global geometry on the variety. This is specially true for homogenous spaces, which are essentially characterized by their VMRT; a beautiful consequence is that homogeneous spaces of Picard number one admit no non-trivial Kähler deformation. Actually, there is one single exception to this statement: the orthogonal Grassmannian
\OGr(2,7)
! We refer to
[
43
,
39
,
40
]
for further details on this topic.

Let us come back to the universal line
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathbb{Q}^{5}\times G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
. The previous discussion shows that
\mathcal{L}
is a conic bundle over the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian.
Indeed, the pencil of
G_{2}
-lines through a given null-plane
N
is nothing but the pencil of isotropic planes in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\subset\OGr(3,7)
containing
N
; this is isomorphic to a smooth conic on
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
, i.e., the image of the quadratic embedding
\mu_{2}:\mathbb{P}(N)\to\Gr(3,V_{7}),\ x\mapsto K_{x}
.
Observe now that the planes in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\subset\OGr(3,7)
containing
\mathbb{P}(N)
are contained in the linear space
\mathbb{P}(N^{\perp})
; each one of them is therefore parametrized by a point in
\mathbb{P}(N^{\perp}/N)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Taking therefore a general 3-dimensional subspace
U\subset N^{\perp}
, disjoint from
N
we get
\mathbb{P}(N^{\perp}/N)\simeq\mathbb{P}(U)
; the set of isotropic planes containing
N
therefore also identifies with the smooth conic
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\cap\mathbb{P}(U)
, i.e., the image of a degree-2 Veronese embedding
\mathbb{P}(N)\to\mathbb{P}(U)
. Choosing
N=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}\right\}
as in the previous exercise, we get that, up to projective equivalence,
\mathbb{P}U=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{0},x_{\gamma},x_{-\gamma}\right\})
and the conic in
\mathbb{P}U
is therefore nothing but the image of the morphism
\nu_{2}
in Equation (
3.3
).

The families of linear spaces
N,\ N^{\perp},\ N^{\perp}/N
, for
[N]
varying in
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
, define vector bundles
\mathcal{U},\ \mathcal{U}^{\perp},\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}
over the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian. The bundle

\mathcal{U}
is simply the restriction of the rank two tautological bundle on
\Gr(2,7)
,
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
is the pullback of the rank 5 tautological bundle on
\Gr(5,V_{7})
under the isomorphism
\Gr(2,V_{7})\simeq\Gr(2,V_{7}^{*})\simeq\Gr(5,V_{7})
induced by
q
. Finally, the composition

\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}\otimes V_{7}\to(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr}\otimes V_{7})/\mathcal{U}^{\perp}

is zero on
\OGr(2,7)
(by the very definition of
\OGr(2,7)
), therefore
\mathcal{U}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
. The quotient of this inclusion defines a rank-3 vector bundle
\mathcal{Q}
(we will adopt the same notation for the restriction to
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
).
We therefore have a
\mathbb{P}^{2}
-bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q})\xrightarrow{\pi}G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
, and the quadratic form
q
defines a global section of
\pi^{*}S^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}
. The previous proof shows that
\mathcal{L}
is the zero locus of this global section.

In fact it is easy to check that
\mathcal{L}
is homogeneous under
G_{2}
, and conclude that
the incidence diagram (
3.2
) coincides with (
3.1
). It contains a lot of geometric information about
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
and
G_{2}
.

1.

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are the two fundamental representations (from which all the others can be constructed).

2.

\mathbb{Q}^{5}
and
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
are the two generalized Grassmannians: the two minimal orbits in the (projectivized) fundamental representations. They have the same dimension and Betti numbers as
\mathbb{P}^{5}
. In particular, the Picard group is
\mathbb{Z}H
, but with
H^{5}=2
for
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
and
H^{5}=18
for
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
.

3.

It is possible to recover
G_{2}
from
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
in the sense that
G_{2}=\Aut(G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7))
(see
[
77
, page 118]
, or
[
23
, Theorem 1]
).

Remark 3.1.22
.

\mathbb{Q}^{5}
and
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
are both Fano varieties, of index
5
and
3
respectively (the index of a Fano manifold
X
is the largest integer
m
such that the canonical divisor
K_{X}=-mH
for some divisor
H
). By adjunction, a codimension two linear section of
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
is a Fano threefold of index one, and a codimension three linear section is a surface with trivial canonical bundle, in fact a K3 surface.
(The fact that they easily provide families of Fano manifolds or K3 surfaces is yet another reason to be interested in homogeneous spaces!)

It was proved by Mukai
[
60
]
that any prime Fano threefold of degree
18
is a section of the
G_{2}
-Grassmannian. The same statement holds for a
general
polarized K3 surface of degree
18
(a polarized K3 surface consists of a pair
(S,L)
where
S
is a smooth simply connected surface with trivial canonical bundle and
L
is an ample line bundle of degree
d=L^{2}
).

3.2
The Cayley Grassmannian

Inside the Grassmannian
\Gr(4,V_{7}),

The
Cayley Grassmannian

\CGr
is defined as the closed subvariety parameterizing 4-planes that are isotropic with respect to
\omega
:
[V]\in\CGr
if and only if
\omega|_{V}\equiv 0
. Recall that the index of a Fano variety was defined in Remark
3.1.22
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

\CGr
is a smooth
8
-dimensional Fano variety of index
4
.

Proof 3.2.2
.

Denote by
\mathcal{T}
the rank 4 tautological bundle on
\Gr(4,V_{7})
. The invariant three-form

\omega
defines a global section
s_{\omega}
of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathcal{T}^{*}
. This is a rank 4 globally generated vector bundle and, as
s_{\omega}
is generic, its zero locus is smooth of codimension
4
; so
\CGr
is smooth of dimension
8
. The generality assumption on
\omega
also ensures that
\CGr
has Picard rank one (see
[
26
]
, Thm 2.2), and its index can be computed by the
adjunction formula
for zero loci of sections of vector bundles: since
K_{\Gr(4,7)}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\Gr(4,7)}(-7)
and
\det(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{O}_{\Gr(4,7)}(3)
, we get

K_{\CGr}\simeq K_{\Gr(4,7)}\otimes\det(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathcal{T})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\CGr}(-4),

whose dual is ample on
\CGr
.
This ensures that the index is a multiple of four; it were bigger than four, general results would imply that it is equal to
8
and that
\CGr
is a quadric, which is not the case.

Proposition 10
.

A codimension
3
subspace
B\subset V_{7}
belongs to
\CGr
if and only if its orthogonal
B^{\perp}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}.

Proof 3.2.3
.

Recall that
\omega
is given in terms of octonionic multiplication by
\omega(x,y,z)=Re((xy)z)
.
The condition that
\omega
vanishes identically on
B
is therefore equivalent to
B.B\subset B^{\perp}.

Suppose this is the case. For
x\in B
, the left multiplication operator by
x
is thus an
endomorphism of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
that maps
B
to
B^{\perp}
. Note that since the invariant quadratic form
q
is nondegenerate,
B
cannot be isotropic; for
x
general in
B
,
q(x)\neq 0
,
L_{x}
is an isomorphism and proportional to its own inverse. This implies that
L_{x}
also maps
B^{\perp}
to
B
, and since this is true for
x
general in
B
, it must be true for any
x\in B
. This means that
B.B^{\perp}\subset B
. But then, if
x\in B
and
y,z\in B^{\perp}\cap Im(\mathbb{O})
, we get

(x,yz)=-(xz,y)=0
since
xz\in B
. So
B^{\perp}.B^{\perp}\subset B^{\perp}
, which means that
B^{\perp}
is a subalgebra of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

The converse implication is similar and left to the reader.

So, the Cayley Grassmannian parametrizes the four-dimensional subalgebras of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Over the real numbers, all subalgebras of
\mathbb{O}
are isomorphic to the quaternion algebra
\mathbb{H}
. Over the complex numbers, this is no longer true, since there exist
degenerate subalgebras
.

Definition 3.2.4
.

A subalgebra
\mathbb{A}\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is said to be nondegenerate if the restriction of the octonionic norm to
\mathbb{A}
is a nondegenerate quadratic form. Otherwise,
\mathbb{A}
is degenerate.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Any 4-dimensional nondegenerate subalgebra
\mathbb{A}\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof 3.2.6
.

Choose two orthogonal norm-one vectors
e_{1},e_{2}
in

\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
; then
{e_{i}}^{2}=-e_{i}\overline{e_{i}}=-1

for
i=1,2
.
Let
e_{3}=e_{1}e_{2}=-e_{2}e_{1};
this an octonion of norm one and since
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is alternative, the subalgebra generated by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
is associative, therefore
e_{1}e_{3}=-e_{2}
and
e_{2}e_{3}=e_{1}
. To conclude the proof, it suffices to show that
e_{3}\not\in\mathrm{span}\left\{1,e_{1},e_{2}\right\}.

From the choice of
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
is imaginary since

\langle e_{3},1\rangle=\langle e_{1}e_{2},1\rangle=\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle=0
. Then suppose that
e_{3}=\alpha e_{1}+\beta e_{2}
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
; multiplying on the left by
e_{1}
yields
-e_{2}=-\alpha 1+\beta e_{3}
, hence
\alpha=0
by taking real parts; and similarly
\beta=0
.

Let us now pass to the study of degenerate subalgebras
\mathbb{A}
.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{A}
is degenerate. Then
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
contains a null-plane
N
.

Proof 3.2.8
.

The degeneracy assumption on
\mathbb{A}
is equivalent to the existence of a non-zero element
x\in\mathbb{A}
such that
\mathbb{A}\subset x^{\perp}.
Observe that such an
x
must necessarily lie in
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})\cap\mathbb{Q}^{5}\subset\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, as
\langle x,1\rangle=0=\langle x,x\rangle
. Moreover the left multiplication operator
L_{x}
preserves
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})

since
\operatorname{Re}(xy)=-(x,\overline{y})=-(x,y)
. But
x
being an imaginary octonion of norm zero,

x^{2}=0
and thus
L_{x}^{2}=0
(
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
being alternative). So
L_{x}
has rank at most one as an endomorphism of
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
, and there exists

y\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
, independent of
x
, such that
xy=0
(and
yx=0
by conjugating).
Necessarily
y
has zero norm, being a zero divisor, and therefore
N=\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}
is a null-plane.

Using these preliminary results we can classify, up to the
G_{2}
action, the 4-dimensional subalgebras
\mathbb{A}
of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. Denote by
r
the rank of the restriction of the octonionic norm to
\mathbb{A}.

Proposition 11
.

Up to the action of
G_{2}
, there are only three possibilities:

1.

r=4
, then
\mathbb{A}
is a quaternion algebra;

2.

r=2
, then there exists a unique null-plane
N
such that
N\subset\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})\subset N^{\perp}

3.

r=1
, and there exists a unique isotropic line
\ell
such that
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})=\ell\mathbb{O}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O})
.

Proof 3.2.9
.

If
r=4,

\mathbb{A}
is nondegenerate, hence isomorphic to
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
by Lemma
3.2.5
. Suppose that
\mathbb{A}
is degenerate; by Lemma
3.2.7
,
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
contains a null-plane
N
. Suppose there is another one, say
N^{\prime}
, and let
\ell
belong to
N\cap N^{\prime}
. Then
\ell.\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})=\ell.(N+N^{\prime})=0
and we can conclude that
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})=\ell\mathbb{O}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O})
and
r=1
.

It follows that if
r=2
,
N
is unique. What remains to prove is that any
z\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
is orthogonal to
N=\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}
, with the notations of Lemma
3.2.7
. For this we note that

xz
belongs to
\mathbb{A}
and is killed by left multiplication by
x
, which implies that
xz\in N
.
So
xz=\alpha x+\beta y
for some scalars
\alpha,\beta
, that we can write as
\beta y=(x-\alpha 1)z
. Since
z\neq 0
and
x-\alpha 1
is invertible, we deduce that
\beta\neq 0
. Then
y
is a linear combination of
xz
and
x
, hence also
yz
. In particular
Re(yz)=Re(xz)=0
, hence
z\in N^{\perp}
.

The previous classification leads to the following

Proposition 12
.

The action of
G_{2}
on
\CGr
has three orbits:

1.

the open one is isomorphic with
G_{2}/SL_{2}\times SL_{2};

2.

the intermediate one is a complement to a conic bundle in a
\mathbb{P}^{2}
-bundle over
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
;

3.

the closed one is isomorphic to the quadric
\mathbb{Q}^{5}.

Proof 3.2.10
.

Given a four-dimensional nondegenerate subalgebra
\mathbb{A}
, with a basis

e_{1},\>e_{2}\>e_{3}
of
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{A})
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2.5
, we can choose a norm-one element
e_{4}
orthogonal to
\mathbb{A}
and define
e_{5}=e_{3}e_{4},\ e_{6}=e_{2}e_{4},\ e_{7}=e_{4}e_{1}
. The multiplication table of octonions in this basis is then the same as for the standard basis, proving that the corresponding change of basis defines an element of
G_{2}
; by construction it maps
\mathbb{A}
to the standard quaternion algebra
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, hence the claim that four-dimensional nondegenerate subalgebras are parametrized by a unique orbit of
G_{2}
.

To prove
(1)
, there remains to compute the stabilizer of
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
Consider the restriction map

r\colon\Stab_{G_{2}}(\mathbb{A})\to\Aut(\mathbb{A})\simeq\SL_{2}.

This is a surjection whose kernel is isomorphic to the unit vectors in
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
(see, e.g., the multiplication by
e_{4}
described above). As these are parametrized by
\SL_{2}
, we get
\Stab_{G_{2}}(\mathbb{A})\simeq\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
.

For the cases
(2)
and
(3)
, we first show that, given a null-plane
N\subset V_{7}
, any three-dimensional subspace
W
of
N^{\perp}
containing
N
is the imaginary part of a degenerate subalgebra
\mathbb{A}
of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
By Lemma
10
, it suffices to check that
W^{\perp}\subset V_{7}
is isotropic with respect to
\omega
.
Since
N
is a null-plane, this is equivalent to stating that, taking an arbitrary
3
dimensional subspace
U
of
N^{\perp}
disjoint from
N
,
we have
x\mathbin{\lrcorner}(y\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)|_{N}\equiv 0
.
However, due to the transitivity of the action on the
G_{2}
Grassmannian, this can be verified directly on a specific null-plane. Take, for instance,

N=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{\alpha},x_{-\beta}\right\},\hskip 14.22636ptU=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{0},x_{\gamma},x_{-\gamma}\right\}.

One checks that the linear morphism
\Gr(2,U)\to\mathbb{P}(V_{7})^{*},\ \mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}\mapsto x\mathbin{\lrcorner}(y\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)
maps
\Gr(2,U)
isomorphically to
\mathbb{P}(q(U))\simeq\mathbb{P}(U)^{*}
and that the latter is contained in
\mathbb{P}(\ann(N))
.
The family of 3-dimensional subspaces of
N^{\perp}
containing
N
identifies with
\mathbb{P}(U)\simeq\mathbb{P}(N^{\perp}/N)
, and a point
u\in\mathbb{P}(U)
defines an algebra
\mathbb{A}
such that
r=2
if and only if it lies outside the smooth conic
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\cap\mathbb{P}(U)
, hence the claim since
N
is uniquely defined by
\mathbb{A}
.
Finally, the family of degenerate subalgebras such that
r=1
identifies with the family of planes in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
defined by

\mathbb{Q}^{5}\rightarrow\OGr(3,7),\qquad[x]\mapsto\mathbb{P}(\ker(x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)),

which, by construction, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
itself.

Over the real numbers, quaternion subalgebras of
the octonions are parametrized by the compact homogeneous space
G_{2}^{c}/SO_{3}(\mathbb{R})\times SO_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
Note that

\Aut(\mathbb{H})=SO_{3}(\mathbb{R})
, so there must exist automorphisms of
\mathbb{O}
that act trivially on
\mathbb{H}
(Exercise
3.6.5
).

3.3
The double Cayley Grassmannian

A “doubled” version of the Cayley Grassmannian is provided by a special compactification of the group
G_{2}
: the
double Cayley Grassmannian

\DGr
, see
[
58
]
. This variety can be defined as parametrizing eight-dimensional subalgebras of the complex bioctonion algebra
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
(hence its name). We will describe the geometric construction of
\DGr
and briefly hint at its connection with octonions.

The first observation is the following. Consider the group
\Spin_{14}
, which is the universal (degree two) cover of
\SO_{14}
. The projectivization of the spinor representation
\mathbb{P}\Delta^{+}_{14}
is prehomogeneous for the action of
\Spin_{14}
, meaning that it contains an open orbit (see Example
6.2
). The stabilizer of an element
\delta
of the open orbit has thus dimension equal to
\dim(\Spin_{14})-\dim(\mathbb{P}\Delta^{+}_{14})=91-63=28=2\dim(G_{2})
. The last equality is not just a coincidence, as shown by the following result:

Proposition 13
.

[
71
,
2
]

\Stab_{\Spin_{14}}(\delta)
is isomorphic with
(G_{2}\times G_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Before sketching the proof, a few words about spinor representations. First recall that, given a complex vector space
W
of even dimension
2n
(the odd-dimensional case is similar) endowed with a nondegenerate quadratic form
q\in S^{2}W^{*}
, one can construct two
half-spin representations

\Delta^{\pm}(W)=\Delta^{\pm}_{2n}
(but a unique one in the odd-dimensional case,
\Delta(W)
).
For this one chooses two transverse maximal isotropic subspaces
E,F\subset W
: so
W=E\oplus F

and
q
restricts to a perfect duality between
E
and
F
. As vector spaces,
\Delta^{+}(W)
and
\Delta^{-}(W)
are isomorphic to the even and odd exterior powers

\Delta^{+}(W)\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{+}F=(\textstyle\bigwedge^{0}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2\lfloor n/2\rfloor})F,\quad\Delta^{-}(W)\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{-}F=(\textstyle\bigwedge^{1}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor+1})F.

(In the odd-dimensional case, one considers the whole exterior algebra
\Delta(W)=\textstyle\bigwedge^{\bullet}F
.) Then
\Delta^{\pm}(W)
is endowed with an action of
\mathfrak{so}(W,q)\simeq\wedge^{2}W
by first defining an action of
W
and its so-called
Clifford algebra
on
\textstyle\bigwedge^{\bullet}F
(see for instance
[
13
,
33
]
). For
\delta\in\Delta^{\pm}(W)
and
w=e+f\in E\oplus F\simeq W
, the action is simply defined by the formula
w\otimes\delta\mapsto e\mathbin{\lrcorner}\delta+f\wedge\delta
, where
e\mathbin{\lrcorner}
is the contraction induced by the identification of
E
with
F^{*}
.
One can check that when
n
is odd,
\Delta^{+}_{2n}
and
\Delta^{-}_{2n}
are dual one to the other, while they are both self-dual when
n
is even. In the odd-dimensional case,
\Delta_{2n+1}
is always self-dual.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

The stabilizer in
\Spin(V_{7})
of a general element of
\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{7})
is isomorphic to
G_{2}
.

Proof 3.3.2
.

We use the theory of
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings, see Appendix
6.2
, that we apply to

\mathfrak{f}_{4}
as in Example
6.2
by choosing
h
dual to the
4
th simple root. Then
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\simeq\mathfrak{so}(V_{7})\oplus\mathbb{C}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\simeq\Delta_{7}
. Moreover, the group
G_{0}
is the semidirect product
\Spin(V_{7})\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{*}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts by homotheties. Since there are only finitely many
G_{0}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
(Theorem
6.2.2
), a general element
\chi\in\Delta_{7}
has a dense
G_{0}
-orbit, and
\Stab_{\Spin(V_{7})}(\chi)
has dimension equal to
\dim(\Spin(V_{7}))+\dim(\mathbb{C}^{*})-\dim(\Delta_{7})=21-8+1=14
. We already know that
G_{2}\subset\Spin(V_{7})
, and that it is connected (and irreducible) of dimension
14
; if we can show that
\Stab_{\Spin(V_{7})}(\chi)\subset G_{2}
, we are done.

Let us consider the Clifford action
V_{7}\otimes\Delta_{7}\to\Delta_{7}
. The element
\chi
defines a three-form
w_{\chi}
on
V_{7}
by the formula
w_{\chi}(x\wedge y\wedge z)=\langle x\cdot(y\cdot(z\cdot\chi)),\chi\rangle
, where we have used both the Clifford action and self-duality of
\Delta_{7}
. One can show that, if
\chi
is in the dense orbit (which in this case is just the complement of a hypersurface, namely the cone over
\OGr(3,7)
), then
w_{\chi}
is a generic form in
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}
(Exercise
3.6.6
). By Theorem
2.5.1
, we deduce that
\Stab_{\Spin(V_{7})}(\chi)\subset\Stab_{\GL(V_{7})}(w_{\chi})\simeq G_{2}
, and the proof is complete.

Remark 3.3.3
.

We have seen that a null-plane is isotropic with respect to the symmetric quadratic form. This means that we have a codimension two embedding
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})\subset\OGr(2,V_{7})
(see also Exercise
3.6.3
). By the classification of homogeneous vector bundles on homogeneous spaces (refer to
[
64
]
), there exists a rank two vector bundle
\mathcal{S}
on
\OGr(2,V_{7})
, called the spinor bundle, whose space of global sections is
\Delta_{7}
. For a general section
\chi\in\Delta_{7}\simeq H^{0}(\OGr(2,V_{7}),\mathcal{S})
, the zero locus of
\chi
is a smooth five dimensional subvariety of
\OGr(2,V_{7})
which, by the previous lemma, is stabilized by
G_{2}
: it is in fact exactly
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
.

Proof 3.3.4

(Sketch of proof of Proposition
13
)
.

Let
V_{14}
be the natural representation of
\Spin_{14}
. One proves that given
\delta
generic in
\Delta_{14}^{+}
, there exists a uniquely defined orthogonal decomposition
V_{14}=V_{7}\oplus V^{\prime}_{7}
with the following properties. Since by restriction
V_{7}
and
V^{\prime}_{7}
are endowed with nondegenerate quadratic forms, one can define their spin representations
\Delta_{7}
and
\Delta^{\prime}_{7}
, as well as an isomorphism
\Delta_{14}\simeq\Delta_{7}\otimes\Delta^{\prime}_{7}
. Then the astonishing fact is that there exist
\chi\in\Delta_{7}
and
\chi^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}_{7}
such that
\delta\simeq\chi\otimes\chi^{\prime}
. As a consequence, the stabilizer of
\delta
in
\Spin_{14}
has to fix the pair
(V_{7},V^{\prime}_{7})
(hence the factor
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in the stabilizer), and the index two subgroup that fixes
V_{7}
and
V^{\prime}_{7}
can be deduced from the stabilizers of
\chi
in
\Spin(V_{7})
and
\chi^{\prime}
) in
\Spin(V_{7}^{\prime})
), hence the two copies of
G_{2}
by Lemma
3.3.1
.

Having obtained this, the motto we will follow is: “Any variety constructed from the only datum of
\delta
(a generic element in
\Delta^{+}_{14}
) will inherit an action of its stabilizer

(G_{2}\times G_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
”. In order to construct such a variety, we
start with the spinor variety
\mathbb{S}_{14}
, which is one of the two isomorphic connected components of the orthogonal Grassmannian
\OGr(7,V_{14})
:

\mathbb{S}_{14}\subset\OGr(7,V_{14}):=\left\{[U_{7}]\in\Gr(7,V_{14})\mid q|_{U_{7}}=0\right\}=\mathbb{S}_{14}\cup\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{14}\subset\Gr(7,V_{14}).

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the restriction to
\mathbb{S}_{14}
of the tautological vector bundle on
\Gr(7,V_{14})
.
It is a standard fact that
\det(\mathcal{U})=\mathcal{L}^{-2}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the positive generator of the Picard group of
\mathbb{S}_{14}
. In particular
\mathcal{L}
is ample, in fact very ample, and defines an embedding
\mathbb{S}_{14}\subset\mathbb{P}(\Delta^{+}_{14})
. The key property of this embedding is that it
sends
x=[U_{7}]\in\mathbb{S}_{14}
to
[\delta_{x}]
, where
\delta_{x}
is a
pure spinor
from which we can recover
U_{7}\subset V_{14}
as the kernel of the Clifford mutiplication
V_{14}\otimes\delta_{x}\subset V_{14}\otimes\Delta_{14}^{+}\longrightarrow\Delta_{14}^{-}
.

Lemma 3.3.5
.

H^{0}(\mathbb{S}_{14},\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{L})\simeq\Delta^{+}_{14}.

Proof 3.3.6
.

This is a special case of the Borel-Weil Theorem
[
73
]
.
Let us be more explicit about how
\delta\in\Delta^{+}_{14}
defines
a section
s_{\delta}
of
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}
. For any point
x:=[U_{7}]\in\mathbb{S}_{14}
,
s_{\delta}(x)
will belong to the fiber
(\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{L})|_{x}
, hence will define an element of
U_{7}
, at least up to scalar. In order to construct this element, consider the linear form on
V_{14}
sending
v
to

\langle v.\delta_{x},\delta\rangle
. Since
v.\delta_{x}=0
for
v\in U_{7}
, the kernel of this linear form is (in general) a hyperplane
H
containing
V_{7}
, whose orthogonal
h=H^{\perp}
is contained in
U_{7}^{\perp}=U_{7}
; this is the subspace we were looking for.

Remark 3.3.7
.

Both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}
are referred to as spinor bundles since their spaces of sections are spin representations.

Now, being equivariant and irreducible
\mathcal{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}
is globally generated. By Bertini’s theorem, since the rank is
7
, for
\delta\in\Delta^{+}_{14}
generic, the zero locus
Y_{\delta}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{S}_{14}\mid s_{\delta}(x)=0\right\}
is a smooth codimension
7
subvariety of
\mathbb{S}_{14}
. Since the latter has dimension
21
,
Y_{\delta}
is a
14
-dimensional variety acted on by
(G_{2}\times G_{2})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
; can you guess which one?

Proposition 14
.

[
58
]

Y_{\delta}
is a compactification of
G_{2}
and identifies with
\DGr
.

More precisely,
Y
admits an action of
G_{2}\times G_{2}
that compactifies the left-right action! As for the Cayley Grassmannian,
there are only three orbits in the double Cayley Grassmannian : the open one, an open subset of a special hyperplane section, and the closed orbit isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}^{5}\times\mathbb{Q}^{5}
. The blowup of the closed orbit is the so-called wonderful compactification of
G_{2}
, see
[
19
]
.

Proof 3.3.8

(Sketch of proof)
.

As mentioned,
\DGr
parametrizes
8
-dimensional subalgebras of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
; the latter is isomorphic (as an algebra) to
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
(Exercise
3.6.7
). A general subalgebra in
\DGr
will be the graph of an element
g\in\GL(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
; this element must satisfy
g(1)=1
and, for its graph to be a subalgebra, one needs that
g
belongs to
G_{2}
. Thus, the graph of
g
defines a
7
-dimensional subspace
L_{g}
of
V_{14}:=\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})\oplus Im(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, which is isotropic with respect to the difference of the octonionic norms on the two copies of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. The map
g\mapsto L_{g}
defines an embedding
G_{2}\subset\DGr\to\mathbb{S}_{14}
. One can show that its image is contained in
Y_{\delta}
(when
\delta
is chosen so as to identify the associated
V_{7}
and
V_{7}^{\prime}
with the two copies of
\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
). Since being a subalgebra is a closed condition and
G_{2}
is open both inside
\DGr
and
Y
, we deduce that
\DGr\simeq Y
.

3.4
The Lie Grassmannian

The canonical three-form
\omega
yields a map from
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}
to
V_{7}
, similar to
a Lie bracket. This is just the skew-symmetrized octonionic multiplication, that is

\omega(x,y,\cdot\,)=\frac{1}{2}\langle xy-yx,\cdot\,\rangle=Im(xy).

Remark 3.4.1
.

This map is not quite a Lie bracket: the Jacobi identity does not hold. Indeed, the map
\phi:\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}\longrightarrow V_{7}
can be read on the Fano plane, in the form
[e_{i},e_{j}]=e_{ij}
. One directly checks that the Jacobi identity gives zero
for
i,j,k
aligned, and otherwise gives
3e_{(ij)k}
.

Although we do not get a Lie algebra structure on
V_{7}
, it may happen that some subspace
\mathfrak{s}\subset V_{7}
is stable under the fake Lie bracket, and that the Jacobi identity holds on
\mathfrak{s}
. We improperly call such subspaces
Lie subalgebras
of
V_{7}
.

Definition 3.4.2
.

We denote by
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
the subvariety of
\Gr(2,V_{7})
parametrizing two-dimensional Lie subalgebras of
V_{7}
.

Proposition 15
.

The subvariety
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
of
\Gr(2,V_{7})
is smooth
of dimension seven. It can be described as the locus where the section of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)

defined by
\star\omega
drops rank. It is acted on by
G_{2}
with only two orbits, the closed
one being
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
.

Remark 3.4.3
.

Recall from Definition
2.3.8
the coassociative 4-form
\star\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{4}V_{7}^{*}
. It defines a section of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
as follows. Consider
P\in\Gr(2,V_{7})
so that
P
is generated by two vectors
x_{1},x_{2}
; then
\star\omega(P)=(x_{1}\wedge x_{2})\lrcorner\star\omega\mod\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{1},x_{2}\right\}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}(V_{7}/P)^{*}
.

Remark 3.4.4
.

Let
s
be a section of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
. Since
\mathcal{Q}
has rank five, the fiber over a point of
\Gr(2,V_{7})
of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
is isomorphic to
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{5}
. Thus, for
P\in\Gr(2,V_{7})
,
s(P)
can be seen as a
5\times 5
skew-symmetric matrix. The locus where the section
s
drops rank is the locus of points
P
such that
s(P)
is a matrix of rank two (recall that skew-symmetric matrices always have even rank).

Proof 3.4.5
.

A plane
P=\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y\right\}
is a Lie subalgebra if and only if
xy
(or
yx
)
belongs to
\mathrm{span}\left\{1,x,y\right\}
, say
xy=ax+by+c
. We can rewrite this a
(x-b1)(y-a1)=ab+c
.
If
ab+c=0
,
X=x-b1
and
Y=y+a1
and inverse to the other, up to constant and conjugation; so they must be proportional, hence also their imaginary parts
x
and
y
, a contradiction!
So
ab+c=0
and
X\bar{Y}=0
. In particular
X
and
Y
are zero divisors, hence isotropic, and orthogonal since
Re(X\bar{Y})=0
, so that
P^{\prime}=\mathrm{span}\left\{X,Y\right\}
is isotropic. If
P^{\prime}\subset V_{7}
, hence
P^{\prime}=P
, we conclude that
P
is a null-plane.

Otherwise, we may suppose that
Y
is imaginary, but not
X
. Up to the action of
G_{2}
, we can let

X=1+ie_{1}
. Then
Y=v+iw
has to verify the only condition that
w=e_{1}v
, with
v
orthogonal to
e_{1}
for the latter to be imaginary. Since we know that
G_{2}^{\mathrm{c}}
acts transitively on pairs of orthogonal real octonions, up to the action of
G_{2}
we can suppose that
Y=y=e_{2}+ie_{3}
.

This means that
G_{2}
acts transitively on the complement of
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
in
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
, which is the orbit if the plane
P_{0}=\mathrm{span}\left\{e_{1},e_{2}+ie_{3}\right\}
. Note that the restriction of
q
to
P_{0}
has rank one, with kernel generated by
y
. By the previous discussion, Lie subalgebras of
V_{7}
containing
y
are the imaginary parts of planes
P^{\prime}
such that
y\subset P^{\prime}\subset R_{y}
; in particular they are parametrized by a projective plane. We can deduce that
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
is irreducible of dimension
5+2=7
. On the other hand, the locus
D
where the section of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{Q}^{*}(1)
defined by
\star\omega
drops rank has codimension
3
, hence also dimension
7
,
and it is preserved by the action of
G_{2}
. So we just need to check that it contains

P_{0}
, which is a straightforward computation; then
D
contains
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
, which must be one irreducible component. A little extra argument shows that in fact
D
is also irreducible, and we are done.

Remark 3.4.6
.

In the previous proof, we were looking at points
P\in\Gr(2,V_{7})
such that
\star\omega(P)
is a
5\times 5
skew-symmetric matrix of rank
2
, and it was stated that this is a codimension
3
locus. This is an application of a Bertini-type Theorem for degeneracy loci
[
7
, Proposition 2.3]
together with the observation that the locus of
5\times 5
skew-symmetric matrices of rank at most
2
has codimension three inside the space of skew-symmetric matrices
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{5}
; indeed it can be identified with the cone over
\Gr(2,5)
.

Although
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
seems to be a mysterious object, it is in fact familiar:

Proposition 16
.

\LieGr(2,V_{7})\simeq\OGr(2,V_{7})
.

Proof 3.4.7

(Sketch of proofs)
.

This follows from the classification of
2
-orbit varieties given in
[
18
]
.

Alternatively, one can show that the Picard number of
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
is one, which allows to apply
[
65
, Theorem 0.1]
. We deduce that
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
has a bigger automorphism group than
G_{2}
, and that this automorphism group acts transitively. Finally, one checks that the only seven-dimensional homogeneous variety containing
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,V_{7})
is
\OGr(2,V_{7})
.

A more explicit proof was given in
[
8
]
, in relation with the decomposition

\wedge^{2}V_{7}=\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus V_{7}.

This decomposition induces an equivariant involution
\iota
of
\wedge^{2}V_{7}
, which does not preserve
G(2,V_{7})
but exchanges
\LieGr(2,V_{7})\simeq\OGr(2,V_{7})
.

3.5
The horospherical
G_{2}
-Grassmannian

Another interesting variety that we can define from
G_{2}
is the following: let
N=\mathrm{span}\left\{e,f\right\}
be a null-plane, and let
\HG
denote the closure of the
G_{2}
-orbit of the point
\mathrm{span}\left\{e+e\wedge f\right\}
inside
\mathbb{P}(V_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2})

[
65
]
.

Proposition 17
.

\HG
is a smooth Fano variety of dimension
7
and Picard number one, acted on by
G_{2}
with
three orbits, but only two orbits of its automorphism group.

Proof 3.5.1
.

Consider the variety
Y
parametrizing the pairs of planes
N\subset V_{7}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}},P\subset\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}

such that
N
is a null-plane and
\operatorname{Im}P\subset N
. This is the subvariety of
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)\times\Gr(2,8)

defined by the tautological section of
Q\boxtimes U^{*}
, a bundle of rank
10
on a variety of
dimension
5+12
; we deduce by Bertini’s Theorem that
Y
is smooth of dimension
7
.

On the other hand, by forgetting
N
, we get a map to

\pi:Y\longrightarrow\Gr(2,\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})=\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}(\mathbb{C}\oplus V_{7}))=\mathbb{P}(V_{7}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}V_{7}),

sending the plane
P=\mathrm{span}\left\{1-f,e\right\}
to
\mathrm{span}\left\{e+e\wedge f\right\}
. So
\pi(Y)=\HG
.
Moreover, from
P
we can recover
N
by projection to the imaginary octonions,
except when
1\in P
. In the latter case,
P=\mathrm{span}\left\{1,x\right\}

for some isotropic
x
in
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, and we know there is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of null-planes containing
x
. This means that
\pi:Y\longrightarrow\HG
is birational and contracts a divisor
E
to
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
,
with fibers isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In such a situation,
X
must be smooth, and
\pi
must be the blow-up of
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
.

Remark 3.5.2
.

This birational projection is very close to the description we gave of the open orbit of

\LieGr(2,V_{7})
in the proof of Proposition
15
. In fact one can show that

\OGr(2,V_{7})\simeq\LieGr(2,V_{7})
deforms to
\HG
. As we already mentionned, it is a very remarkable and unexpected fact that
\OGr(2,V_{7})
is the only rational homogeneous variety with Picard number one which is not globally rigid
[
66
,
49
,
42
]
.

Remark 3.5.3
.

Note that
\HG-\mathbb{Q}^{5}\simeq Y-E
is an
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-fibration over
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
.
Over the open
G_{2}
-orbit, this restricts to a
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}
-fibration over
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)
. In fact,

\HG
is an instance of a horospherical variety, which is roughly a relative toric variety
over a flag manifold. This is precisely this structure that explains the fact that the automorphism group is bigger than
G_{2}
, and is not semisimple.

Remark 3.5.4
.

In this lecture we have encountered several varieties on which
G_{2}
acts with an open orbit, isomorphic with
G_{2}/H
for some subgroup
H
. There is a whole theory of equivariant compactifications of such quotients
G/H
for
H
a closed subgroup of a reductive group
G
. This includes and generalizes the famous theory of toric varieties, and behaves particularly nicely when
H
is a so-called

spherical subgroup
(see e.g.
[
67
]
.

3.6
Exercises

Exercise 3.6.1
.

\noway

Show that
\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))\setminus\mathbb{Q}^{5}\simeq G_{2}/\SL_{3}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Exercise 3.6.2
.

Show that
\ker(L_{x}|_{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}})=\ker(x\mathbin{\lrcorner}\omega)
for every
[x]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
.

Exercise 3.6.3
.

\noway

Show that not every line in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
is a
G_{2}
-line. More precisely, show that the lines passing through a point
[x]\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}
are parametrized by a three-dimensional variety in which the set of
G_{2}
-lines through
[x]
has codimension two.

Exercise 3.6.4
.

✍
Similarly to Diagram
3.1
and Proposition
9
, discuss the incidence diagrams for the other complex Lie groups of rank two.

Exercise 3.6.5
.

Describe the automorphisms of
\mathbb{O}
that act trivially on
\mathbb{H}
.

Exercise 3.6.6
.

Let
\chi
be a spinor in the dense orbit inside the spinor representation
\Delta_{7}
; show that the associated three-form
\omega_{\chi}
defined by
\omega_{\chi}(x\wedge y\wedge z)=\langle x\cdot(y\cdot(z\cdot\chi)),\chi\rangle
belongs to the open orbit in
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{7}
(
Hint
: search in the literature for representative of orbits in
\Delta_{7}
and use such a representative to do the explicit computation).

Exercise 3.6.7
.

Show that
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
as algebras (
Hint
: show and use the fact that
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C}
).

Exercise 3.6.8
.

Construct a diagram

\textstyle{{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\LieGr(2,V_{7})}
\textstyle{\CGr,}

where the fibers of
p
are
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s, while the generic fiber of
q
are conics.

Exercise 3.6.9
.

✍
Show that
\Aut(\HG)
is bigger than
G_{2}
.

Solution

(Hint Ex.
3.6.1
)
.

Compute the stabilizer
\Stab_{G_{2}}([x])
of a point
[x]\in\mathbb{P}(V_{7})\setminus\mathbb{Q}^{5}
. Note that if
\phi\in\Stab_{G_{2}}(x)
, then
\phi
must also preserve
V_{x}:=x^{\perp}\subset V_{7}
and
\omega|_{V_{x}}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}V_{x}^{*}
, the restriction of the invariant three-form.
\omega|_{V_{x}}
must be in the orbit of
\omega_{1}
from Proposition
1
, hence there is a pair of distinguished 3-dimensional subspaces
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
preserved by
\phi
such that
V_{x}=\Lambda_{1}\oplus\Lambda_{2}
. When each of the two spaces is preserved, use the quadratic form
q|_{V_{x}}
to relate the actions of
\phi
acts on

\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
.

Solution

(Ex.
3.6.3
)
.

The Grassmannian
\Gr(2,V_{7})
has dimension
10
; the subvariety
\OGr(2,V_{7})
has dimension
7
since
the condition that a quadratic form vanishes on a line is verified in codimension three. Therefore the incidence variety parametrizing pairs
(x,\ell)\in\mathbb{Q}^{5}\times OG(2,V_{7})
with
x\in\ell
has
dimension
8
and by homogeneity, all the fibers of its projection to
\mathbb{Q}^{5}
have the same dimension
3
. This means that there is a three-dimensional family of lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{5}

passing through a given point
x
. The same analysis with
\OGr(2,V_{7})
replaced by
G_{2}\mathrm{Gr}(2,7)

shows that there is only a one-dimensional family of special lines through
x
.

Solution

(Ex.
3.6.8
)
.

We have seen that
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
coincides with the locus of points
P\in\Gr(2,V_{7})
where
\star\omega(P)\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}(V_{7}/P)^{*}
has rank two. This means that, if
P
belongs to
\LieGr(2,V_{7})
, the kernel of
\star\omega(P)
is a three-dimensional subspace of
V_{7}/P
, i.e., a five-dimensional subspace
U
of
V_{7}
containing
P
. On the other hand,
\CGr
is the subvariety of
\Gr(3,V_{7})
parametrizing subspaces
A
such that
\omega|_{A^{\perp}}=0
, or equivalently
\star\omega(A,A,A,\cdot\,)=0
(meaning that, whenever we contract
\star\omega
with three vectors in
A
, we get zero).
This suggests to construct
I
inside the flag variety
Fl(2,3,5,V_{7})
as the locus of flags
P\subset A\subset U
with
\dim(P)=2
,
\dim(A)=3
,
\dim(U)=5
satisfying the condition that
\star\omega(P,P,U,\cdot\,)=0
(meaning that contracting
\star\omega
with two vectors in
P
and one in
U
always yields zero). Notice that this condition automatically implies that
\star\omega(A,A,A,\cdot\,)=0
, so
A\in\CGr
. We can thus define
{p}(P,A,U)=P
and
{{q}}(P,A,U)=A
.

\textstyle{{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\LieGr(2,V_{7})}
\textstyle{\CGr.}

The fibers of
p
are
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s, while the generic fiber of
q
are conics. Indeed, recall the interpretation of elements
A\in\CGr
as subalgebras in
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. The fiber of
q
over
A
parametrizes Lie subalgebras contained in
A
. If
A
is generic, then
A=Im\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
and the fiber is a conic, a point in the fiber being uniquely determined by the kernel of the restriction of
q
.

Solution

(Ex.
3.6.9
)
.

The observation is that
V_{7}
act on
X
through

v.(e+f_{1}\wedge f_{2})=e+q(v,f_{1})f_{2}-q(v,f_{2})f_{1}+f_{1}\wedge f_{2}
. If such an action of
v\in V_{7}
were defined by an element
g\in G_{2}
, then
g\cdot(e+f_{1}\wedge f_{2})=g\cdot e+g\cdot(f_{1}\wedge f_{2})=(e+q(v,f_{1})f_{2}-q(v,f_{2})f_{1})+f_{1}\wedge f_{2}
. This would mean that
g\cdot f_{1}\wedge f_{2}=f_{1}\wedge f_{2}
, i.e., that
g
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
as the identity; this is not possible because
v\in V_{7}
does not act as the identity on the first factor. We deduce that
\Aut(\HG)
is not contained in
G_{2}
.

Chapter 4
The Tits-Freudenthal magic square

In this lecture, we will present two constructions of the exceptional Lie algebras that use Jordan algebras and triality. Both constructions will depend on the choice of a pair of complexified normed algebras and will lead to the so-called Magic Square of Lie algebras. We will also begin to study the geometric incarnations of these constructions.

4.1
The Jordan algebra
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})

Let
\mathbb{A}
be one of the complexified normed algebras
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. We let
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
be the space of Hermitian matrices with coefficients in
\mathbb{A}
:

H_{3}(\mathbb{A})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x&u&v\\
\bar{u}&y&w\\
\bar{v}&\bar{w}&z\end{pmatrix},\quad u,v,w\in\mathbb{A},x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.

Definition 4.1.1
.

A Jordan algebra is a non-associative commutative algebra over a field that satisfies the Jordan identity

(x\circ y)\circ(x\circ x)=x\circ(y\circ(x\circ x))
(where
\circ
is the multiplication).

The easiest way to obtain a Jordan algebra structure is to start from an associative algebra and modify the product as
x\circ y=(xy+yx)/2
. Therefore, if
\mathbb{A}\neq\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
there is a
Jordan algebra
structure on
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
defined by the symmetric
product
A\circ B=(AB+BA)/2
.
This Jordan algebra has rank three in the sense that for any
A\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A})

there is a cubic relation

A^{3}-\tr(A)A^{2}+\sigma_{2}(A)A-Det(A)Id=0,

where
\tr(A)
is the usual trace and
\sigma_{2}(A)=\frac{1}{2}(\tr(A)^{2}-\tr(A^{2}))
. No effort is needed to check that
H_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})
identifies with symmetric
3\times 3
complex matrices. The verification that
H_{3}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})
can be identified with the space of
3\times 3
complex matrices is more involved, and even more so is that
H_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
is isomorphic to the space of skew-symmetric
6\times 6
matrices (
\noway
☞
Exercise
4.3.1
). However, the real challenge is to understand that for
A=\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, one still gets a Jordan algebra, usually called the
exceptional simple Jordan algebra
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

When
\mathbb{A}
is not
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, one can also define a Jordan algebra structure on
H_{n}(\mathbb{A})
for any
n>3
in the same way. The exception is that one can define a Jordan algebra structure on
H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
when
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is not even associative, but not on
H_{n}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
for
n>3
.

Rank structure in
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})

Among Hermitian operators, one has Hermitian projections. Those of rank-one satisfy
A^{2}=A
and
\tr(A)=1
. Projectivizing, we get the variety

X_{a}^{2}:=\left\{[A]\in\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A}),\quad A^{2}=\tr(A)A\right\}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A}).

Here
a
is the complex dimension of
\mathbb{A}
, while the meaning of the superscript
2
will be clarified later on.
Now, Hermitian projections of rank one are in bijection with lines (even though over
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, "lines" do not clearly make sense!). This allows to identify (at least formally for
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
) the varieties
X_{a}^{2}
with projective planes over normed algebras:

X_{a}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A}).

A dense open subset of
X_{a}^{2}
is the projectivization of the image of the map

(x,y)\in\mathbb{A}^{2}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&x&y\\
\overline{x}&x\overline{x}&y\overline{x}\\
\overline{y}&x\overline{y}&y\overline{y}\end{pmatrix}\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A}).

The cone over
X_{a}^{2}
parametrizes rank-one matrices inside
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. Summing two rank-one matrices yields a rank (at most) two matrix, characterized by the vanishing of the determinant

Det_{a}(M)=\frac{1}{3}tr(M^{3})-\frac{1}{2}tr(M)tr(M^{2})+\frac{1}{6}tr(M)^{3}.

This formula makes sense even over
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
! Moreover, writing explicitly the determinant in coordinates, one checks that the derivatives of this cubic polynomial
vanish exactly on
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Let
C_{a}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
be the cubic hypersurface defined by
Det_{a}
. We get a stratification by the rank as follows:

X_{a}^{2}=\Sing C_{a}\subset C_{a}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A}).

One also defines
SL(\mathbb{A})
as the subgroup of
GL(H_{3}(\mathbb{A}))
that preserves the determinant
Det_{a}
. It preserves the hypersurface
C_{a}
, hence also its singular locus
X_{a}^{2}
, and one can show that modding out by the center, we get the full automorphism group of
X_{a}^{2}
.

Let us discuss what these varieties look like when
a=1,2,4
.

{ex}

[
a=1
:
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
]
This is clearly the complex projective plane
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. What is interesting in this case is the embedding into the space of Hermitian matrices. We have seen that
H_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})
is the space of symmetric matrices, and indeed the embedding
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})
is the second Veronese embedding
v_{2}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{5}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the singular locus of
C_{1}
, which is defined by the usual determinant of symmetric matrices.

Let us denote by
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
the variety parametrizing free rank-one
\mathbb{A}
-modules inside
\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

{ex}

[
a=2
:
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
]
Recall that
H_{3}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})
is the space of complex
3\times 3
matrices. In the projectivization of this space, rank-one matrices are parametrized by
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, embedded in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{8}
via the usual Segre embedding. Let us check that we can identify
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
(and more generally,
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
with
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
).
Let
I
be the complex structure on the real normed algebra
\mathbb{C}
, and
i
the complex structure coming from the complexification. Left multiplication
L_{I}
by
I
satisfies
L_{I}^{2}=-\id
, so we have two eigenspaces
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{i}
and
(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{-i}
for
i
and
-i
of the same complex dimension
n
, and the algebra multiplication does not mix the two factors in the decomposition

(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}=(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{i}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{-i}
. The first factor is the image of
L_{1-iI}
, while the second one is the image of
L_{1+iI}
. Given a free
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}
-module
[E]\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, one constructs two one dimensional subspaces
E_{i}:=E\cap(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{i}
and
E_{-i}:=X\cap(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{-i}
. Vice versa, given any two
E_{i}
and
E_{-i}
, one can reconstruct
E=E_{i}+E_{-i}
. This defines the isomorphism
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

{ex}

[
a=4
:
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
] Recall that
H_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
can be identified with
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{6}
. We will now show that
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}\simeq\Gr(2,6)\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
(and
C_{4}
is the cubic hypersurface defined by the vanishing of the Pfaffian), more generally

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq\Gr(2,2n)\subset H_{n}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
. The idea that since
\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
is associative,
a Hermitian rank-one matrix inside
H_{n}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
is of the form
v_{2}(a)=(\bar{a}_{i}a_{j})

for some
a\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
(note that
v_{2}(az)=|z|^{2}v_{2}(a)
if
z\in\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
). The image
L_{a}
of
(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}
by right multiplication by
v_{2}(a)
is
E(a):=\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}a\simeq\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}
and has complex dimension four. In particular, it is stable by the left multiplication
L_{I}
by
I
, whose eigenspaces
E_{i}(a)

and
E_{-i}(a)
are both two-dimensional, since they are exchanged by right
multiplication by
J
. Notice in particular that
E_{-i}(a)
is thus uniquely defined by the formula
E_{-i}(a)=JE_{i}(a)
once
E_{i}(a)
has been chosen. There is a splitting
(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}=(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{i}\oplus(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})^{n}_{-i}
,
and the maps
[a]\mapsto E(a):=\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}a\mapsto E_{i}(a)
define the isomorphisms

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq\Gr(2,2n)
.
All of this is compatible with what happens for
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}
, and we get a diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
\textstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Gr(2,2n)}
\textstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}}
\textstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n-1}.}

{ex}

[
a=0
]
Let us include in the discussion the degenerate case
a=0
, obtained by letting
\mathbb{A}=0
: we just get the space
H_{3}(0)
of diagonal
3\times 3
matrices with entries in
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\mathbb{C}
. Then
\mathbb{P}H_{3}(0)
identifies with
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
X_{0}^{2}
with the three points corresponding to diagonal matrices with only one non-zero entry.

Playing around with Jordan algebras

One can think of the cubic
C_{a}
as the projective dual hypersurface to
X_{a}^{2}
, parametrizing tangent hyperplanes to
X_{a}^{2}
as points inside the
dual projective space. Conversely the derivatives of the determinant yield a birational map

\textstyle{I_{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Bl_{\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{Bl_{\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial Det_{a}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})^{*}.}

(4.1)

Remark 4.1.3
.

The derivatives of a polynomial
f
over a vector space
V
define a map
\partial f\colon V\to V^{*}
. Indeed,
\partial f
takes a point
p\in V
and a vector
x\in V
(seen as a vector in the tangent space of
V
at
p
) and gives a number
\partial_{x}f(p)
. So we get
0\in V^{*}
when all the derivatives of
f
vanish at
p
. In the projective version, this means
that the indeterminacy locus
of
\partial f\colon\mathbb{P}(V)\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}(V^{*})
is the singular locus of the hypersurface
(f=0).

{ex}

[
a=0
, continued]
The determinant on
H_{3}(0)
is just
Det_{0}=xyz
, so that
\partial Det_{0}:\mathbb{P}^{2}\dasharrow\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
is nothing else than the usual Cremona transformation
[x,y,z]\mapsto[yz,zx,xy]
. In diagram (
4.1
), the triangle having the three indeterminacy points as vertices is blown-up to a hexagon, whose edges coming from the triangle are contracted in
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

Being generalizations of the Cremona transformation, it is no surprise that the other
\partial Det_{a}
have very special properties:

1.

Polynomials whose derivatives define birational maps are called
homaloïdal polynomials
and are very uncommon
[
24
]
. Here our homaloïdal polynomial is a cubic, and by symmetry the inverse map is of the same type; so our birational transformation is quadro-quadric, and its indeterminacy locus
X_{a}^{2}
is smooth and connected; it was proved in
[
27
]
that there is no other birational transformation with these properties.

2.

A fiber over
M\in\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{*}
is a copy of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{a+1}
, that meets
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
along a quadric
Q_{M}\simeq\mathbb{Q}^{a}
. These quadrics behave like lines (they are called
\mathbb{A}
-lines) in a plane projective geometry:

•

two (general)
\mathbb{A}
-lines meet at a single point,

•

two (general) points are joined by a unique
\mathbb{A}
-line.

3.

The four varieties
X_{a}^{2}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
are the four
Severi varieties
: the only smooth subvarieties

X^{2m}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3m+2}

whose secant varieties are not the full space. According to Zak
[
82
]
, this is impossible in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
for
n<3m+2
.

Remark 4.1.4
.

In (2), the words "general" have to be added because we are working over the complex numbers, which makes some pathologies possible that would not exist over the real numbers (like isotropic octonions or null-planes). As a matter of fact, over the real numbers, "general" can be suppressed and one get a genuine plane projective geometry (with some subtle pathologies over the octonions)
[
31
]
.

Traceless Hermitian matrices

There is a natural hyperplane of
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
given by traceless Hermitian matrices
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}
. From
X_{a}^{2}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
we can thus define the subvarieties
X_{a}^{1}:=X_{a}^{2}\cap\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}
by taking hyperplane sections (again, the reason for the superscript
1
will be explained later).
Its automorphism group is
\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{A}))
, that
identifies with
SO_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. Indeed,
Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{A}))
must fix the identity of the Jordan algebra
e\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, hence also the quadratic form
Det_{a}(e,x,y)
, as well as the orthogonal
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}
to
e
, and as a consequence also
X_{a}^{1}
. It turns out that
SO_{3}(\mathbb{A})
and
SL(\mathbb{A})
are semisimple Lie groups whose Dynkin diagrams can be read on the two following rows:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}{}{}\hbox to16.62pt{\vbox to6.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-1.18024pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{{
