In terms of Lie algebras, the second row is deduced from the first one by

folding
the Dynkin diagram. Alternatively, the Lie algebras are related by
a decomposition

\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{A})=\mathfrak{so}_{3}(\mathbb{A})\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}.

{ex}

[
a=1
]
If
a=1
then
X_{a}^{2}
is
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
embedded in
\mathbb{P}^{5}
by the second Veronese embedding
v_{2}
. So a general hyperplane section is a conic
v_{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})
. The Lie algebra of its automorphism group is
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\simeq\mathfrak{so}_{3}=\mathfrak{so}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})
while
Lie(\Aut(X_{a}^{2}))=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})
. The decomposition
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{so}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}})_{0}
is the decomposition of (traceless) matrices into skew-symmetric and symmetric parts.

{ex}

[
a=2
]
If
a=2
then
X_{a}^{2}
is
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, so a hyperplane section is the flag variety
\Fl(1,2,3)
. The Lie algebra of the automorphism group of
\Fl(1,2,3)
is
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
while
Lie(\Aut(X_{a}^{2}))=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
and we get a (non-diagonal!) decomposition
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{3}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
, where
H_{3}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})_{0}
is identified with
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
, i.e., a hyperplane section of
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
.

{ex}

[
a=4
]
If
a=4
then
X_{a}^{2}
is
\Gr(2,6)\subset\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{6}
. A hyperplane section
\IGr(2,6)\subset\Gr(2,6)
is defined by a general element
e\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{6})^{*}
, i.e., a symplectic form. The variety
\IGr(2,6)
parametrizes those planes which are isotropic with respect to this symplectic form: it is a homogeneous variety under the action of
\Sp_{6}
. The Lie algebra of its automorphism group is
\mathfrak{sp}_{6}
while
Lie(\Aut(X_{a}^{2}))=\mathfrak{sl}_{6}
and we get a decomposition
\mathfrak{sl}_{6}=\mathfrak{sp}_{6}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{6}
, i.e., again a decomposition of matrices in symmetric and skew-symmetric ones, but with inverted roles.

Remark 4.1.5
.

One notices, maybe surprisingly, that in all the examples above,
\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{A}))
acts transitively on
X_{a}^{1}
. This is also the case for
a=8
, as we shall see.

Remark 4.1.6
.

From what we have said one can deduce that
\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{A}))
can be seen as the stabilizer inside
\Aut(X_{a}^{2})
of a general element
e\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. This implies that the complement of
X_{a}^{2}
in
\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
is essentially the symmetric space
SL_{3}(\mathbb{A})/SO_{3}(\mathbb{A})
.

The exceptional Jordan algebra

We summarize in this short section what happens when
a=8
, i.e., when
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
. The first important result is the following:

Theorem 4.1.7
.

[
14
]

F_{4}=\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))
.

Note that
H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
has dimension
27
while, from the theory of simple Lie algebras, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
of
F_{4}
has a unique minimal irreducible representation
V_{\omega_{4}}
(with highest weight
\omega_{4}
) of dimension
26
, all the non-trivial representations having higher dimension. This is in agreement with what we have seen so far! Indeed,
\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))
must fix the identity in
H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
, and preserve the space
H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})_{0}
of traceless matrices, which identifies with the minimal irreducible representation of
F_{4}
. Moreover
X_{8}^{1}
identifies with the minimal orbit
F_{4}/P_{4}
inside
\mathbb{P}V_{\omega_{4}}
: a
15
-dimensional complex variety, that we will also denote by
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{0}
.

Now, we know that
X_{8}^{1}
is a hyperplane section of
X_{8}^{2}=\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and that
F_{4}=\Aut(H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}))
is the stabilizer inside
\Aut(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2})
of a general point
e\in H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
. What is this mysterious group
\Aut(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2})
? Well, it is a “new", or rather exceptional, group, which turns out to be simple: the group
E_{6}

[
14
]
. Then one can show that
H_{3}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})
identifies with the irreducible
27
-dimensional
E_{6}
-representation
V_{\omega_{1}}
whose highest weight is the fundamental weight
\omega_{1}
(again, this is the non-trivial representation of
E_{6}
of minimal dimension); this yields the interpretation of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
as the minimal orbit
E_{6}/P_{1}
inside
\mathbb{P}(V_{\omega_{1}})
(see Lemma
3.1.5
and discussion that follows it). This variety is known as the (complex)
Cayley plane
.

Remark 4.1.8
.

The Weyl group of
E_{6}

is (almost) the automorphism group of the configuration of
27
lines on a smooth cubic surface, and this is no coincidence: J. Lurie
[
56
]
showed how to reconstruct the full
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
, with its minimal representation, from the combinatorics of the
27
lines. In fact, let us identify the
27
lines with coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{27}
on
\mathbb{C}^{27}
. Let
\Delta
be the set of triangles in the cubic surface and define
P(x)=\sum_{(ijk)\in\Delta}x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}
. Then in suitable coordinates,
P
is nothing else than
Det_{8}
!

Remark 4.1.9
.

The action of
F_{4}
on
\mathbb{O}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is transitive over the
reals numbers (and the real Cayley plane can be identified with
F_{4}/\Spin_{9}
) but not over the
complex numbers since it stabilizes
\mathbb{O}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{0}
. In fact the actions of
F_{4}
on the latter and its complement are both transitive.

The magic square from Jordan algebras

Jordan algebras were studied a lot in the 1940-50’s in connection with quantum mechanics
and its use of Hermitian operators. At this time, Chevalley’s theorem
4.1.7
urged the need to construct
the other exceptional groups and algebras in terms of specific algebraic structures. This was accomplished in the 1950-60’s by Tits, Freudenthal, Vinberg.

Consider a pair
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
of (complexified, if you wish) normed algebras and let

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}):=\mathfrak{der}\mathbb{A}\times\mathfrak{der}H_{3}(\mathbb{B})\oplus({\mathrm{I}m}\mathbb{A}\otimes H_{3}(\mathbb{B})_{0}),

where
\mathfrak{der}\mathbb{A}
and
\mathfrak{der}H_{3}(\mathbb{B})
denote the spaces of derivations of these two algebras, i.e., linear operators satisfying the Leibniz rule.
One can define a Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
, so that

\mathfrak{der}\mathbb{A}\times\mathfrak{der}H_{3}(\mathbb{B})
is a Lie subalgebra acting on

{\rm Im}\mathbb{A}\otimes H_{3}(\mathbb{B})_{0}
in a natural way.
The result of this construction (over the complex numbers) is
the
Tits-Freudenthal magic square of Lie algebras
:

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}

\mathfrak{sl}_{3}

\mathfrak{sp}_{6}

\mathfrak{f}_{4}

\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathfrak{sl}_{3}

\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{3}

\mathfrak{sl}_{6}

\mathfrak{e}_{6}

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathfrak{sp}_{6}

\mathfrak{sl}_{6}

\mathfrak{so}_{12}

\mathfrak{e}_{7}

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathfrak{f}_{4}

\mathfrak{e}_{6}

\mathfrak{e}_{7}

\mathfrak{e}_{8}

This provides a uniform description of the exceptional Lie algebras (except
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
!) over the complex numbers! Over the reals, one can use different forms of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
(the split octonions, typically) to obtain distinct real Lie algebras with the same complexification. The usual normed algebras always lead to compact forms.

Remark 4.1.10
.

Look carefully at the second line… can you see it? What about the first line?

4.2
Triality

It is amazing that
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
has a natural Lie
algebra structure, but also the symmetry of the square appears to be miraculous, since the definition of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is very non-symmetric.
A more symmetric variant of the Tits-Freudenthal construction was found by Vinberg
in 1966, and rediscovered by several people (Allison, Dadok-Harvey, Barton-Sudbery,
Landsberg-M.) using triality.
Triality groups generalize automorphism groups of normed algebras. They are defined as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Tri(\mathbb{A})&=&\left\{(U_{1},U_{2},U_{3})\in SO(\mathbb{A})^{3},\;U_{1}(xy)=U_{2}(x)U_{3}(y)\;\;\forall x,y\in\mathbb{A}\right\}.\end{array}

The three projections
\pi_{i}:\Tri(\mathbb{A})\longrightarrow SO(\mathbb{A})
give three representations of the triality group, that we denote
\mathbb{A}_{1},\mathbb{A}_{2},\mathbb{A}_{3}
. The triality Lie algebras

\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\simeq\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{A})^{3},\;u_{1}(xy)=u_{2}(x)y+xu_{3}(y)\;\;\forall x,y\in\mathbb{A}\right\}

for
a=1,2,4
are
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})=0,\;\mathfrak{ab}_{2},\;\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{3}
, where
\mathfrak{ab}_{2}
is abelian and two-dimensional.

Theorem 4.2.1

(Cartan 1925)
.

The group
\Tri(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\Spin_{8}
. Each projection

\pi_{i}
is a twofold cover of
SO_{8}
. The three corresponding
8
-dimensional
representations of
\;\Spin_{8}
are inequivalent.

Proof 4.2.2
.

See e.g.
[
35
, Theorem 14.19]
.

That we have (at least) double covers is clear since we can fix for example
U_{1}
and change the
signs of
U_{2}
and
U_{3}
.
If you don’t know Spin groups, the Lie algebra version is also nice:

\mathfrak{so}_{8}\simeq\left\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})^{3},\;u_{1}(xy)=u_{2}(x)y+xu_{3}(y)\;\;\forall x,y\in\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.

Triality is related to the fact that the Dynkin diagram
D_{4}
is the only one with a
threefold symmetry. The three exterior nodes correspond to the three
8
-dimensional representations

V_{8}
,
\Delta_{+}
,
\Delta_{-}
, and the symmetry means that these representations can be exchanged
by some outer automorphisms (that we can realize by permutations of
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}
, and some
conjugations.) In particular, these three representations are in fact indistinguishable.
(For higher spin groups only the two half-spin representations are indistinguishable.)

Remark 4.2.3
.

Sometimes one starts with the
construction of spin representations, proves the triality Theorem, and then deduces
that
G_{2}
is the generic stabilizer for the transitive action of
\Spin_{7}
on
the seven dimensional sphere in
\Delta_{+}
.

The magic square from triality

Let
(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
be normed algebras, with the three actions of their triality
Lie algebras
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})
and
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{B})
. There is a natural Lie algebra structure on

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})=\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{B})\oplus(\mathbb{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{1})\oplus(\mathbb{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{2})\oplus(\mathbb{A}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{3}).

Of course
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{B})
is a Lie subalgebra,
acting naturally on
\mathbb{A}_{i}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{i}

[
51
]
. The bracket of

a_{1}\otimes b_{1}\in\mathbb{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{1}
with
a_{2}\otimes b_{2}\in\mathbb{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{2}

is simply

a_{1}a_{2}\otimes b_{1}b_{2}
, considered as an element of
\mathbb{A}_{3}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{3}
. Now, by anti-linearity of the Lie bracket, this forces
[a_{2}\otimes b_{2},a_{1}\otimes b_{1}]=-a_{1}a_{2}\otimes b_{1}b_{2}
. For the other maps, one should be careful with signs:
[a_{1}\otimes b_{1},a_{3}\otimes b_{3}]=-\overline{a}_{1}a_{3}\otimes\overline{b}_{1}b_{3}\in\mathbb{A}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{2}
,
[a_{2}\otimes b_{2},a_{3}\otimes b_{3}]=a_{3}\overline{a}_{2}\otimes b_{3}\overline{b}_{2}\in\mathbb{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{B}_{1}
. Finally, if
c_{i}\in\mathbb{A}_{i}
and
d_{i}\in\mathbb{B}_{i}
, then
[a_{i}\otimes b_{i},c_{i}\otimes d_{i}]
must belong to
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{B})
; to such an element we can associate
q(b_{i},d_{i})a_{i}\wedge c_{i}+q(a_{i},c_{i})b_{i}\wedge d_{i}\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{A}_{i}\oplus\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{B}_{i}
, where
q
is the symmetric form. Then one obtains an element of
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{B})
by applying the duals of the inclusion maps
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{A}_{i})\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{A}_{i}
. Note that
by definition,
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
is graded over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, starting from

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})\oplus\mathbb{A}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{A}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{A}_{3}.

Remark 4.2.4
.

Let
u,v\in\mathbb{A}_{1}
and denote by
\psi(u\wedge v)
the corresponding element in
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{A})
. Then the action on
\mathbb{A}_{1}
is given by the natural action of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{A}_{1}\simeq\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{A}_{1})
:
\psi(u\wedge v)_{1}x=4q(u,x)v-4q(v,x)u
. The action on
\mathbb{A}_{2}
and
\mathbb{A}_{3}
is instead given essentially by multiplication:
\psi(u\wedge v)_{2}=\overline{u}(vx)-\overline{v}(ux)
,
\psi(u\wedge v)_{3}=(xu)\overline{v}-(xv)\overline{u}
.

4.3
Exercises

Exercise 4.3.1
.

\noway

☞

Show that
H_{3}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}})
is isomorphic to the space of
3\times 3
complex matrices, and that
H_{3}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
is isomorphic to the space of skew-symmetric
6\times 6
matrices.

Exercise 4.3.2
.

✍

Check that the first instance (
a=1
) of Diagram (
4.1
) is well-known:

\textstyle{I_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{5}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{5}}

Exercise 4.3.3
.

For
a=1,2,4
, observe that the quadrics that define "lines" in
X_{a}^{2}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}

can be seen as entry loci: any
P\in Sec(X_{a}^{2})-X_{a}^{2}

belongs to infinity many secant lines, and the intersection points of these lines with
X_{a}^{2}
are parametrized by an
a
-dimensional
quadric
Q_{P}
.

Exercise 4.3.4
.

✍

Compute
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
and determine the three triality representations.

Exercise 4.3.5
.

✍

Construct
\mathfrak{so}_{8}
from four copies of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and decompose the triality representations.

Exercise 4.3.6
.

Check the geometric version of triality: any maximal isotropic subspace of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
is of the form
Im(R_{p})
or
Im(L_{p})
for some zero divisor
p\in\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
.

Exercise 4.3.7
.

Check the Jacobi identity on
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R})
.

Exercise 4.3.8
.

✍

Use this model to describe the root system of
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
.

Solution

(Exercise
4.3.2
)
.

I_{1}
is the blow-up of the Veronese surface in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{5}
and
\partial Det_{1}
is given by the complete linear system of quadrics vanishing on this surface. The study of this blow-up was instrumental in Schubert’s proof that there exist exactly
3264
conics tangent to
5
given general conics (see for instance
[
28
]
).

Solution

(Exercise
4.3.4
)
.

For
(0,u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})
, the triality equations become
u_{2}(x)y+xu_{3}(y)=0
, so
u_{3}
is determined by
u_{2}
(just let
x=1
). Thus elements of the form
(0,u_{2},u_{3})
are contained in a copy of
\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\mathfrak{so}_{4}\simeq\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\times\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. One checks that the triality equations define three supplementary equations on
\mathfrak{so}_{4}
so that
\{(0,u_{2},u_{3})\in\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})\}
form a (diagonal) subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. Of course, putting to zero
u_{2}
or
u_{3}
instead of
u_{1}
one gets two other copies of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. These algebras commute since, for instance,
[(0,u_{2},u_{3}),(v_{1},0,v_{3})]=(0,0,w_{3})
for some
w_{3}
, and the triality equations easily imply that
w_{3}=0
. Finally
\mathfrak{tri}(\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{3}
.

Solution

(Exercise
4.3.5
)
.

(Sketch) One can construct the explicit decomposition from very natural morphisms, but one can also do it using graded Lie algebras. In particular, this example appears already in
6.2
. One obtains a decomposition
\mathfrak{so}_{8}=(\mathfrak{so}_{8})_{0}\oplus(\mathfrak{so})_{1}=(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\oplus 4}\oplus(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 4}
. The space
(\mathfrak{so})_{1}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 4}
is the tensor product of the four standard
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-representations corresponding to the four copies of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. Each
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
corresponds to one of the four extremities of the affine Dynkin diagram of type
D_{4}
: three corresponding to simple roots at the extremes of the Dynkin diagram of
D_{4}
, and one for the highest root. The three triality representations are of the form
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{i}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}_{j}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}_{k}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}_{l}
for
ijkl
a permutation of
1234
.

Solution

(Exercise
4.3.6
)
.

Recall that the zero divisors in a unital composition algebra are the elements of norm zero. Let
p\in\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
be us such a zero divisor. Then any element of the form
px
is again a zero divisor, so the image
Im(L_{p})
of left multiplication by
p
is isotropic, in particular its dimension is at most
4
. The same holds for the kernel of
L_{p}
, which is again made of zero divisors and thus isotropic. So both are
4
-dimensional. We get a map from the quadric
\mathbb{Q}^{6}\subset\mathbb{P}V_{8}
to the orthogonal Grassmannian
\OGr(4,V_{8})\subset\mathbb{P}\Delta_{+}
given by
p\mapsto Im(L_{p})
(and similarly for right multiplication with
\Delta_{-}
and
Im(R_{p})
). This is an isomorphism since
\mathbb{Q}^{6}
and
\OGr(4,V_{8})
are both six-dimensional quadrics.

Solution

(Exercise
4.3.8
)
.

(Sketch) The Lie algebra of
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
is obtained as

\mathfrak{e}_{8}\simeq\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{so}_{8}\times\mathfrak{so}_{8}\oplus(V_{8}^{(1)}\otimes V_{8}^{(2)})\oplus(\Delta_{+}^{(1)}\otimes\Delta_{+}^{(2)})\oplus(\Delta_{-}^{(1)}\otimes\Delta_{-}^{(2)}).

We deduce that two copies of the root system of type
D_{4}
are contained in the root system of type
E_{8}
. The remaining roots come from the three remaining factors. For instance, let us denote by
\delta_{1}^{(i)}\dots\delta_{8}^{(i)}
the weights of
V_{8}^{(i)}
for
i=1,2
. Then
\delta_{j}^{(1)}+\delta_{k}^{(2)}
are roots of
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
. All the roots of
E_{8}
are obtained in this way (
48
roots from the two copies of
\mathfrak{so}_{8}
and
3\times 64=192
from the three triality factors).

Chapter 5
The magic square and open problems

In the previous section, we have constructed the Magic Square from Jordan algebras and triality, together with some "natural" varieties
X_{a}^{1}
and
X_{a}^{2}
. No we discuss the full geometric version of the magic square, making the varieties
X_{a}^{1}
and
X_{a}^{2}
appear in a quite natural fashion.

5.1
Geometric version of the magic square

This was developed mainly by Freudenthal in the 1950-60’s, based on "points"
in certain geometries
[
31
,
30
]
, that are parametrized (over the complex numbers) by the following homogeneous spaces:

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{C}}

v_{4}(\mathbb{P}^{1})

Fl_{3}

\IGr(2,6)

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{0}

\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{C}}

v_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}

\Gr(2,6)

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}

\mathbb{H}^{\mathbb{C}}

\IGr(3,6)

\Gr(3,6)

\OGr(6,12)^{+}

E_{7}^{hs}

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}

F_{4}^{ad}

E_{6}^{ad}

E_{7}^{ad}

E_{8}^{ad}

The varieties in the first row are the varieties
X_{a}^{1}
, while the varieties
X_{a}^{2}
are the varieties from the second row (mystery unveiled on the apices
1
and
2
). The formers are hyperplane sections of the latters. Moreover, each variety is homogeneous for the action of a Lie group whose Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})
. We can even complete it to a magic triangle
[
22
]
(the first line gives the parameter
a
):

-\frac{2}{3}

0

1

2

4

8

v_{4}(\mathbb{P}^{1})

Fl_{3}

\IGr(2,6)

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{0}

3pts

v_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2})

\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}

\Gr(2,6)

\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{2}

v_{3}(\mathbb{P}^{1})

(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{3}

\IGr(3,6)

\Gr(3,6)

\OGr(6,12)^{+}

E_{7}^{hs}

G_{2}^{ad}

SO_{8}^{ad}

F_{4}^{ad}

E_{6}^{ad}

E_{7}^{ad}

E_{8}^{ad}

We will denote by
X_{a}^{i}
the variety corresponding to the
i
th row. Note that each space
X_{a}^{i}=G/P
from row
i
and column
a
comes with a homogeneous embedding inside
\mathbb{P}V_{a}^{i}
for some irreducible
G
-representation
V_{a}^{i}
, whose dimension is given below:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\dim X_{a}^{1}=2a-1,&&\dim V_{a}^{1}=3a+2,\\
\dim X_{a}^{2}=2a,&&\dim V_{a}^{2}=3a+3,\\
\dim X_{a}^{3}=3a+3,&&\dim V_{a}^{3}=6a+8,\\
\dim X_{a}^{4}=6a+9,&&\dim V_{a}^{4}=\dim\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{O})=2\frac{(3a+7)(5a+8)}{a+4}.\end{array}

The last formula is the most remarkable. To be able to express a dimension by a rational function is completely unexpected! This formula was essentially discovered by Vogel
[
80
]
from the construction of knot invariants,
then expanded by Deligne and led to his conjectures on
the exceptional series
of Lie algebras
[
20
,
21
]
.

We already know that
V_{a}^{2}=H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
,
V_{a}^{1}=H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}
,
X_{a}^{2}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
X_{a}^{1}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}_{0}=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}\cap\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}
. There remains to understand the last two rows. For later use, let us explain some notation we are using.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
G
be a simple complex Lie group, acting on its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
via the adjoint representation. The image inside
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{g}
of the minimal non-trivial nilpotent orbit is called the
adjoint variety

G^{\ad}\subset\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{g}
. Equivalently, it is the unique closed orbit inside
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{g}
.

Definition 5.1.2
.

The variety
E_{7}^{hs}
is the
Freudenthal variety
, defined as the minimal
E_{7}
-orbit
E_{7}^{hs}=E_{7}/P_{7}\subset\mathbb{P}V_{\omega_{7}}
inside the projectivization of the representation
V_{\omega_{7}}
of
E_{7}
with highest weight
\omega_{7}
(its minimal representation, of dimension
56
).

5.2
The third row and varieties
X_{a}^{3}

Freudenthal defined for the third row a synthetic geometry modeled on symplectic geometry in five projective dimensions. The geometric elements are
points, isotropic lines and isotropic planes. Points are parametrized by adjoint varieties. Isotropic planes are parametrized by
X_{a}^{3}
of dimension
3a+3
.

{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 55.12 2.98 M 57.09 2.98 C 57.09 4.07 56.21 4.95 55.12 4.95 C 54.03 4.95 53.15 4.07 53.15 2.98 C 53.15 1.89 54.03 1.01 55.12 1.01 C 56.21 1.01 57.09 1.89 57.09 2.98 Z M 55.12 2.98}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Up to isomorphisms, two different planes have only three possible relative positions. They are called
incident
when the corresponding points are joined by a line contained in
X_{a}^{3}
(refer to Section
6.3
for the description of projective linear spaces inside homogeneous varieties in terms of Dynkin diagrams).

The connection between the second and the third row goes in both directions. The space of lines in
X_{a}^{3}
passing
through a given point of
X_{a}^{3}
is a copy of
X_{a}^{2}
(this is a nice application of the theory of Tits’ shadows as presented in Section
6.3
; this is also another instance of a VMRT, see Remark
3.1.21
). This also means that the Lie algebra of the stabilizer of any given point of
X_{a}^{3}
contains a copy of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{C})
, and that the isotropy representation on the tangent space is a copy of
V_{a}^{2}=H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, containing
X_{a}^{2}
.

Conversely, we can reconstruct
X_{a}^{3}
from
X_{a}^{2}
.
Recall the latter is
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
the space of rank-one elements in

\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. This is also the singular locus of the space of elements of rank at most two, which is the cubic
C_{a}
of equation
(Det_{a}=0)
.

Theorem 5.2.1

(Integrating the cubic,
[
50
]
)
.

X_{a}^{3}\subset\mathbb{P}V_{a}^{3}
is the image of the cubic map

M\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A})\mapsto[1,M,\partial Det_{a}(M),Det_{a}(M)]\in\mathbb{P}(V_{a}^{3}),

where
V_{a}^{3}=\mathbb{C}\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})^{*}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{*}
admits a natural symplectic
structure, invariant under
\Aut(X_{a}^{3})
(
\noway
☞
Exercise
5.4.1
).
Moreover
X_{a}^{3}
is Legendrian.

Remark 5.2.2
.

Legendrian means that any affine tangent space is maximal isotropic with respect to the symplectic structure (i.e., Lagrangian).
Mukai coined these varieties twisted cubics over Jordan algebras
[
61
]
.

Remark 5.2.3
.

For
a=-\frac{2}{3}
,
M
is just a scalar and we get a rational cubic in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
:

X^{3}_{-\frac{2}{3}}=v_{3}(\mathbb{P}^{1}),\hskip 56.9055ptX^{3}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This is a geometric incarnation of folding! Moreover the twisted cubic curve has the well-known property that its tangent developpable is a quartic surface in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
.
For
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{P}(M_{2,2,2})
, where
M_{2,2,2}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the space of cubic matrices, we also have an invariant quartic polynomial given by Cayley’s hyperdeterminant

\begin{array}[]{rcl}HDet(A)&=&a_{000}^{2}a_{111}^{2}+a_{001}^{2}a_{110}^{2}+a_{010}^{2}a_{101}^{2}+a_{100}^{2}a_{011}^{2}\\
&&-2a_{000}a_{001}a_{110}a_{111}-2a_{000}a_{010}a_{101}a_{111}-2a_{000}a_{011}a_{100}a_{111}\\
&&-2a_{001}a_{010}a_{101}a_{110}-2a_{001}a_{011}a_{110}a_{100}-2a_{010}a_{011}a_{101}a_{100}\\
&&+4a_{000}a_{011}a_{101}a_{110}+4a_{001}a_{010}a_{100}a_{111}.\end{array}

Hyperdeterminants are equations of dual varieties, but here this is the same as the tangent variety,
because of the Legendrian property.

Remarkably, these properties propagate to the whole row
X_{a}^{3}\subset\mathbb{P}V_{a}^{3}
.
The tangent variety of
X_{a}^{3}
is always a quartic hypersurface, with a singular locus
W_{a}\supset X_{a}^{3}
of dimension
5a+4
.
The equation
HDet_{a}
of the quartic hypersurface admits a uniform expression, independently of
a
.
The string

X_{a}^{3}\subset W_{a}\subset Tan(X_{a}^{3})\subset\mathbb{P}V_{a}^{3}

(5.1)

is the full stratification into orbit closures of
\Aut(X_{a}^{3})
(Exercise
5.4.2
).
Let us conclude this section with one last property that the varieties
X_{a}^{3}
share.

Definition 5.2.4
.

A projective variety
X\subset\mathbb{P}(V)
has the One Apparent Double Point property (OADP) if through any general point
x
in
\mathbb{P}(V)
passes a unique secant line to
X
.

Theorem 5.2.5
.

[
17
, Proposition 8.4]

X_{a}^{3}
has the OADP property.

Remark 5.2.6
.

In the first lecture we have already seen that
\Gr(3,6)\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{6})
has the OADP property: a general trivector in six variables can be written as the sum of two pure trivectors in a unique way.

5.3
The fourth row and varieties
X_{a}^{4}

Freudenthal’s so-called metaplectic geometries describe the fourth row. There are four types of elements in such a geometry, called points, lines, planes and symplecta, and they are parametrized by homogeneous spaces. (Recall that such a homogeneous space is defined by a subset of vertices of the Dynkin diagram - the black dots in the pictures below.)
All the varieties in the fourth row are modeled on the geometry of
F_{4}
and the four
F_{4}
-Grassmannians.

\hline\cr\end{array}

Remark 5.3.1
.

Let
G
be a simple Lie group and
P
a parabolic subgroup. Since
P
is its own normalizer, there is a canonical interpretation of the homogeneous space
G/P
as a conjugation class of parabolic subgroups. Now, there is a natural incidence relation between parabolic subgroups: simply ask that their intersection contains a Borel subgroup. This point of view, that generalizes Freudenthal’s
constructions, was widely
expanded by Tits, who discussed the properties of the incidence geometries one obtains this way
[
76
]
. This ultimately led to the revolutionary notion of Tits building.

By fixing a point and looking at lines-planes-symplecta through this point,
one obtains the symplectic geometries from the third line.
In particular, the space of lines in
X_{a}^{4}
passing
through a given point is isomorphic to
X_{a}^{3}
(refer again to Section
6.3
). This also means that
V_{a}^{3}
can be seen as a subspace of the tangent space at a point of
X_{a}^{4}
. In fact, we will see that
V_{a}^{3}
is a hyperplane in this tangent space. Notice also that each simplex is an adjoint variety for the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{H})
of the third line.

Contact structures

Recall that the adjoint variety
G^{ad}
can be defined as the unique closed
G
-orbit inside
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{g}
,
where
\mathfrak{g}
is a simple complex Lie algebra and
G=\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
.

Example.

A_{n}^{ad}\simeq\Fl(1,n,n+1)
is a variety
of partial flags. At a point
f=(V_{1}\subset V_{n})
of
Fl:=A_{n}^{ad}
, the cotangent space is

\Omega_{Fl,f}=\left\{X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n+1},\quad X(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})\subset V_{n},\quad X(V_{n})\subset V_{1},\quad X(V_{1})=0\right\}.

It contains the line
L^{*}_{f}=\{X\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n+1},\;X(\mathbb{C}^{n+1})\subset V_{1},\;X(V_{n})=0\}.

Dualizing, an letting
f
vary in
Fl
, we get an exact sequence of vector bundles

0\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\rightarrow T_{Fl}\rightarrow L\rightarrow 0,

where
T_{Fl}
is the tangent bundle. The subbundle
\mathcal{H}
defines a
contact distribution
, i.e.,
a distribution
of tangent hyperplanes
which is maximally non integrable, as in the following definition:

Definition 5.3.2
.

A (holomorphic)
contact structure
on
X
is an exact sequence of vector bundles

0\to\mathcal{H}\to T_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\to}}L\to 0,

where
L
is a line bundle and the restriction of
d\theta
to
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{H}
is everywhere nondegenerate.

This means that the induced linear map

\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}T_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d\theta}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{X}\longrightarrow L

defines at every point a non degenerate skew-symmetric form on
\mathcal{H}
.
In particular
X
must have odd dimension
2n+1
, and then
K_{X}=-(n+1)L

[
6
]
.

The contact structure on
A_{n}^{ad}
extends to any adjoint variety
G^{ad}
. It comes from the famous Kostant-Kirillov-Souriau symplectic
form on a coadjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(see e.g.
[
6
]
).
If
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}
has stabilizer
H\subset G
, then

T_{\lambda}\mathcal{O}\simeq\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
and one can let

\omega_{\lambda}(\overline{X},\overline{Y})=\lambda([X,Y])

for
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\overline{X},\overline{Y}
the corresponding tangent vectors.
This is a well-defined two-form, closed and nondegenerate: coadjoint orbits are (open) symplectic manifolds.

When
\mathfrak{g}
is complex semisimple,
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\simeq\mathfrak{g}
has finitely many nilpotent orbits (see Section
6.2
),
which are (open) holomorphic symplectic manifolds. In particular the nilpotent cone is a symplectic
singular variety
\mathcal{N}
, which can be resolved by the Springer resolution
\Tot(\Omega_{G/B})\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
. At the other extreme there is a unique
minimal
nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}_{min}
, and by definition
G^{ad}=\mathbb{P}\mathcal{O}_{min}.
The contact structure on
G^{ad}
is the projective version of the symplectic structure on
\mathcal{O}_{min}.

One can show that any line in
G^{ad}
is a contact line, i.e., its tangent vector is contained in
\mathcal{H}
. This explains the discrepancy between

\dim X_{a}^{8}=6a+9\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\dim V_{a}^{3}=6a+8.

Remark 5.3.3

(Going upwards in the magic square)
.

Through this remark we summarize the connection between the different rows of the magic square. Let us start from the last row and the variety
X_{a}^{4}=G^{ad}
. Since it is a contact manifold, the tangent bundle of
X_{a}^{4}
contains a subbundle
\mathcal{H}
, which is endowed with a symplectic form with values in
L
. At each point of
X_{a}^{4}
, the Lie algebra of the stabilizer group contains a copy of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{H})
, and we can identify its actin on the fiber of
\mathcal{H}
with the symplectic vector space
V_{a}^{3}
. Moreover, the lines contained in
X_{a}^{4}
and passing through the point can be identified with
X_{a}^{3}\subset\mathbb{P}V_{a}^{3}
; this is another example of a nice VMRT
3.1.21
. Because of this close connection with adjoint varieties, the varieties

X_{a}^{3}
are sometimes called
subadjoint
varieties (see e.g.
[
41
]
).

In fact, for each point of
X_{a}^{3}
there is a decomposition of
V_{a}^{3}
as a direct sum
\mathbb{C}\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})^{*}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{*}
, with the tangent space of
X_{a}^{3}
at the point identified with
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
. Once again, looking at lines inside
X_{a}^{3}
passing through the point one obtains the subvariety
X_{a}^{2}\subset\mathbb{P}H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
, which is the space of rank-one Hermitian matrices
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Finally,
X_{a}^{1}
is the hyperplane section of
X_{a}^{2}
defined by intersecting with the hyperplane of traceless matrices
H_{3}(\mathbb{A})_{0}\subset H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

One can add a further column to the Magic Square, with
a=6
. Indeed, recall from Exercise
2.6.8
that the orthogonal to any null-plane in the octonions is a six-dimensional subalgebra
\mathbb{S}
of
\mathbb{O}^{\mathbb{C}}
, called the sextonion algebra. Then we can define

\Tri(\mathbb{S})
and
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{S})
as if
\mathbb{S}
was a genuine normed algebra.
In particular

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{S})
is a (non-semisimple) Lie algebra of dimension
190
, coined
E_{7\frac{1}{2}}

[
53
]
; its existence had already been guessed by string theorists. Remarkably, Freudenthal geometries can also be constructed for
\mathbb{S}
, with the major difference that the parameter spaces are no longer homogeneous, and in fact become singular. See also
[
81
]
.

5.4
Exercises

Exercise 5.4.1
.

\noway

☞

Guess the symplectic structure! And deduce that
X_{a}^{3}
is Legendrian: each
affine tangent space is a Lagrangian subspace of
V_{a}^{3}
.

Exercise 5.4.2
.

✍

Prove that stratification (
5.1
) is the full stratification into orbit closures of
\Aut(X_{a}^{3})
by observing that the tangent bundle is a bundle in Jordan algebras,
in which we know the orbits. For
a=2
describe three desigularisations of the intermediate orbit.

Exercise 5.4.3
.

✍

When
\mathfrak{g}
is classical, describe its adjoint variety with its
contact structure.

Solution

(Exercise
5.4.1
)
.

Let
V
be a complex vector space. There is a natural symplectic structure
\omega
on
V\oplus V^{*}
given by
\omega((v_{1}+w_{1},v_{2}+w_{2})=w_{1}(v_{2})-w_{2}(v_{1})
. By applying this to
V=\mathbb{C}\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
one deduces a symplectic structure on
V_{a}^{3}
for which
\mathbb{C}\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
is isotropic. One checks that this can be arranged so that the symplectic structure is invariant under
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{H})
. Then to ensure that
X_{a}^{3}
is Legendrian, by homogeneity it suffices to check that the Zariski tangent space at one chosen point is isotropic. Of course one chooses this point to be the image of the matrix
0\in H_{3}(\mathbb{A})
via the above cubic map, i.e., the point
[1,0,0,0]
. Since
Deta_{a}
is a cubic map and
\partial Det_{a}
is quadratic, both vanish on the Zariski tangent space of
X_{a}^{3}
at
[1,0,0,0]
, which therefore can be identified with
\mathbb{C}\oplus H_{3}(\mathbb{A})\subset V_{a}^{3}
. But then it is clear that this subspace is isotropic.

Solution

(Exercise
5.4.3
)
.

We have already seen the case of
A_{n}
, so we are left with the symplectic and orthogonal groups.

•

Let
G=Sp_{2n}=Sp(\omega)
where
\omega\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{2n})^{*}
is symplectic, and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}\simeq S^{2}\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. Any vector in
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
is isotropic with respect to
\omega
and
\mathbb{P}^{2n-1}
is a
Sp_{2n}
-homogeneous space which embeds inside
\mathbb{P}S^{2}\mathbb{C}^{2n}
via the second Veronese embedding
v_{2}
; it is the adjoint variety:
Sp_{2n}^{ad}=v_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2n-1})
. The tangent bundle of
\mathbb{P}^{2n-1}
at
x
is
Hom(x,\mathbb{C}^{2n}/x)
and contains the contact hyperplane
Hom(x,x^{\perp}/x)
.
This yields a short exact sequence

0\to Hom(\mathcal{O}(-1),\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\perp}/\mathcal{O}(-1))\to T_{\mathbb{P}^{2n-1}}\to\mathcal{O}(2)\to 0.

The contact bundle
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\perp}/\mathcal{O}(-1)
is called the null-correlation bundle.

•

Let
G=SO_{m}=SO(q)
where
q\in S^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{*}
is symmetric nondegenerate, and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{m}\simeq\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}
. So we need to search for the adjoint variety of
SO_{m}
a the minimal orbit inside
\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Clearly
\Gr(2,m)\subset\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
is
SO_{m}
-invariant, but it is not homogeneous. The adjoint variety is
SO_{m}^{ad}=\OGr(2,m)\subset\Gr(2,m)
, the Grassmannian of isotropic two-dimensional subspaces. It can as well be seen as the zero locus of
q
seen (by restriction) as a section of
S^{2}\mathcal{U}^{*}
over
\Gr(2,m)
, if
\mathcal{U}
denotes the tautological rank two bundle. Since the tangent bundle of
\Gr(2,m)
is
\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{Q}
, for
\mathcal{Q}
the quotient bundle,
the normal exact sequence on
\OGr(2,m)
reads:

0\to T_{\OGr(2,m)}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\to S^{2}\mathcal{U}^{*}\to 0.

Now
q
gives an isomorphism between
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and its dual, and this isomorphism sends an isotropic 2-dimensional subspace
x\subset\mathbb{C}^{m}
into its dual
x^{*}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{m}/x^{\perp}
(where
\perp
is with respect to
q
). So there is an exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\to 0
, which induces an exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{*}\to 0
. The normal map
\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{*}\to S^{2}\mathcal{U}^{*}
restricts to zero on
\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}
and is induced by the natural projection
\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{*}\to S^{2}\mathcal{U}^{*}
, whose kernel is
\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{U}^{*}\simeq\mathcal{O}(1)
. The upshot is the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}\to T_{\OGr(2,m)}\to\mathcal{O}(1)\to 0,

which gives the contact structure on
\OGr(2,m)
, with
L=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{U}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\perp}/\mathcal{U}
.

5.5
Open problems

We list a few open problems related to the lectures.

Problem 1.
Hartshorne’s conjecture: any smooth subvariety of
\mathbb{P}^{N}
of dimension
n
and codimension
c
is a complete intersection if
c<n/2
.

Problem 1bis.
Hartshorne’s conjecture in codimension
2
: equivalently (by Serre’s construction) any rank two vector bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{N}
is decomposable for
N\geq 6
.

Major problem in the 1980’s, but zero progress except Zak’s theorem, and most people gave up
[
54
,
82
]
.

Problem 2.
Classify smooth subvarieties of
\mathbb{P}^{N}
with codegree four. Prove they "essentially" all come from the third row of the Magic Square.
(see
[
32
]
).

Problem 3.
Classify varieties with the OADP property (see
[
16
]
).

Problem 4.
Classify smooth Legendrian varieties.

Buczynski showed how to construct many of them by cutting a given one by a linear space of codimension two. He also
gave conditions that imply homogeneity. See
[
12
]
for more on this topic. The problem of characterizing homogeneous Legendrian
varieties by intrinsic properties was recently addressed in
[
41
]
.

Problem 5.
LeBrun-Salamon conjecture: smooth (complex) contact manifolds with Picard number one of Fano type are adjoint varieties of simple Lie algebras

[
55
]
.

Complex contact manifolds are twistor spaces of manifolds with holonomy
Sp(1)Sp(2n)
in Berger’s classification, also called quaternion-Kähler manifolds.
So this is another holonomy class than
G_{2}
-manifolds! The Fano condition corresponds to asking that the quaternion-Kähler manifold has positive scalar curvature.

It is known that contact manifolds with Picard number
>1
are projectivized cotangent bundles
[
46
]
. This includes adjoint varieties of type
A
.

Problem 6.
Prove that the VMRT of such a contact manifold is a homogeneous Legendrian variety.

That the variety of minimal rational tangents (VMRT) at a generic point is a smooth Legendrian variety was proved by Kebekus
[
45
]
.

Problem 7.
Classify special Cremona transformations.

Special Cremona tranformations are birational maps
\mathbb{P}^{n}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{n}

that become regular after blowing-up a smooth connected subvariety. Those
defined by quadratic polynomials, as well as their inverse (quadro-quadric transformations) were classfied in
[
27
]
.

Problem 8.
Find nice projective embeddings of the homogeneous spaces

E_{6}/PSp_{8},\qquad E_{7}/PGL_{8},\qquad E_{8}/Spin_{16}.

The last one, of dimension
128
, potentially qualifies over the real numbers as a projective plane over
\mathbb{O}\otimes\mathbb{O}
,
whatever that means. Over the complex numbers no nice projective embedding has been described.

Chapter 6
Appendix

6.1
Elements of projective duality

In this appendix we summarize some elements of projective duality used in the text. For further details we refer to
[
34
]
and
[
75
]
. Let
V\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
a vector space and
\mathbb{P}(V)
the projective space of one-dimensional subspaces of
V
. For any subspace
U\subset V
, the projectivization

\mathbb{P}(U)\subset\mathbb{P}(V)
is a linear subspace. We denote by
V^{*}
the dual of
V
and
\check{\mathbb{P}}(V)=\mathbb{P}(V^{*})
;
a point in
\check{\mathbb{P}}(V)
corresponds to a hyperplane in
\mathbb{P}(V)
. For
L=\mathbb{P}(U)\subset\mathbb{P}(V)
linear, its
projective dual

L^{*}=\mathbb{P}(\ann(U))\subset\check{\mathbb{P}}(V)
, is the set of hyperplanes containing
L
. Note that
\dim L^{*}=\dim V-\dim L
and
(L^{*})^{*}=L
. This notion of duality extends to algebraic subvarieties, as we will now explain.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
be a closed subvariety; we denote by
X_{\rm{sm}}
the smooth locus of
X
, which is open and dense in
X
. For a point
x\in X_{{\rm{sm}}}
, the
embedded tangent space
is

\hat{T}_{x}X=\mathbb{P}(T_{v}C(X))\subset\mathbb{P}^{n},

where
C(X)\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
is the affine cone of
X
and
v\in x
is any nonzero vector. A hyperplane
H\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
is
tangent
to
X
if is contains
\hat{T}_{x}X
for some
x\in X_{\rm{sm}}
.

Definition 6.1.1
.

The
projective dual variety of
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the variety
X^{\vee}\subset\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
defined as the Zariski closure of the set of tangent hyperplanes:

X^{\vee}:=\overline{\left\{\,H\in\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\mid\exists\,x\in X_{{\rm{sm}}}\mbox{ s.t. }\hat{T}_{x}X\subset H\,\right\}}.

Consider the incidence variety
\mathcal{N}_{X}^{0}=\{\,(x,H)\in\mathbb{P}^{n}\times\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\mid x\in X_{\rm{sm}}\text{ and }H\supset\hat{T}_{x}X\,\}
. It sits inside the projectivized cotangent bundle
\mathbb{P}(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{n})\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}\times\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
since the latter is the locus of pairs
(x,H)
such that
x\in H
. Moreover, the projection
\pi_{1}\colon\mathcal{N}_{X}^{0}\to X_{\rm{sm}}
shows that
\mathcal{N}_{X}^{0}
is the projectivized conormal bundle
\mathbb{P}(N^{*}_{X_{\rm{sm}}/\mathbb{P}^{n}})
. The closure of the image of the second projection
\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{N}_{X}^{0}\to\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is precisely
X^{\vee}
. The Zariski closure
\mathcal{N}_{X}
of
\mathcal{N}_{X}^{0}
is called the
conormal variety
of
X
. It has many useful properties that shed light onto the geometry of the dual variety. One important result is the following.

Theorem 6.1.2

(Biduality Theorem)
.

For any projective variety
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
, the bidual

(X^{\vee})^{\vee}=X.

Moreover, if
z
is a smooth point of
X
and
H
is a smooth point of
X^{\vee}
, then
H
is tangent to
X
at
z
if and only if
z
, regarded as a hyperplane in
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
, is tangent to
X^{\vee}
at
H
.

Proof 6.1.3
.

For a proof, see
[
34
, I, Theorem 1.1]
.

Theorem 6.1.4
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
be an irreducible projective variety. Then

1.

If
X
is smooth then
\mathcal{N}_{X}
is smooth.

2.

If
X^{\vee}
is a hypersurface then
\pi_{2}
is birational.

3.

If
X
is smooth and
X^{\vee}
is a hypersurface, then
\pi_{2}
is a resolution of singularities.

Proof 6.1.5
.

See
[
75
, Theorem 1.15]
.

Typically,
X^{\vee}\subset\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is a hypersurface,
defined by a polynomial
\Delta_{X}
called
the
discriminant
of
X
.

To compute the codimension and the degree of
X^{\vee}
, at least when
X
is smooth, one may follow Cayley’s method, that consists in realizing
\mathcal{N}_{X}
as the degeneracy locus of a section of a twisted jet bundle, see
[
34
, Chapter 2]
. In particular, this leads to the following powerful result of Katz and Kleimann, later generalized by Holme.

Theorem 6.1.6
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
be an irreducible smooth projective variety. Consider the polynomial

c_{X}(q)=\sum_{i=0}^{\dim X}q^{i+1}\int_{X}c_{\dim X-i}(\Omega^{i}_{X})\cdot c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(1))^{i}.

Then
\codim X^{\vee}=\mu
is the maximum power of
q-1
that divides
c_{X}(q)-c_{X}(1)
, and
\deg X^{\vee}=c_{X}^{(\mu)}(1)/\mu!

Proof 6.1.7
.

See
[
34
, II, Theorem 3.4]
.

{ex}

Let
X=\Gr(3,7)\subset\mathbb{P}^{34}
the Grassmannian of
3
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
in its Plücker embedding. One may compute that

\begin{split}c_{X}(q)=462\,q^{13}-3\,234\,q^{12}+10\,836\,q^{11}-22\,932\,q^{10}+34\,062\,q^{9}-37\,338\,q^{8}\\
+30\,952\,q^{7}-19\,600\,q^{6}+9\,492\,q^{5}-3\,500\,q^{4}+980\,q^{3}-210\,q^{2}+35\,q,\end{split}

hence
c_{X}^{\prime}(1)=7
. Therefore,
X^{\vee}
is a degree seven hypersurface.

In the context of actions of algebraic groups, the biduality theorem has also some strong consequences. In
[
69
]
, Pyasetskiĭ has observed that if a connected algebraic group acts linearly on a vector space with finitely many orbits, then the dual representation has the same property; moreover the numbers of orbits are the same.

Theorem 6.1.8
.

Suppose that a connected algebraic group
G
acts linearly on a vector space
V
with finitely many orbits. Then the dual action on
V^{*}
has the same number of orbits. Let

V=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{O}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad V^{*}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{\prime}

be the orbit decompositions. Then the bijection is defined as follows:
\mathcal{O}_{i}
corresponds to
\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{O}_{i}})
is projectively dual to
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\prime}})
.

Proof 6.1.9
.

The argument is to first show that the orbits in
V
are affine cones, and so are the orbits in
V^{*}
; here it is used that there are finitely many orbits. Then the Biduality Theorem is applied to the projectivizations of the orbit closures. For details, see
[
75
, §2.2]
.

{ex}

Consider the action of
\GL_{6}
on
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{6}
. As shown in
§§
2.1
, this action has four orbits
beside the origin
\mathcal{O}_{0}=\{0\}
:
\mathcal{O}_{1}
consisting of decomposable tensors (the affine cone of
\Gr(3,6)
); then the set
\mathcal{O}_{2}
of rank-five forms; finally
\mathcal{O}_{3}
and
\mathcal{O}_{4}
consisting of rank-six forms,
\mathcal{O}_{4}
being the dense orbit and
\overline{\mathcal{O}_{3}}
the affine cone over the tangential variety to
\Gr(3,6)
. Identifying
\mathbb{C}^{6}
with its dual, Projective Duality sends
\mathcal{O}_{0}
to
\mathcal{O}_{4}
,
\mathcal{O}_{1}
to
\mathcal{O}_{3}
, while the intermediate orbit closure
\mathcal{O}_{2}
is self-dual.

6.2
Gradings of Lie algebras

Gradings of Lie algebras have appeared in an explicit (gradings of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{so}_{8}
) or more intrinsic way (prehomogeneous spaces) during these lectures. Let us give a very concise overview of the subject, hopefully of practical use. We refer to
[
78
,
47
,
57
]
for more details. We will restrict to a simple situation, even though everything can be done more generally.

\mathbb{Z}
-Gradings

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the complex Lie algebra of an algebraic group
G
.

Definition 6.2.1
.

A
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
\mathfrak{g}
is a decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{i}
such that
[\mathfrak{g}_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{j}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{i+j}
. In particular
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is a subalgebra and
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
is a
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
-representation for all
i
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple, fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
and denote by
\Delta\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
the set of roots of
\mathfrak{g}
. The elements in
\mathfrak{h}
are commuting semisimple elements and thus their action on
\mathfrak{g}
can be diagonalized; this yields the so-called Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, where the
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
’s are one-dimensional and, if
h\in\mathfrak{h}
and
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
,
[h,x]=\alpha(h)x
. Recall that one can choose a preferred basis of roots
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\Delta
called
simple
roots; their relative position (from which
\mathfrak{g}
can be recovered) is encoded in the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
, whose vertices correspond indeed to simple roots.

{ex}

[Main example]

Define a grading on
\mathfrak{g}
as follows: choose
h\in\mathfrak{h}
such that
\alpha(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
, and let

\mathfrak{g}_{i}:=\left\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid[h,x]=ix\right\}.

By the Jacobi-identity, this is a grading of
\mathfrak{g}
; in fact all
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings are obtained in this way.

Let
G_{0}\subset G
be the connected subgroup corresponding to
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. Since each
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
is stabilized by
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, this group acts on each
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
. One of the interests of
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings is the following:

Theorem 6.2.2

(
[
78
]
)
.

The action of
G_{0}
on
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
has only finitely many orbits.

In order to give a proof of this theorem, we recall some basic facts about nilpotent elements in semisimple Lie algebras.

The nilpotent cone

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple Lie algebra and
G=Aut(\mathfrak{g})
the corresponding adjoint Lie group. An element
x\in\mathfrak{g}
is
nilpotent
(respectively
semisimple
) if the linear operator
\ad(x):=[x,\cdot\,]\in End(\mathfrak{g})
is nilpotent (resp. diagonalizable). Orbits of semisimple elements are completely described by Chevalley’s restriction Theorem (Theorem
6.2.3
): any semisimple element is contained in a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
(a maximal subalgebra consisting of commuting semisimple elements) and all Cartan subalgebras are conjugate under the action of
G
; moreover, two elements in
\mathfrak{h}
are conjugate under
G
if and only if they are conjugate under the Weyl group, which is finite. In particular, semisimple orbits form a continuous family of dimension equal to the rank of
G
, defined as the dimension of any Cartan subalgebra. Finally, semisimple orbits are those that are closed in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Nilpotent orbits have somehow “opposite” properties to semisimple ones. For instance, they are never closed, since the closure of each nilpotent orbit contains
0
, and in fact these orbits can be caracterized by this property. More precisely, if
x
is nilpotent then
\lambda x
is in the same orbit for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
: nilpotent orbits are pointed cones. This follows from the Jacobson-Morozov Theorem, which states that any nilpotent element
x\in\mathfrak{g}
can be extended into a
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
(x,y,h)
such that
\mathrm{span}\left\{x,y,h\right\}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
with its canonical basis (with
h
the semisimple element). In particular
[h,x]=2x
. Then the one dimensional torus
\exp(\mathbb{C}h)\simeq\mathbb{C}^{*}\subset G
(where
h
is seen as a linear operator on
\mathfrak{g}
via the adjoint action, and
\exp
is the exponential of linear operators) acts on
\mathbb{C}x
with weight
2
, i.e.,
\exp(\lambda h)\cdot x=2^{\lambda}x
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
.

This implies for instance that the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}
, the union of all nilpotent elements in
\mathfrak{g}
, can be described as

\mathcal{N}=\left\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid f(x)=0\mbox{ for any }f\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}\right\}\subset\mathfrak{g}.
Combining this with the Chevalley’s restriction Theorem, one can get a complete picture of orbits in semisimple Lie algebras, generalizing Jordan’s reduction of matrices. Recall that the Weyl group
W
is a finite reflection group actin on
\mathfrak{h}
.

Theorem 6.2.3

(Chevalley’s restriction theorem)
.

Restriction to any Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
induces an isomorphism between
G
-invariant polynomials on
\mathfrak{g}
and
W
-invariant polynomials on
\mathfrak{h}
:

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]^{W}.

The orbit picture is as follows. The GIT quotient
\mathfrak{g}/\hskip-2.84526pt/G=\Spec\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}
coincides with the finite quotient
\mathfrak{h}/W=\Spec\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]^{W}
. Since
W
is a finite reflection group,
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]^{W}
is a polynomial algebra. The GIT quotient parametrizes semisimple (or equvalently, closed) orbits. On the other extreme, nilpotent orbits are all contracted to
0\in\Spec\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]^{W}
by the quotient morphism. Any
x\in\mathfrak{g}
admits a unique Jordan decomposition
x=x_{s}+x_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
with
x_{s}
semisimple,
x_{n}
nilpotent and
[x_{s},x_{n}]=0
; then the image of
x
in

\mathfrak{g}/\hskip-2.84526pt/G
is the same as the image of
x_{s}
. A very important fact
which distinguishes nilpotent and semisimple orbits is the following:

Theorem 6.2.4
.

There are finitely many orbits in the nilpotent cone of a semisimple Lie algebra.

The nilpotent orbits can be classified using the theory of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triples.

{ex}

Let us consider
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{n+1}
. Nilpotent elements can be put in Jordan normal form with only zeroes on the diagonal. The sizes of the blocks determine uniquely the nilpotent orbit the element belongs to, hence a bijection between nilpotent orbits
and partitions of
n+1
.

Proof 6.2.5

(Proof of Theorem
6.2.2
)
.

We want to show that there are finitely many
G_{0}
-orbits of elements
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. First we notice that any element
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is nilpotent (
ad^{n}(x)=0
for
n>>0
since it sends
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
to
\mathfrak{g}_{k+n}
). Then we show that, for each nilpotent
G
-orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, each irreducible component of
\mathcal{O}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is a
G_{0}
-orbit; since irreducible components are finitely many, the result then follows from the finiteness of nilpotent orbits.

To prove the claim it suffices to show that for any
x\in\mathcal{O}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, the tangent space at
x
of its
G_{0}
-orbit is equal to the tangent space at
x

of
\mathcal{O}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. The former is
[\mathfrak{g}_{0},x]
. The latter is
[\mathfrak{g},x]\cap\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, and clearly contains the former. Now, consider an element
[y,x]\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
with
y\in\mathfrak{g}
. If the decomposition of
y
with respect to the grading is
y=\sum_{k}y_{k}
, then
[y_{k},x]\in\mathfrak{g}_{k+1}
for any
k
, hence
[y,x]=[y_{0},x]\in[\mathfrak{g}_{0},x]
.

Remark 6.2.6
.

The fact that the number of nilpotent orbits in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is finite also holds for any
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-grading (see next section), with exactly the same proof.

We conclude that
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is a
G_{0}
prehomogeneous space, meaning that it contains a dense orbit. However, these are very special prehomogeneous spaces (called
parabolic
), since a prehomogeneous space does not need to have finitely many orbits in general. We also point out that Vinberg managed to classify orbits in parabolic prehomogeneous spaces in terms of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triples
[
79
]
.

Let us come back to
6.2
and let us now suppose that
h
is of a very special form: we ask that there exists
1\leq j\leq n
such that
h(\alpha_{j})=1
and
h(\alpha_{u})=0
for
u\neq j
. Recall that any root
\alpha
can be written as an integer linear combination of simple roots with non-negative or non-positive coefficients
\alpha=\sum_{i}n^{\alpha}_{i}\alpha_{i}
. One easily deduces that
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha,\,n^{\alpha}_{j}=0}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{C}h_{j}
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{\prime}
is the semisimple Lie algebra whose Dynkin diagram is obtained by erasing the
j
-th node from the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
, and
h_{j}\in\mathfrak{h}
. Also
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
can be recovered in a similar manner: it is the
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
-representation whose highest weight is
-\alpha_{j}|_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}
. Let us give a few examples of such gradings that already appeared in these lectures.

{ex}

Consider the exceptional group
E_{6}
with its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
, and the previous grading defined by
j=2
(in Bourbaki’s notation, as in the diagram below). By removing the second root, we obtain
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{sl}_{6}\oplus\mathbb{C}h_{2}
, which corresponds to
G_{0}\simeq\GL_{6}
acting on
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{6}
. As we have already seen, this action has only five orbits:
\{0\}
, the cone over
\Gr(3,6)
, the set of partially decomposable tensors, the tangential variety of
\Gr(3,6)
and the open orbit.

E_{6}={}{}\hbox to52.12pt{\vbox to19.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.14217pt\lower-4.27603pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{ex}

In fact, we can run the previous example also with
E_{7}
and
E_{8}
, always choosing the second root. The result in both cases is that
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{n}
has finitely many orbits under the action of
\GL_{n}
when
n=7
(choose
E_{7}
) and
n=8
(choose
E_{8}
). When
n=9
, the orbits of
\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{9}
can still be classified by using
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-gradings, but there are continuous families (more precisely, a four dimensional family)
[
29
]
.

{ex}

Again, consider the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{e}_{6},\mathfrak{e}_{7},\mathfrak{e}_{8}
, but this time choose
j=1
. Then
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{so}_{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}h_{1}
, with
n=10,12,14
respectively, and
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\Delta_{n}
is a half-spin representation. We deduce that
\Delta_{n}
has finitely many orbits in these cases. Once more, the next case
n=16
has a continuous family of orbits and appears in a
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-grading of
\mathfrak{e}_{8}

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-Gradings

We will be very brief on
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-gradings and will focus on examples. The definition is similar to the one of
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings, but this time replace
\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}_{m}:=\mathbb{Z}/m
. The general construction of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-gradings is formally similar to
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings. Fix a semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
; in the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-gradings, one should consider an affine Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 6.2.7
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
has no outer automorphisms, there is only one affine Dynkin diagram
G^{(1)}
, obtained by adding to the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{g}
a node corresponding
to the lowest root. Lie algebras with outer automorphisms have additional affine Dynkin diagrams.

Now, the choice of a function
f\colon\phi\to\mathbb{Z}
, where
\phi
is the set of nodes of the diagram, defines a
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-grading for a certain
m
, and all
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-gradings are obtained this way (the construction for
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings is the same but starts from actual Dynkin diagrams). If
\phi
vanishes on all but one node, say the
j
-th node, then
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is the semisimple Lie algebra whose Dynkin diagram is obtained by erasing the
j
-th node from the affine Dynkin diagram, and
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is the representation with highest weight
-\alpha_{j}|_{\mathfrak{g}_{0}}
.

{ex}

\mathfrak{g}_{2}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}
.
Choosing a node gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-grading, where
m
is the label of the node. So for instance choosing the first node on the left gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
-grading, i.e., no grading at all:
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{0}=(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{1}=\mathfrak{g}_{2}
. Choosing the last node on the right gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
grading with
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{0}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
,
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{-1}=(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{*}
(can you recognize it?). Choosing the central node gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading with
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{0}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes S^{3}\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

{ex}

\mathfrak{so}_{8}

.
Choosing the central node gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading with
(\mathfrak{so}_{8})_{0}=(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})^{\oplus 4}
and
(\mathfrak{so}_{8})_{1}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 4}
.

.
Choosing the upper node gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-grading with
(\mathfrak{e}_{8})_{0}=\mathfrak{sl}_{9}
,
(\mathfrak{e}_{8})_{1}=\textstyle\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{9}
and
(\mathfrak{e}_{8})_{-1}=\textstyle\bigwedge^{6}\mathbb{C}^{9}
. Choosing the first node on the left gives a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading with
(\mathfrak{e}_{8})_{0}=\mathfrak{so}_{16}
and
(\mathfrak{e}_{8})_{1}=\Delta_{16}
. These two examples were mentioned in the previous subsection.

A remarkable property of the
G_{0}
-action on
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is that a version of Jordan reduction holds. In particular,
similarly to the classification of nilpotent and semisimple elements in semisimple Lie algebras (which can be seen as
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
-graded algebras), Vinberg showed that there are finitely many nilpotent
G_{0}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, while semisimple orbits are parametrized by a quotient of an affine space by a complex reflection group
[
78
]
.

6.3
Tits’ shadows

By a
Fano variety of subvarieties
, we will mean an algebraic variety (or scheme) parametrizing certain classes of subvarieties inside a fixed ambient variety. The theory of Tits’ shadows (
[
77
]
, see also
[
52
]
) allows to describe certain Fano varieties of subvarieties inside a
G
-homogeneous space
X
. It works particularly well to describe Fano varieties of lines (parametrizing
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s inside
X
). This theory starts from the observation that
G
will act as well on such a Fano variety of subvarieties. If the action is again transitive, the Fano variety of subvarieties itself will be another
G
-homogeneous space, and one can decide combinatorially which one directly from the Dynkin diagram.
Let us explain how.

Suppose
G
is semisimple with Dynkin diagram
D
. Recall that by
G
-homogeneous space
X
we mean a rational projective
G
-homogeneous variety; it can be seen as a quotient
X=G/P
, where
P
is a
parabolic
subgroup. Such
G
-homogeneous classified by combinatorially classifying parabolic subgroups
P
; the latter are in correspondence with sets of simple roots, i.e., sets of nodes of the Dynkin diagram
D
. For
I
such a set we let
P_{I}
be the corresponding parabolic subgroup. If
\omega=\sum_{i\in I}\omega_{i}
is the associated sum of fundamental weights, then
G/P_{I}
is the minimal orbit inside the projectivization
\mathbb{P}V_{\omega}
of the irreducible representation with highest weight
\omega
.

{ex}

For
G=\SL_{n+1}
, choose
I=\{\alpha_{i}\}
the
i
-th simple root. Then
G/P_{I}=G/P_{i}
is the Grassmannian
\Gr(i,n+1)\subset\mathbb{P}(\wedge^{i}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
.

For
X=G/P_{I}
, we choose another set
J
of simple roots, disjoint from
I
, and let
D^{\prime}
be the Dynkin diagram obtained by removing from
D
the nodes of
J
. Denote by
G^{\prime}
a semisimple group wit Dynkin diagram
D^{\prime}
. The homogeneous space
G/P_{I\cup J}
projects onto both
G/P_{I}
and
G/P_{J}
, and the fibers of the projection
G/P_{I\cup J}\to G/P_{J}
are isomorphic to
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{I}
. They map isomorphically to
G/P_{I}
where there images are the
Tits shadows
. Thus
G/P_{J}
can be seen as parametrizing a family of subvarieties, isomorphic to
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{I}
, inside
G/P_{I}
. In many cases this family is the whole Fano variety of subvarieties
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{I}\subset G/P_{I}
.

Fact
: Assume that
I
is made of a single root
\{\alpha_{i}\}
, which is not a long root (this includes the case where all roots have the same length). Then the Fano variety of linear spaces
\mathbb{P}^{k}
of dimension
k
inside
G/P_{I}
is the union of all those homogeneous varieties
G/P_{J}
such that
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{I}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{k}
. Equivalently, the connected component of
D^{\prime}
that contains
I
must be of type
A_{k-1}
, with
\alpha_{i}
being an extreme node of
D^{\prime}
.

{ex}

Let us describe Tits’ shadows yielding linear projective spaces inside the Grassmannians
X=\SL_{n+1}/P_{i}=\Gr(i,n+1)
.

In the following pictures, we have marked with
\bullet
the node corresponding to
I
, with
\times
the nodes of
J
; the connected component of
D^{\prime}
containing
I
(of type
A_{k-1}
) has been framed in a rectangle. For simplicity, we fixed
n=7
and
i=3
.

The Fano variety of lines in
X
is given by the flag variety
\Fl(i-1,i+1,n+1)=\SL_{n+1}/P_{i-1,i+1}
, as shown by Tits’ shadow described by the following picture.

Geometrically this corresponds to the fact that each point of
\Fl(i-1,i+1,n+1)
, that is, each flag of subspaces
V_{i-1}\subset V_{i+1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
,
defines a line in
\Gr(i,n+1)
. Or course this is just the line parametrizing
[U_{i}]\in\Gr(i,n+1)
such that
V_{i-1}\subset U_{i}\subset V_{i+1}
. All lines in
\Gr(i,n+1)
are obtained in this way.

For linear subspaces of dimension two there are in general two families, obtained as follows: any flag
V_{i-2}\subset V_{i+1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
from
\Fl(i-2,i+1,n+1)
defines the
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
\Gr(i,n+1)
parametrizing
[U_{i}]\in\Gr(i,n+1)
such that
V_{i-2}\subset U_{i}\subset V_{i+1}
. And similarly for a flag
V_{i-1}\subset V_{i+2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. Thus (except if
i
is too small or too big)
\Hil_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(X)=\Fl(i-2,i+1,n+1)\cup\Fl(i-1,i+2,n+1).

These two families are described by the following Tits’ shadows:

{ex}

In order to cconclude with an example of exceptional type, consider the diagrams
corresponding to the generalized Grasmannians of type
E_{6}
,

We know that
E_{6}/P_{1}
(and symmetrically
E_{6}/P_{6}
) is the Cayley plane,
while
E_{6}/P_{2}
is the adjoint variety
E_{6}^{ad}
. Playing with Tits shadows, we see that
E_{6}/P_{3}
is the Fano variety of lines on
E_{6}/P_{1}
, and also parametrizes families of
\mathbb{P}^{4}
’s on both
E_{6}/P_{2}
and
E_{6}/P_{6}
. Similarly,
E_{6}/P_{4}
is the Fano variety of lines on
E_{6}/P_{2}
, and also the Fano variety of planes on both

E_{6}/P_{1}
and
E_{6}/P_{6}
!

In particular, we can recover all the homogeneous spaces of
E_{6}
just from
the Cayley planes and the adjoint variety. This implies that the whole representation theory of
E_{6}
can be recovered from the minimal representation of dimension
27
,
and the adjoint representation; a fact which, obviously, was already known to E. Cartan.

Remark 6.3.1
.

When
\alpha_{i}
is a short root (which may happen only if
G
is not simply laced) then the Fano variety of
\mathbb{P}^{k}
’s inside
G/P_{I}
is not homogeneous. For instance, for
k=1
there are two orbits in general, the closed one being of the form
G/P_{J}
such that
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}_{I}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We already know a typical example: the
G_{2}
-quadric
\mathbb{Q}^{5}=G_{2}/P_{1}
, whose space of lines is
\OGr(2,7)
and contains two orbits;
the closed one is the
